Voice rewrite: before and after, all twelve articles

Every changed paragraph, heading and scrollytelling step, side by side. Grey chips are live values computed on the page, and blue text is maths.

What changed

  • All twelve published articles were rewritten into a guided, “we/let’s” voice: whole sentences, the so and because said out loud, bold terms defined where they first appear, far fewer dashes, and “Thanks for reading!” at the end of each conclusion.
  • Every number, live value, hedge, claim, equation and section stayed as it was. Button labels, readouts, legends and the landing-page blurbs weren’t touched.
  • The pre-rewrite prose is kept in archive/prose-before-2026-09-15/, and the voice rules are now in CLAUDE.md and reference/writing-the-prose.md.

Content slips fixed while rewording

  • k-means: the prose named the wrong marker (“crosses” → “diamonds”), and “six rounds” is now computed instead of typed.
  • smote: “a year” → “a month”. f1-score: “engine blowout” → “catastrophic pump failure”, and “camera” → “sensor”, to match the data.
  • population-stability-index: two cross-references pointed at the wrong section, “the right-hand chart” is now “the second chart” (the panels stack on phones), and “a million months of data” is now “all that extra data” (it was a million applications).
  • constrained-choice: MRS, CES, Cobb–Douglas and indifference curves are now defined where they first appear, and σ = 1 is introduced before the lab uses it.
  • nash-equilibrium: “your own payoffs determine what makes them willing to mix” had it backwards. It now says your payoffs set the mix the other player needs to keep you willing to mix.

Bugs fixed

  • A past text rename had turned width into W in four components (k-means and LightGBM), breaking SVG and CSS widths without any error.
  • The Shapley Values page’s tab title said “XGBoost”.
  • Constrained Choice and Nash Equilibrium had no gap between paragraphs.
  • Also fixed: two DBSCAN browser checks that found sections by their old headings, and the Svelte 5 ship.sh failing on a leftover /tmp log.

k-means

ChoosingK

Choosing k, and the criteria that cannot help
Choosing k, and the criteria that can't help
Nothing so far has said where k comes from. It is an input, and the algorithm is entirely happy to return four clusters, or eleven, from the same points. So the obvious move is to try several and pick the best — except that inertia falls every single time you add a cluster, all the way down to zero when every point is its own centroid. The objective cannot choose k, because minimising it always says "more".
So far, we haven't said where k comes from. It's an input, and the algorithm will happily return four clusters, or eleven, from the same points. The obvious approach is to try several values and pick the best one. The problem is that inertia falls every time we add a cluster, all the way down to zero when every point is its own centroid. This means the objective can't choose k for us, because minimising it always says "more".
What people do instead is look for the bend: the k after which the improvements stop being worth having. Then, because that is a judgement call dressed as a measurement, they reach for a second opinion — most often the silhouette, which is not the k-means objective at all but a comparison, for each point, between how far it sits from its own cluster and how far from the nearest other one.
Instead, people usually look for the bend in the curve: the value of k after which adding clusters stops improving things by much. Since that's a judgement call dressed up as a measurement, they often ask for a second opinion as well. The most common one is the silhouette, which isn't the k-means objective at all. For each point, it compares how far the point sits from its own cluster with how far it sits from the nearest other cluster.
On the three real groups both criteria agree, and they agree with your eyes: the inertia curve bends hard at k = BEST_SILHOUETTE_K and the silhouette peaks there at ELBOW[BEST_SILHOUETTE_K …. That is the case everybody puts in the tutorial, and it is not the case that teaches you anything.
Try a few values of k in the figure above. On the three real groups, both criteria agree with each other and with your eyes: the inertia curve bends sharply at k = BEST_SILHOUETTE_K, and the silhouette peaks there at ELBOW[BEST_SILHOUETTE_K …. This is the case that shows up in most tutorials, but it isn't the one that teaches us anything.
Now switch the toggle. Those UNIFORM.length points are drawn uniformly from a box — there is no group structure in them whatsoever, by construction. The inertia curve falls just as smoothly as before and has no bend to find. And the silhouette does not shrug: it peaks at k = NOISE_BEST_K.k, at NOISE_BEST_K.silhouette.…. Lower than the ELBOW[BEST_SILHOUETTE_K … the real structure scores — but nothing in the number tells you where the line between them is, and you do not normally have both to compare.
Now switch the toggle to Pure noise. These UNIFORM.length points are drawn uniformly from a box, so by construction there's no group structure in them at all. The inertia curve still falls steadily, but this time there's no bend to find. The silhouette, however, still gives an answer: it peaks at k = NOISE_BEST_K.k, with a score of NOISE_BEST_K.silhouette.…. That's lower than the ELBOW[BEST_SILHOUETTE_K … scored by the real structure, but nothing in the number itself tells you where the line between the two lies, and in practice you won't have both to compare.
Which is the entire point of the toggle. Comparing your inertia curve against the curve you would get from structureless data drawn over the same region is the only thing that turns "the bend is around three" into a statement with a null hypothesis behind it. Done properly, with reference datasets rather than by eye, that is the gap statistic — and it is the one method in this neighbourhood that can return the answer "one cluster".
That comparison is the whole point of the toggle. Comparing your inertia curve with the one you'd get from structureless data spread over the same region is the only way to turn "the bend is around three" into a statement with a null hypothesis behind it. When this is done properly, using reference datasets rather than eyeballing, it's called the gap statistic, and of the methods we've discussed, it's the only one that can return the answer "one cluster".

Conclusion

What you actually have
What k-means actually gives you
A partition of your points into k groups, and a guarantee that no single point would rather be in a different group and no single centroid would rather be somewhere else. That is a real property and it is worth having. It is also the entire list.
When k-means finishes, you get a partition of your points into k groups, along with a guarantee that no single point would rather be in a different group and no single centroid would rather be somewhere else. That's a real property, and it's worth having, but it's also the only thing you're guaranteed.
It is not a minimum of the objective — you saw 1 - FORGY.rate, 0 of runs stop short of one. It is not the structure in the data — you saw the objective actively prefer the wrong grouping in all four of the shapes above. And it is not evidence that groups exist, because the same procedure returns a tidy NOISE_BEST_K.k clusters from UNIFORM.length points of uniform noise.
The result isn't necessarily a minimum of the objective, since we saw 1 - FORGY.rate, 0 of runs stop short of one. It isn't necessarily the structure in the data either, since we saw the objective actively prefer the wrong grouping in all four of the shapes above. And it isn't evidence that groups exist at all, because the same procedure returns a tidy set of NOISE_BEST_K.k clusters from UNIFORM.length points of uniform noise.
The limits worth knowing before you act on a cluster
Things to keep in mind before you act on a cluster
Your scaling decision is the model. The objective is a sum of squared distances, so multiplying a feature by ten multiplies its say in the answer by a hundred. Standardising every column is the usual reflex and it is not neutral — it asserts that a standard deviation of income and a standard deviation of age should count the same. There is no scaling-free version of k-means to fall back on. Whatever you chose, that is the modelling assumption, and it belongs in the write-up.
How you scale the features is part of the model. The objective is a sum of squared distances, so multiplying a feature by ten multiplies its influence on the answer by a hundred. Standardising every column is the usual reflex, but it isn't neutral: it assumes that a standard deviation of income and a standard deviation of age should count the same. Since there's no scaling-free version of k-means to fall back on, whatever scaling you choose is a modelling assumption, and it belongs in the write-up.
It has no way to decline. Ask for six clusters in data with none and you get six, with centroids, sizes and a silhouette score. Nothing in the output distinguishes a discovered group from a slice of a continuum. The one method here that can answer "there is only one cluster" is the gap statistic, because it is the only one that compares against a reference distribution.
It can't say no. If you ask for six clusters in data that has none, you'll get six, complete with centroids, sizes and a silhouette score. Nothing in the output distinguishes a genuine group from an arbitrary slice of a continuum. Of the methods in this article, only the gap statistic can answer "there is only one cluster", because it's the only one that compares against a reference distribution.
The mean is not robust, and squaring makes it worse. A single stray point pulls its centroid, and pulls with weight proportional to its distance. On real data that usually means a handful of outliers quietly claim a cluster of their own — which is occasionally what you wanted and usually not. k-medians and k-medoids exist for this and cost more.
The mean isn't robust, and squaring makes it worse. A single stray point pulls its centroid towards it, with a weight proportional to its distance. On real data, that usually means a handful of outliers quietly claim a cluster of their own, which is occasionally what you want but usually isn't. k-medians and k-medoids were designed for this situation, although they cost more to run.
Distances stop discriminating in high dimensions. As the number of features grows at a fixed sample size, the distance to the nearest point and to the farthest point converge, and "assign to the nearest centroid" is deciding on an increasingly thin margin. Reducing dimension first is the standard answer, and it is a second modelling choice stacked on the first — PCA-then-k-means finds clusters in the projection, not in your data.
Distances become less informative in high dimensions. As the number of features grows while the sample size stays fixed, the distances to the nearest and farthest points converge, so "assign to the nearest centroid" ends up deciding on thinner and thinner margins. The standard remedy is to reduce the dimension first, but that's a second modelling choice stacked on top of the first: running PCA and then k-means finds clusters in the projection, not in your data.
Membership is less stable than it looks. Cluster numbering is arbitrary between runs, which everybody knows. The part that matters is that points near a boundary change cluster under a different seed or a different bootstrap sample, and a report that ranks customers by cluster is treating those assignments as facts. If a decision hangs on a label, the quantity to measure is how often that label survives resampling — not the silhouette, which says nothing about it.
Cluster membership is less stable than it looks. Most people know that cluster numbers are arbitrary from one run to the next. What matters more is that points near a boundary can change cluster under a different seed or a different bootstrap sample, yet a report that ranks customers by cluster treats those assignments as facts. If a decision depends on a label, measure how often that label survives resampling. The silhouette won't help here, because it says nothing about stability.
There is no ground truth it is failing to find. Kleinberg's impossibility result says no clustering function can be at once scale-invariant, able to produce every partition, and consistent under shrinking clusters together. Some property has to go. So "the correct clustering" is not a target k-means is missing; it is not a well-posed thing to miss, and the useful question is always whether these groups are useful for the decision you are about to make.
There's no ground truth for it to miss. Kleinberg's impossibility theorem says that no clustering function can be scale-invariant, able to produce every possible partition, and consistent (i.e. unchanged when clusters are made tighter and pushed further apart) all at once, so at least one of those properties has to go. This means "the correct clustering" isn't a target that k-means keeps missing, because it isn't well defined in the first place. The useful question is always whether these groups help with the decision you're about to make.
Say what you ran. The initialiser, the number of restarts, the scaling, the seed, and k. "We ran k-means with k = 4" describes a draw from a distribution without saying how many draws you took — and since scikit-learn 1.4, the default is one.
Report exactly what you ran. That means the initialiser, the number of restarts, the scaling, the seed, and k. Saying "we ran k-means with k = 4" describes a draw from a distribution without saying how many draws you took, and since scikit-learn 1.4, the default is just one.
A note on the numbers here
A note on the numbers
Every figure on this page is computed in your browser when it loads, from a seeded generator, so the numbers in the sentences and the pixels in the charts come from the same objects. The MAIN.length points are three Gaussian blobs; the best solution found for k = 3 scores BEST.inertia and was reached by FORGY.good of FORGY.trials random starts and PLUSPLUS.good of PLUSPLUS.trials k-means++ starts. A companion script re-derives all of it outside the browser and fails loudly if any claim in the prose stops matching the code — including the monotonicity of every trace, and the fact that the generated grouping scores worse than the clustering in all four shapes.

The MAIN.length points are drawn from three Gaussian blobs, and the best solution found for k = 3 scores BEST.inertia. That includes the check that no trace ever goes up, and the fact that the generated grouping scores worse than the clustering in all four shapes.
It is worth ending on where this came from. Lloyd was not looking for clusters. He was working out how to place quantisation levels for a telephone signal, in a 1957 memo that Bell Labs did not publish for twenty-five years, and the algorithm has not changed since. What kept it is not that it is good at finding groups — it is often bad at that, in ways that are entirely predictable from three lines of algebra about a perpendicular bisector. What kept it is that both of its steps are a single pass over the data, and at a billion points that is still the only property anybody is buying.
Let's end where the algorithm began. Lloyd wasn't looking for clusters at all. He was working out where to place quantisation levels for a telephone signal, in a 1957 memo that Bell Labs didn't publish for twenty-five years, and the algorithm hasn't changed since. It hasn't survived because it's good at finding groups. As we've seen, it's often bad at that, in ways that are entirely predictable from a few lines of algebra about a perpendicular bisector. It has survived because both of its steps are a single pass over the data, and when you have a billion points, that's still the property that matters most.

Intro

Here is a pile of points and a suspicion that they arrived in groups. Nothing is labelled. Nobody has told you how many groups there are or where they sit. You would like the groups.
Suppose we have a pile of points and a hunch that they came from a few distinct groups. None of the points are labelled, and nobody has told us how many groups there are or where they sit, but we'd still like to find them. This task is called clustering, and k-means is the classic way to approach it.
Stated carefully, the job is to choose a partition — every point into exactly one group — that makes the groups as tight as possible. And stated that way it looks like something you could brute-force, right up until you count the partitions. Twenty points into three groups can be done smallText ways. The MAIN.length points in the chart below can be done big.mantissa × 10big.exponent ways. There is no cleverness waiting to rescue you either: finding the tightest partition is NP-hard, and it stays NP-hard for points in a plane.
More precisely, we want to split the points into a partition, where every point belongs to exactly one group, and we want those groups to be as tight as possible. Put that way, it sounds like something we could solve by brute force: try every partition and keep the best one. The trouble is how many partitions there are. Twenty points can be split into three groups in smallText different ways, and the MAIN.length points in the chart below can be split in big.mantissa × 10big.exponent ways. A cleverer search won't save us either, because finding the tightest partition is NP-hard, even when the points lie in a plane.
So look at what is easy. If somebody handed you the three centres, grouping the points would be trivial — each point goes to whichever centre is nearest. And if somebody handed you the three groups, finding the centres would be trivial — each centre is the average of its group. Both halves of the problem are one line of arithmetic. Each of them just needs the other half first.
So instead, let's look at what is easy. If someone gave us the three centres, grouping the points would be simple, because each point would just join whichever centre is nearest. And if someone gave us the three groups instead, finding the centres would be just as simple, since each centre is the average of its group. Each half of the problem takes one line of arithmetic, but each one needs the answer to the other half first.
Stuart Lloyd's move, in a 1957 Bell Labs memo about quantising telephone signals, was to stop trying to break the circle and simply go round it. Guess the centres. Group the points by the guess. Move each centre to the middle of the group it just got. Repeat until nothing moves. That is the whole algorithm, and it is what almost everybody means when they say "k-means".
Stuart Lloyd's solution, written up in a 1957 Bell Labs memo about quantising telephone signals, was to stop trying to break this circle and simply go around it. We start by guessing the centres. Then we group the points using that guess, move each centre to the middle of the group it was just given, and repeat until nothing moves. That's the whole algorithm, and it's what almost everyone means when they say "k-means".
It is below, one half-step at a time. The three crosses are the centres — centroids — and you can drag them anywhere you like. The number underneath is the only thing the algorithm is actually trying to make small.
You can try it yourself below, one half-step at a time. The three diamonds are the centres, which we'll call centroids, and you can drag them anywhere you like. The number underneath the chart is the only thing the algorithm is actually trying to make small.

LloydLab

Drag a centroid and the shaded territory follows it immediately — but the points keep the colours they were last given, and the number can go up. Only the two buttons are guaranteed to bring it down.
When you drag a centroid, its shaded territory follows it right away. The points, however, keep the colours they were last given, so the number can go up. Only the algorithm's own steps (assigning points and moving centroids) are guaranteed never to increase it.

ScrollCenter

Two tight groups of V.trueSizes[0] and a broad one of V.trueSizes[2]. The two tight ones come out perfectly. The broad one does not come out at all — its points are split V.confusion[2].join(" / ") across all three clusters, which is to say it was never a cluster as far as the algorithm is concerned.
This dataset has two tight groups of V.trueSizes[0] points each and a broad group of V.trueSizes[2]. The two tight groups come out perfectly, but the broad one doesn't come out at all. Its points are split V.confusion[2].join(" / ") across all three clusters, so as far as the algorithm is concerned, it was never a cluster in the first place.
There is no parameter here for how wide a cluster is. A centroid owns a region of space, and every region is charged for distance at the same rate, so a large loose group and a small tight one cannot be told apart by anything the objective can see.
The problem is that k-means has no parameter for how wide a cluster is. Each centroid owns a region of space, and every region is charged for distance at the same rate, so nothing the objective can see distinguishes a large, loose group from a small, tight one.
Three parallel diagonal bands, sheared from the same round blobs. The clustering cuts straight across them: only A.purity of points end up in a cluster that matches their band.
Next, we have three parallel diagonal bands, made by shearing the same round blobs. The clustering cuts straight across them, and only A.purity of the points end up in a cluster that matches their band.
And here is the thing to sit with. A long band has enormous squared distance to its own centre — the correct grouping scores A.trueInertia, while the crosswise slicing scores A.fit.inertia. The wrong answer is not a local minimum the algorithm got stuck in. It is genuinely better by the measure it was given.
Here's the part worth pausing on. A long band has a huge squared distance to its own centre, so the correct grouping scores A.trueInertia, while the crosswise slicing scores just A.fit.inertia. In other words, the wrong answer isn't a local minimum that the algorithm got stuck in. It's actually better according to the measure the algorithm was given.
S.trueSizes[0] points on the left, S.trueSizes[1] and S.trueSizes[2] in the two small groups on the right. k-means cuts the big one in half — S.confusion[0][0] and S.confusion[0][1] points — and merges the two small ones into a single cluster of S.sizes[2].
This time there are S.trueSizes[0] points in the group on the left, and S.trueSizes[1] and S.trueSizes[2] in the two small groups on the right. k-means splits the big group in two (S.confusion[0][0] and S.confusion[0][1] points) and merges the two small ones into a single cluster of S.sizes[2].
Inertia is a sum over points, so a group with S.trueSizes[0] members contributes far more of it than one with S.trueSizes[1]. Buying a reduction where the points are is simply worth more, and the objective is not weighing anything else.
Since inertia is a sum over points, a group with S.trueSizes[0] members contributes far more to it than a group with S.trueSizes[1]. Reducing distances where most of the points are is simply worth more, and the objective doesn't weigh anything else.
Two interleaving arcs, and the clustering is a vertical cut down the middle: M.confusion[0][1] of one arc's points on one side and M.confusion[0][0] on the other. Every arc is split roughly in half.
Finally, we have two interleaving arcs, and the clustering is just a vertical cut down the middle, with M.confusion[0][1] of one arc's points on one side and M.confusion[0][0] on the other. Both arcs are split roughly in half.
This one is not a matter of degree. A cluster in k-means is the set of points nearer to its centroid than to any other, which is an intersection of half-planes — a convex polygon. Neither arc is convex, so neither arc is a shape the algorithm is able to express, at any k, from any start, with any amount of compute.
Unlike the first three, this failure isn't a matter of degree. A cluster in k-means is the set of points closer to its centroid than to any other centroid, which is an intersection of half-planes, and therefore a convex polygon. Neither arc is convex, so the algorithm can't represent either of them, at any k, from any start, with any amount of compute.
Restarts fix a search problem. They do nothing about the harder question, which is whether the thing being searched for is the thing you wanted. Below are four datasets where the groups are obvious to you and unavailable to the algorithm — each fitted with the k it was generated with, taking the best of sixty k-means++ starts, so that nothing here can be blamed on bad luck.
Restarts solve a search problem, but they do nothing about a harder question: is the thing we're searching for actually the thing we want? Below are four datasets where the groups are obvious to you but out of reach for the algorithm. Each one is fitted with the k it was generated with, keeping the best of sixty k-means++ starts, so none of the results can be blamed on bad luck.
All four are the same sentence
Four failures, one cause
They look like four separate weaknesses and they are one. Go back to the assignment step: a point joins cluster i rather than j when it is nearer to the first centroid, and for squared Euclidean distance that condition simplifies to a linear one. The boundary is the perpendicular bisector of the segment between the two centroids — a straight line, always, no matter what the data looks like. Which is why the shaded regions in every chart on this page have straight edges.
These might look like four separate weaknesses, but they're really one. Let's go back to the assignment step. A point joins cluster i rather than cluster j when it's nearer to the first centroid, and for squared Euclidean distance, that condition simplifies to a linear one. The boundary between the two clusters is the perpendicular bisector of the segment joining their centroids, which is always a straight line, no matter what the data looks like. That's why the shaded regions in every chart on this page have straight edges.
So the clusters k-means is capable of returning are exactly the cells of a Voronoi diagram: convex, straight-sided, and defined by one location apiece. There is nowhere in that description to record that a cluster is wide, or elongated, or heavily populated, because a centroid is a point and a point has no width, no orientation and no mass.
This means the clusters k-means is capable of returning are exactly the cells of a Voronoi diagram: convex, straight-sided, and each defined by a single location. There's no room in that description to record that a cluster is wide, elongated or heavily populated, because a centroid is just a point, and a point has no width, no orientation and no mass.
The same fact, said in the language of models: k-means is what you get from fitting a mixture of spherical Gaussians that all share one variance and one mixing weight, and then taking hard assignments instead of soft ones. Every panel above is one word in that sentence being false — shared variance, then spherical, then equal weight, then Gaussian at all. Reach for a Gaussian mixture and the first three become parameters you can fit. For the fourth you need a method that never compares a point to a centre in the first place, like spectral clustering or DBSCAN.
We can say the same thing in the language of statistical models. k-means is what you get when you fit a mixture of spherical Gaussians that all share one variance and one mixing weight, and then take hard assignments instead of soft ones. Each panel above breaks one part of that description: first the shared variance, then the spherical shape, then the equal weights, and finally the assumption that the groups are Gaussian at all. If you switch to a Gaussian mixture model, the first three become parameters you can fit. For the fourth, you need a method that never compares a point to a centre in the first place, such as spectral clustering or DBSCAN.
And notice what the numbers under each panel have been saying. In all four cases the grouping the data was generated from has a higher inertia than the one k-means returned — for the stretched bands, times higher. The algorithm did not fail to solve its problem. It solved it, and the solution is not the structure. No initialisation, no restart count and no amount of compute touches that, because there is nothing broken to fix.
Finally, notice what the numbers under each panel have been telling us. In all four cases, the grouping the data was generated from has a higher inertia than the one k-means returned (for the stretched bands, it's times higher). So the algorithm didn't fail to solve its problem. It solved it, but the solution simply isn't the structure we were looking for. No initialisation, restart count or amount of compute can change that, because nothing is broken.

Not a rare accident
This isn't a rare accident
Run it FORGY.trials times from FORGY.trials independent random starts and count where it lands. FORGY.good reach BEST.inertia. The other badForgy — 1 - FORGY.rate, 0 of them — stop somewhere between 48000 and 49100, up to worstExcess, 0 worse.
To see how often this happens, let's run the algorithm from FORGY.trials independent random starts and count where each run ends up. Of those, FORGY.good reach BEST.inertia. The other badForgy runs (1 - FORGY.rate, 0 of them) stop somewhere between 48000 and 49100, up to worstExcess, 0 worse.
Look at the gap in the middle of the histogram. These are not near misses shading into each other; they are FORGY.buckets.length distinct fixed points, and every one of the bad ones is the same structural mistake made in slightly different places. One run of k-means gives you a sample from this distribution. It does not tell you which one you got.
Notice the gap in the middle of the histogram. These aren't near misses that shade into one another. Instead, they're FORGY.buckets.length distinct fixed points, and every one of the bad ones is the same structural mistake made in slightly different places. So a single run of k-means gives you one sample from this distribution, and it doesn't tell you which kind of sample you got.
Two fixes, and only one of them works
Two fixes, but only one of them works
k-means++ picks the first centre at random and every later one with probability proportional to its squared distance from the nearest centre already chosen, so unclaimed territory is where the next centre probably goes. It is a genuinely good idea with a proof attached, and here it moves the success rate from FORGY.rate, 0 to PLUSPLUS.rate, 0. Better. Not a fix.
The first fix is k-means++. It picks the first centre at random and each later one with probability proportional to its squared distance from the nearest centre already chosen, so the next centre will probably land in territory that nobody has claimed yet. It's a good idea, and it comes with a proof. Here it raises the success rate from FORGY.rate, 0 to PLUSPLUS.rate, 0, which is an improvement, but it isn't a fix.
What actually fixes it is embarrassingly dull: run it more than once and keep the lowest inertia. Ten independent random starts fail together about N_INIT_10.forgy times. That is the whole technique.
The fix that actually works is almost boring: run the algorithm several times and keep the result with the lowest inertia. The chance that ten independent random starts all fail is only about N_INIT_10.forgy, and that's the whole technique.
Which is worth knowing, because it is no longer the default. Since version 1.4 scikit-learn's n_init='auto' means one run when the initialiser is k-means++ — so out of the box you are drawing a single sample from the right-hand distribution below, not the best of ten.
This is worth knowing, because it's no longer the default. Since version 1.4, scikit-learn's n_init='auto' means just one run when the initialiser is k-means++. So out of the box, you're drawing a single sample from the k-means++ distribution in the chart, not the best of ten.
Everything in the last section was a guarantee: each half-step is exactly optimal, the objective never rises, the whole thing terminates. None of it said the answer would be any good. A fixed point is a place where neither move helps — and there is usually more than one.
Everything in the last section was a guarantee: each half-step is exactly optimal, the objective never rises, and the algorithm always terminates. However, none of those guarantees say the answer will be any good. A fixed point is simply a place where neither step can improve things, and there is usually more than one of them. Scroll on to see what that means in practice.

Writing it down
Writing down the objective
There is one quantity behind everything above. Write r_ij = 1 when point i belongs to cluster j and 0 otherwise — each point in exactly one cluster — and let \mu_j be that cluster's centroid. Then:
Everything we just saw revolves around a single quantity. To write it down, let r_ij = 1 when point i belongs to cluster j and 0 otherwise (so each point sits in exactly one cluster), and let \mu_j be the centroid of that cluster. The quantity is then:
The sum of squared distances from every point to the centroid of its own cluster. It goes by inertia, or within-cluster sum of squares, and it is the number under the chart. Notice that it takes two arguments. Almost everything worth knowing about k-means follows from that.
In words, this is the sum of squared distances from every point to the centroid of its own cluster. It's called the inertia, or the within-cluster sum of squares, and it's the number shown under the chart above. Notice that it takes two arguments: the assignment and the centroids. As we'll see, almost everything worth knowing about k-means follows from that one fact.
Because with two arguments you have two different minimisations available, and both of them are trivial. Hold the centroids fixed and ask which assignment is best: each point contributes \lVert x_i - \mu_j \rVert^2 and nothing else does, so every point independently picks its nearest centroid. No search, no iteration — the exact minimiser, in one pass.
With two arguments, there are two different ways to minimise J, and both of them turn out to be easy. First, let's hold the centroids fixed and ask which assignment is best. A point's assignment only affects its own term, \lVert x_i - \mu_j \rVert^2, so every point can simply pick its nearest centroid, independently of all the others. That takes no search and no iteration, and it gives us the exact minimiser in a single pass:
Now hold the assignment fixed and ask which centroids are best. J splits into k independent problems, one per cluster, and each is smooth. Differentiate and set to zero:
Next, let's hold the assignment fixed and ask which centroids are best. Now J splits into k independent problems, one per cluster, and each of them is smooth, so we can differentiate and set the result to zero:
The mean of the cluster. Also exact, also one pass. So the algorithm you dragged around above is not a heuristic bolted onto a hard problem — it is two exact minimisations of the same function, taken in turn, each one optimal in the argument it is allowed to touch and blind to the other. The technical name for that shape is block coordinate descent.
In other words, the best centroid is simply the mean of its cluster, and just like the assignment, we can compute it exactly in a single pass. This means the algorithm you played with above isn't a heuristic bolted onto a hard problem. It's two exact minimisations of the same function, taken in turn, where each step is optimal for the argument it controls and leaves the other one alone. This pattern is known as block coordinate descent.
The mean is not a stylistic choice
Why the mean, and not something else?
Nothing above works for a different distance. The mean falls out of that derivative because the distance is squared and Euclidean. Swap in absolute distance and the same argument produces the coordinate-wise median, which is a different algorithm — k-medians. Swap in an arbitrary dissimilarity and the update step may have no closed form at all, which is why k-medoids gives up and restricts every centre to being one of the data points. "k-means with cosine distance" is not a thing you can ask for without saying what happened to this step.
This derivation only works for one particular distance. The mean falls out of the derivative because the distance is squared and Euclidean. If we used absolute distance instead, the same argument would give us the coordinate-wise median, which leads to a different algorithm called k-medians. With an arbitrary dissimilarity, the update step may have no closed form at all, which is why k-medoids gives up and requires every centre to be one of the data points. So if you ask for "k-means with cosine distance", you also need to say what happens to this step.
Neither half-step can ever increase J, because each one returns the minimum over what it controls and the current value is always one of the candidates it minimised over. So the sequence of values is non-increasing, which you can watch happening in the trace under the chart — the line only ever goes down.
Neither half-step can ever increase J. Each one returns the minimum over the variables it controls, and the current value is always one of the options it considered, so the result can't be worse than where it started. As a result, the sequence of values never increases. You can see this in the trace under the chart, where the line only ever goes down unless you drag a centroid yourself.
That alone does not prove it terminates; a sequence can decrease forever. The argument that finishes it is a counting one. After an update step, J is completely determined by the assignment, since the centroids are that assignment's means. There are at most k^n assignments. And whenever the assignment step actually changes a label, J strictly falls — so no assignment can ever come back. Finitely many assignments, none repeated: it has to stop.
On its own, that doesn't prove the algorithm stops, since a sequence can keep decreasing forever. To finish the argument, we need to count. After an update step, J is completely determined by the assignment, because the centroids are just that assignment's means. There are at most k^n possible assignments, and whenever the assignment step actually changes a label, J strictly falls, which means no assignment can ever come back. Since there are only finitely many assignments and none of them can repeat, the algorithm has to stop.
In practice it stops fast. Starting the MAIN.length points from three centroids stacked in the bottom-left corner — about as bad as you can draw — takes corner.iterations rounds to go from an inertia of corner.startInertia to BEST.inertia. The worst case is spectacularly worse than that: there are datasets in the plane that force exponentially many iterations. You will not meet one.
In practice, it stops quickly. If we start the MAIN.length points from three centroids stacked in the bottom-left corner, which is about as bad a start as you can draw, it takes corner.iterations rounds to go from an inertia of corner.startInertia to BEST.inertia. The worst case is far slower, since there are datasets in the plane that force exponentially many iterations. Fortunately, you're very unlikely to run into one.
The word gets used for at least four different objects, and the confusion between them is where most arguments about k-means actually live.
The name "k-means" gets used for at least four different things, and mixing them up is behind most arguments about the algorithm. Let's separate them.
Minimise J over all assignments and centroids. This is the thing you want. It is NP-hard, including for points in the plane.
Minimise J over all assignments and centroids. This is what we actually want, but it's NP-hard, even for points in the plane.
The alternation above. Cheap, always terminates, and returns a fixed point — not a minimum. When somebody says "k-means", this is nearly always what they ran.
The alternating procedure above. It's cheap and always terminates, but it returns a fixed point rather than a minimum. When someone says they ran "k-means", this is nearly always what they mean.
Where the centroids start. Not part of Lloyd's algorithm, not usually mentioned, and — as the next section is about — frequently the thing that decided your answer.
Where the centroids start. It isn't part of Lloyd's algorithm and it's rarely mentioned, but as we'll see in the next section, it often decides the answer you get.
An input. The algorithm will return exactly k clusters for any k you name, on any data, including data with no clusters in it.
An input that you choose. The algorithm will return exactly k clusters for any k you give it, on any data, including data with no clusters in it at all.

Title

Two moves, repeated until nothing changes. The algorithm always stops, it never makes things worse, and it is under no obligation whatsoever to be right.
Two simple steps, repeated until nothing changes. Let's see why k-means always finishes, why it never makes things worse, and why it can still stop at the wrong answer.

isolation-forest

Conclusion

What it buys, and what it costs
What it gives you, and what it costs
Two details in the original paper are easy to skip past and matter more than they look. The first is the subsample. Every tree here saw PSI accounts, not all of them, and the paper's default is 256 — a number that does not grow with your dataset. Smaller samples are not a concession to speed; they improve the result. A dense cluster crowded into a small sample stops looking dense, so genuine anomalies sitting near it stop being buried by their neighbours, and a small tight clump of anomalies stops hiding itself. Those two failures have names in the paper: swamping and masking.
Two details in the original paper are easy to skip past, but they matter more than they seem. The first is the subsample. Every tree here saw PSI accounts rather than all of them, and the paper's default is 256, a number that doesn't grow with your dataset. Smaller samples aren't just a concession to speed, because they actually improve the result. When a dense cluster is thinned out into a small sample, it stops looking so dense. As a result, genuine anomalies sitting near it stop being buried by their neighbours, and a small, tight clump of anomalies stops hiding itself. The paper calls these two failures swamping and masking.
The second is what the algorithm never does. It computes no distances, fits no density, and inverts no matrices. Building a tree costs a pass over a fixed sample regardless of how much data you have, so training is linear in the number of rows with a small constant, and memory is bounded by N_TREES × PSI points. On a table wide enough to make a nearest-neighbour method uncomfortable, that difference stops being an optimisation and starts being the reason the job finishes.
The second detail is what the algorithm never does. It computes no distances, fits no density and inverts no matrices. Building a tree costs a single pass over a fixed-size sample, no matter how much data you have, so training is linear in the number of rows with a small constant, and memory is bounded by N_TREES × PSI points. On a table wide enough to make a nearest-neighbour method struggle, that difference stops being a mere optimisation and becomes the reason the job finishes at all.
The price is visible in the last chart. Every cut is parallel to an axis, so the score surface is built from rectangles, and it leaks: extend a region of low score along an axis and you get bands of artificially normal-looking space where no data lives at all. If your anomalies are defined by a relationship between two features rather than by either one — a transfer size that is only strange given its frequency — axis-aligned cuts will find them slowly, or not at all. That is the problem the extended isolation forest addresses, by drawing cuts at random angles instead of along the axes.
The price is visible in the last chart. Because every cut is parallel to an axis, the score surface is built from rectangles, and it leaks. Regions of low score stretch out along the axes, creating bands of artificially normal-looking space where no data lives at all. If your anomalies are defined by a relationship between two features rather than by either one alone (for example, a transfer size that's only strange given its frequency), axis-aligned cuts will find them slowly, or not at all. The extended isolation forest addresses this problem by drawing cuts at random angles instead of along the axes.
It is also worth remembering that the model hands you a ranking, not a decision. Every point gets a score; nothing in the method tells you where to draw the line. That choice is yours, and it belongs with the people who will have to act on whatever crosses it.
Finally, it's worth remembering that the model hands you a ranking, not a decision. Every point gets a score, but nothing in the method tells you where to draw the line. That choice is yours, and it belongs with the people who will have to act on whatever crosses it.

If isolation forests were new to you, the two papers linked below are unusually readable for the genre and worth the hour.

Intro

Suppose you are handed n bank accounts and told that a handful of them are behaving strangely. Nobody will tell you which ones, or what "strangely" means. All you get is a couple of numbers per account: how big its transfers usually are, and how many it makes in a week.
Suppose we're handed n bank accounts and told that a handful of them are behaving strangely. Nobody will tell us which ones they are, or even what "strangely" means. All we get is a couple of numbers for each account: how big its transfers usually are, and how many transfers it makes in a week.
The instinctive move is to describe what normal looks like and then flag whatever fails to match. Fit a density and take the low-probability tail. Measure each point's distance to its nearest neighbours and take the lonely ones. Both work. Both also spend nearly all of their effort building a careful picture of the majority — which is the part you were never asked about — and both get slower and shakier as you add columns.
The instinctive approach is to describe what normal looks like and then flag whatever doesn't match. For example, we could fit a density and flag the low-probability tail, or measure each point's distance to its nearest neighbours and flag the lonely ones. Both approaches work. However, both spend nearly all of their effort building a careful picture of the majority, which is the part we were never asked about, and both get slower and shakier as we add more columns.
In 2008, Fei Tony Liu, Kai Ming Ting and Zhi-Hua Zhou proposed turning the problem around. Do not model normal at all. Instead, take a point and try to separate it from everything else using random cuts, and simply count how many cuts that takes. Anomalies, they observed, are few and different: few, so there is empty space around them; different, so their values sit away from the bulk. Both properties make a point easy to cut off. The count is the detector.
In 2008, Fei Tony Liu, Kai Ming Ting and Zhi-Hua Zhou proposed turning the problem around. Their idea was to skip modelling normal behaviour altogether. Instead, we take a point, try to separate it from everything else using random cuts, and simply count how many cuts that takes. As they observed, anomalies are few and different. Because they're few, there's empty space around them, and because they're different, their values sit away from the bulk of the data. Both properties make a point easy to cut off, so the number of cuts itself becomes the detector.
There are planted accounts in the data below that were planted to be odd. Nothing that follows ever sees that label — it is only there so we can check the answer at the end.
The data below contains planted accounts that were deliberately planted to be odd. Nothing in the algorithm ever sees that label; it's only there so we can check the answer at the end.

LineChart

Below are the accounts again, and one of them is yours to move. Every time you let go, a fresh isolation tree grows around it: a random feature, a random split between the smallest and largest value left in the box, over and over until nothing else shares the box with your point.
Below are the accounts again, but this time one of them is yours to move. Every time you let go of it, a fresh isolation tree grows around it, picking a random feature and a random split between the smallest and largest values left in the box, over and over, until nothing else shares the box with your point.
Drop it out in the empty corners and it falls out of the crowd in two or three cuts. Drop it into the middle of a habit and the algorithm has to keep slicing. That number of cuts — nothing more elaborate — is the whole signal.
If you drop it in one of the empty corners, it's separated from the crowd in two or three cuts. If you drop it in the middle of a habit, the algorithm has to keep slicing. That number of cuts is the whole signal, and there's nothing more elaborate to it.
Two things are worth noticing while you drag. The first is how little the algorithm knows: it never measures a distance, never fits a density, never sees the labels. It only ever asks how much slicing a point survives. The second is that a single tree is noisy — press grow a new tree without moving the point and the count jumps around. The score underneath it barely moves, because it is an average over N_TREES trees. That gap between one tree and many is the next thing to look at.
There are two things worth noticing as you drag. The first is how little the algorithm knows. It never measures a distance, never fits a density and never sees the labels; it only asks how much slicing a point can survive. The second is that a single tree is noisy. If you press grow a new tree without moving the point, the count jumps around, while the score underneath it barely moves, because the score is an average over N_TREES trees. That gap between one tree and many is what we'll look at next.

A different sample and different random splits give a different count for the very same account. One tree is a coin flip with opinions. This is not a flaw to be tuned away — the randomness is what makes each tree cheap.
With a different sample and different random splits, we get a different count for the very same account. In other words, a single tree's answer is partly luck. This isn't a flaw to be tuned away, though, because the randomness is exactly what makes each tree so cheap to build.
Grow $N_TREES of them and the counts pile up into a distribution. Its mean is what the algorithm actually uses, written E[h(x)]. The spread is wide; the mean is stable.
If we grow $N_TREES trees, the counts pile up into a distribution. The algorithm uses the mean of this distribution, written E[h(x)]. Notice that while the spread is wide, the mean is stable.
A raw depth is not comparable across datasets — deeper trees are expected when there are more points. Dividing by c(n), the average depth you would expect anyway, and pushing the result through a negative exponent gives a number between 0 and 1 that means the same thing everywhere.

Run that score at every position, not just where the data sits, and the model becomes visible. Light where a point would be hard to isolate — down in the crowd — and dark out in the empty space, where two or three cuts would do it. If we compute that score at every position, not just where the data sits, the model itself becomes visible. They're made of rectangles, and they extend along the axes well past where any data sits, because every cut this algorithm can make is parallel to an axis.
One tree is noisy. A forest is not.
One tree is noisy, but a forest isn't
A single tree's answer depends on which points it happened to sample and where its splits happened to land. The fix is the usual one, and it is the reason this is a forest rather than a tree.
A single tree's answer depends on which points it happened to sample and where its splits happened to land. The fix is the usual one: grow many trees and average them. That's why the algorithm is a forest rather than a single tree.

An isolation tree does something almost aggressively simple. It picks a feature at random, picks a split point at random between the smallest and largest value that feature takes, and cuts. Then it does the same thing again inside one of the halves. It never looks at where the other points are beyond those two numbers, and it never looks at a label.
An isolation tree works in a remarkably simple way. It picks a feature at random, picks a split point at random between the smallest and largest values that feature takes, and cuts. Then it does the same thing again inside one of the two halves. Apart from those two numbers, it never looks at where the other points are, and it never looks at a label. Scroll through the steps below to see it in action.

TextAndMathEquations

Write h(x) for the number of cuts one tree needs to isolate the point x — its path length, the depth of the leaf it ends up alone in. A single tree's value of h(x) is close to useless on its own, so we grow N_TREES of them, each on its own random subsample, and average: E[h(x)].
Let's write h(x) for the number of cuts one tree needs to isolate the point x. This is called the point's path length, and it's the depth of the leaf where the point ends up alone. On its own, a single tree's value of h(x) is close to useless, so we grow N_TREES trees, each on its own random subsample, and take the average, E[h(x)].
That average still is not comparable to anything. Depth grows with sample size: isolating a point among a thousand takes more cuts than isolating it among ten, for reasons that have nothing to do with the point. We need to divide by the depth we would have expected anyway.
However, that average still isn't comparable to anything. Depth grows with sample size, since isolating a point among a thousand others takes more cuts than isolating it among ten, for reasons that have nothing to do with the point itself. To fix this, we need to divide by the depth we would have expected anyway.
Here the algorithm gets a gift. Because splits are drawn uniformly, an isolation tree has the same structure as a random binary search tree, and the average depth of an unsuccessful search in such a tree with n nodes is known exactly:

Divide by that yardstick and push the ratio through a negative exponent, and you get the anomaly score:
If we divide by that yardstick and push the ratio through a negative exponent, we get the anomaly score:
The shape of that expression is worth a moment. When a point takes exactly the expected number of cuts, the exponent is -1 and the score is 0.5. Isolate it instantly and E[h(x)] \to 0, so the score climbs toward 1. Keep having to cut and the score falls toward 0. The scale is fixed, which is the entire point: a score of 0.7 means the same thing on a hundred rows as on ten million.
It's worth taking a moment to look at the shape of this expression. When a point takes exactly the expected number of cuts, the exponent is -1 and the score is 0.5. If a point is isolated instantly, then E[h(x)] \to 0, so its score climbs toward 1. And if we keep having to cut, the score falls toward 0. Because the scale is fixed, a score of 0.7 means the same thing on a hundred rows as it does on ten million, which is exactly what we wanted.

One caveat that follows directly from the formula: if every score in your dataset comes back near 0.5, that is not a failure to be tuned around. It is the model telling you it found no point meaningfully easier to isolate than any other.

Title

Anomalies are few, and they are different. Both of those make them easy to cut off from everything else — and that is the whole algorithm.
Anomalies are few, and they're different. As we'll see, both of those properties make them easy to cut off from everything else, and that idea is the whole algorithm.

xgboost

Conclusion

In an era dominated by large language models and deep neural networks, it is easy to wonder why tree-based gradient boosting remains the gold standard for tabular data. The answer lies in the nature of structured business data:
In an era dominated by large language models and deep neural networks, it's natural to wonder why tree-based gradient boosting remains the gold standard for tabular data. The answer lies in the nature of structured business data:
1. Heterogeneous features: Tabular datasets mix currencies, percentages, categorical codes, and missing values. Neural networks want all of that scaled, normalized and embedded first. A tree only ever asks whether a value is above or below a threshold, so any strictly increasing transform of a feature — kroner to euros, kWh to joules, a rank transform — leaves every split decision untouched. Missing values are not a preprocessing problem either: each branch learns which way to send them.
1. Heterogeneous features: Tabular datasets mix currencies, percentages, categorical codes, and missing values. Neural networks need all of that scaled, normalized and embedded first. A tree, on the other hand, only ever asks whether a value is above or below a threshold, so any strictly increasing transform of a feature (kroner to euros, kWh to joules, or a rank transform) leaves every split decision untouched. Missing values aren't a preprocessing problem either, since each branch learns which way to send them.
No algorithm is a silver bullet, and the honest list of caveats is longer than the list of strengths.
Of course, no algorithm is a silver bullet, and an honest list of caveats is longer than the list of strengths.
Extrapolation: Decision trees create piece-wise constant step functions. If our heating dataset only saw temperatures down to -10°C, the model will predict the exact same constant value for -40°C. It cannot extrapolate continuous trends beyond the minimum and maximum boundaries of its training data.
Extrapolation: Decision trees create piecewise constant step functions. If our heating dataset only saw temperatures down to -10°C, the model would predict exactly the same value for -40°C, because it can't extrapolate trends beyond the range of its training data.
Unstructured modalities: On images, audio, or raw text, convolutions and attention build hierarchical representations that boosted trees have no mechanism to express. This is not a close contest.
Unstructured modalities: On images, audio, or raw text, convolutions and attention build hierarchical representations that boosted trees have no way to express, and it isn't a close contest.
It hands you a score, not a probability. For a classifier, the number that comes out of the sigmoid is a ranking, and it is usually not calibrated — especially once you have tuned for AUC or used class weights. If a downstream decision multiplies that number by a cost, fit a calibration map on held-out data and check the reliability curve before anyone treats it as a probability.
It hands you a score, not a probability. For a classifier, the number that comes out of the sigmoid is a ranking, and it's usually not calibrated, especially once you've tuned for AUC or used class weights. If a downstream decision multiplies that number by a cost, fit a calibration map on held-out data and check the reliability curve before anyone treats it as a probability.
It has a lot of knobs, and they interact. Random forests are famously forgiving; XGBoost is not. Depth, \eta, the number of rounds, subsampling, \lambda and \gamma all trade against each other, and the number of rounds in particular should never be chosen by hand — as the shrinkage charts above showed, training error keeps improving long after held-out error has turned around. Early stopping on a validation set is not a refinement here, it is the mechanism.
It has a lot of knobs, and they interact. Random forests are famously forgiving, but XGBoost isn't. Depth, \eta, the number of rounds, subsampling, \lambda and \gamma all trade off against each other, and the number of rounds in particular should never be chosen by hand. As the shrinkage charts above showed, training error keeps improving long after held-out error has turned around. Early stopping on a validation set isn't just a refinement here; it's the mechanism that makes the whole thing work.
Its feature importances mislead. The default gain and split-count importances systematically favour high-cardinality and continuous features, which have more thresholds available to them, over binary ones that may matter more. If the importances are going to be shown to anyone who will act on them, compute SHAP values instead and be explicit that they explain the model, not the world.
Its feature importances mislead. The default gain and split-count importances systematically favour high-cardinality and continuous features, which have more thresholds available to them, over binary features that may matter more. If the importances will be shown to anyone who's going to act on them, compute SHAP values instead, and be explicit that they explain the model, not the world.

HessianFigure

How much each point counts when choosing the next split. Squared error gives every point the same say. Logistic loss gives the loudest say to the points the model is least sure about, and almost none to the ones it has already settled — which is the entire reason for carrying a second derivative around.
How much each point counts when the next split is chosen. Squared error gives every point the same say, while logistic loss gives the loudest say to the points the model is least sure about and almost none to the ones it has already settled. That difference is the whole reason for carrying a second derivative around.

Intro

A single decision tree is like an over-eager student. Given a training set, it carves the feature space into narrower and narrower boxes until it gets almost every training example right. But show it fresh, unseen data, and it often trips over its own complexity. It suffers from high variance.
A single decision tree is like an over-eager student. Given a training set, it carves the feature space into narrower and narrower boxes until it gets almost every training example right. But when we show it fresh, unseen data, it often trips over its own complexity. In other words, it suffers from high variance.
The standard remedy is to assemble a crowd. In a Random Forest, you train hundreds of deep trees independently on randomized bootstrap samples of your data, then average their predictions. It is the wisdom of the crowd: individual errors cancel out, and the ensemble stabilizes.
The standard remedy is to assemble a crowd. In a Random Forest, we train hundreds of deep trees independently on randomized bootstrap samples of the data, and then average their predictions. It's the wisdom of the crowd: individual errors cancel out, and the ensemble stabilizes.
Gradient boosting takes the opposite philosophy. Instead of training a committee of independent trees and hoping their mistakes cancel, what if you trained a sequence of humble, shallow trees where each newcomer is assigned a single, specific job: predict the mistakes made by the team so far?
Gradient boosting takes the opposite approach. Instead of training a committee of independent trees and hoping their mistakes cancel, what if we trained a sequence of humble, shallow trees, and gave each newcomer a single, specific job: predict the mistakes made by the team so far?
In 2016, researchers Tianqi Chen and Carlos Guestrin published XGBoost (short for eXtreme Gradient Boosting). It took competitive machine learning by storm, claiming dozens of victories across Kaggle competitions and becoming the workhorse of real-world tabular data pipelines. XGBoost is not just "fast boosting" — it is a mathematically elegant framework that unifies loss functions, leaf regularization, and tree growth into a single closed-form objective.
In 2016, researchers Tianqi Chen and Carlos Guestrin published XGBoost (short for eXtreme Gradient Boosting). It took competitive machine learning by storm, claiming dozens of victories across Kaggle competitions and becoming the workhorse of real-world tabular data pipelines. XGBoost isn't just "fast boosting", though. It's a mathematically elegant framework that unifies loss functions, leaf regularization, and tree growth into a single closed-form objective.
To see how it works, we will follow a concrete challenge: predicting daily household electricity demand (in kilowatt-hours) based on outdoor temperature (^\circ\textC). The physics is thoroughly non-linear: sub-zero cold drives heavy heating demand, mild spring days sit at an efficient refrigeration baseline, and scorching heat waves spike air conditioning. Let us watch how trees and gradients conquer this curve.
To see how it works, we'll follow a concrete example: predicting daily household electricity demand (in kilowatt-hours) from the outdoor temperature (^\circ\textC). The relationship is thoroughly non-linear. Sub-zero cold drives heavy heating demand, mild spring days sit at an efficient refrigeration baseline, and scorching heat waves send air-conditioning use soaring. Let's see how trees and gradients handle this curve.

RegularizationFigure

Both penalties are in the objective, and both are easiest to understand on one tree rather than on an ensemble. Below is the same first stump, with the two knobs exposed. There is no shrinkage here and no second round — just one split and its two leaves.
Both penalties appear in the objective, and both are easiest to understand on a single tree rather than on a whole ensemble. Below is the same first stump, with the two knobs exposed. There's no shrinkage here and no second round, just one split and its two leaves.
That asymmetry is the point of an L2 penalty on leaf values. A leaf resting on two or three days is mostly reporting whatever those particular days happened to do; a leaf resting on twenty is reporting something closer to a trend. λ pulls on the first much harder than on the second, without anyone having to decide in advance which leaves are trustworthy.
That asymmetry is the whole point of an L2 penalty on leaf values. A leaf resting on two or three days mostly reports whatever those particular days happened to do, while a leaf resting on twenty reports something closer to a trend. λ pulls on the first much harder than on the second, without anyone having to decide in advance which leaves to trust.
Try the odd day. It is a single mild-weather Tuesday with heating-season demand — one uninsulated greenhouse, or a heat pump left running. At λ = 0 it drags its leaf noticeably. Raise λ and the leaf comes back toward the crowd. Raise γ far enough and the algorithm declines to split at all.
Now try adding the odd day. It's a single mild Tuesday with heating-season demand, as if someone had an uninsulated greenhouse or left a heat pump running. At λ = 0, it drags its leaf noticeably, but as you raise λ, the leaf moves back toward the crowd. And if you raise γ far enough, the algorithm declines to split at all.

ScrollCenter

With no shrinkage each tree tries to erase the entire remaining error, and for a while that works beautifully: this is the fastest curve down. Then the two lines come apart. Training error keeps falling — all the way to $HIST[0].train[nRounds] kWh by round $nRounds — while error on days the model has never seen bottoms out at $HIST[0].best kWh at round $HIST[0].bestRound and then starts climbing again.

That gap is overfitting, and notice that it is only visible because the held-out curve is on the chart. Plot the training curve alone and this looks like unambiguous progress.
That gap is overfitting, and notice that we can only see it because the held-out curve is on the chart. If we plotted the training curve alone, this would look like steady progress.
Scale each tree down by $katexify("\eta = 0.25") and every round accomplishes less, so the descent is slower — at round 10 this curve is still at $HIST[1].test[10] kWh while full steps had already reached $HIST[0].test[10].
If we scale each tree down by $katexify("\eta = 0.25"), every round accomplishes less, so the descent is slower. At round 10, this curve is still at $HIST[1].test[10] kWh, while full steps had already reached $HIST[0].test[10].
It ends up in a better place anyway. Its best held-out error is $HIST[1].best kWh at round $HIST[1].bestRound, against $HIST[0].best for full steps. Each tree leaves signal on the table, and the next tree gets to use it — so the ensemble commits to any one tree's opinion of the data more slowly.
Even so, it ends up in a better place. Its best held-out error is $HIST[1].best kWh at round $HIST[1].bestRound, compared with $HIST[0].best for full steps. Each tree leaves some signal on the table for the next tree to use, so the ensemble commits to any one tree's view of the data more slowly.
Drop $katexify("\eta") to 0.08 and the curve does not even enter this zoomed frame until round $entersZoom(HIST[2]). At round $nRounds it is still descending — it reaches $HIST[2].test[nRounds] kWh and has not yet found its floor, let alone turned upward.
If we drop $katexify("\eta") to 0.08, the curve doesn't even enter this zoomed frame until round $entersZoom(HIST[2]). At round $nRounds, it's still descending: it has reached $HIST[2].test[nRounds] kWh and hasn't found its floor yet, let alone turned upward.
That is the real trade: a smaller $katexify("\eta") buys a lower eventual error but demands more trees to get there, and on this budget it has not caught $katexify("\eta = 0.25") yet. Which is why nobody picks the number of rounds by hand — you train with a small $katexify("\eta") and stop when a held-out score stops improving.
This is the real trade-off. A smaller $katexify("\eta") buys a lower eventual error but needs more trees to get there, and on this budget it hasn't caught up with $katexify("\eta = 0.25") yet. That's why nobody picks the number of rounds by hand. Instead, you train with a small $katexify("\eta") and stop when a held-out score stops improving.
The mathematics so far is regularized gradient boosting. What made XGBoost the default was systems work:
Everything we've covered so far is regularized gradient boosting, but what made XGBoost the default choice was its systems engineering:
1. Weighted quantile sketch. Rather than evaluating every possible split point, propose a few candidates per feature from an approximate quantile summary — weighted by the second-order Hessians, so the summary is finer where the loss is most sensitive.
1. Weighted quantile sketch. Rather than evaluating every possible split point, XGBoost proposes a few candidates per feature from an approximate quantile summary. The summary is weighted by the second-order Hessians, so it's finer where the loss is most sensitive.
2. Sparsity awareness. Missing values are not imputed. Each branch learns a default direction, chosen by whichever side gives more gain, and the split scan visits only the non-missing entries.
2. Sparsity awareness. Missing values aren't imputed. Instead, each branch learns a default direction, chosen by whichever side gives more gain, and the split scan only visits the non-missing entries.
3. Cache-aware blocks. Data is held in compressed, pre-sorted column blocks so gradient statistics are read in contiguous order instead of chasing pointers through a shuffled index.
3. Cache-aware blocks. The data is held in compressed, pre-sorted column blocks, so gradient statistics are read in contiguous order instead of chasing pointers through a shuffled index.
Gradient boosting is gradient descent, and it has the same knob: how far to step. Take the full step and each tree wrings everything it can out of the current errors. Take a fraction of it and you need more trees.
Since gradient boosting is gradient descent, it has the same knob: how far to step. If we take the full step, each tree wrings everything it can out of the current errors. If we take only a fraction of it, we need more trees.
The catch is that you cannot settle this on the training set. Every tree is fit to the training residuals, so on the training set less shrinkage always looks better — right down to memorising the noise. So both curves below are drawn: the dashed one is error on the energyData.length days the model was fit to, the solid one is error on testData.length held-out days it has never seen.

Three systems ideas, not statistics ones

ScrollSide

Before training any trees, XGBoost starts with a constant baseline prediction: ${katexify("\haty^(0) = \bary \approx " + baseScore.toFixed(1) + "\text kWh")}. It is the simplest possible prior — the overall mean of our ten days.
Before training any trees, XGBoost starts with a constant baseline prediction, ${katexify("\haty^(0) = \bary \approx " + baseScore.toFixed(1) + "\text kWh")}. This is the simplest possible starting point: the overall mean of our ten days.
For each day, we measure the gap between the true demand and our baseline: ${katexify("r_i = y_i - \haty^(0)")}. Under squared error, the negative gradient is exactly this residual: $katexify("-g_i = r_i"). The rose arrows point up for days that used more electricity than average — the cold ones — and down for days that used less.
For each day, we measure the gap between the true demand and our baseline, ${katexify("r_i = y_i - \haty^(0)")}. Under squared error, the negative gradient is exactly this residual, so $katexify("-g_i = r_i"). The rose arrows point up for days that used more electricity than average (the cold ones) and down for days that used less.
XGBoost tests every midpoint between adjacent temperature observations. For each candidate threshold, it computes the Similarity Score of the left and right groups, and evaluates the Gain. The highest gain occurs at $bestCandidate.splitValue…°C, splitting off the coldest $bestCandidate.leftCount days.
Next, XGBoost tests every midpoint between adjacent temperature observations. For each candidate threshold, it computes the Similarity Score of the left and right groups and evaluates the Gain. The highest gain occurs at $bestCandidate.splitValue…°C, which splits off the coldest $bestCandidate.leftCount days.
Look closely at what the other leaf now contains: mild days at 17°C sitting alongside the hottest day at 33°C, which used nearly three times as much power. Demand is U-shaped in temperature and one threshold cannot express that. A single stump on this data is close to the worst case — which is exactly why we are going to need a lot of them.
Now look closely at what the other leaf contains: mild days at 17°C sitting alongside the hottest day at 33°C, which used nearly three times as much power. Demand is U-shaped in temperature, and a single threshold can't express that. In fact, a single stump is close to the worst case for this data, which is exactly why we're going to need a lot of them.
With the split locked in at $bestCandidate.splitValue…°C, XGBoost calculates the optimal output score for each leaf using: ${katexify("w^* = \frac\sum r_i|I| + \lambda")}. The left leaf outputs +$wLeftReg.toFixed(1) kWh (cold demand surge), while the right leaf outputs $wRightReg.toFixed(1) kWh.
With the split locked in at $bestCandidate.splitValue…°C, XGBoost calculates the optimal output for each leaf using ${katexify("w^* = \frac\sum r_i|I| + \lambda")}. The left leaf outputs +$wLeftReg.toFixed(1) kWh (the cold-weather surge in demand), while the right leaf outputs $wRightReg.toFixed(1) kWh.
Notice the $katexify("\lambda") in the denominator! Without regularization ($katexify("\lambda = 0")), the leaves jump to +$wLeftNoReg.toFixed(1) and $wRightNoReg.toFixed(1). Increasing $katexify("\lambda") to 5 shrinks them down to +$wLeftHeavyReg.toFixed(1) and $wRightHeavyReg.toFixed(1).
Notice the $katexify("\lambda") in the denominator! Without regularization ($katexify("\lambda = 0")), the leaves jump to +$wLeftNoReg.toFixed(1) and $wRightNoReg.toFixed(1), but increasing $katexify("\lambda") to 5 shrinks them to +$wLeftHeavyReg.toFixed(1) and $wRightHeavyReg.toFixed(1).
Notice which leaf moved further. λ competes against $katexify("|I|"), the number of points in the leaf, so a leaf resting on $leftPts.length days is pulled toward zero much harder than one resting on $rightPts.length. There is a figure below where you can push both penalties around yourself.
Notice which leaf moved further. λ competes with $katexify("|I|"), the number of points in the leaf, so a leaf resting on $leftPts.length days is pulled toward zero much harder than one resting on $rightPts.length. Further down, there's a figure where you can adjust both penalties yourself.
Instead of jumping directly to the new tree's full prediction, XGBoost multiplies its leaf values by the learning rate (shrinkage) $katexify("\eta = 0.3"): ${katexify("\haty^(1)(x) = \haty^(0) + 0.3 \cdot f_1(x)")}. The blue prediction line steps closer to the data, and the residual errors shrink!
Instead of jumping straight to the new tree's full prediction, XGBoost multiplies its leaf values by the learning rate (also called shrinkage), $katexify("\eta = 0.3"), giving ${katexify("\haty^(1)(x) = \haty^(0) + 0.3 \cdot f_1(x)")}. As a result, the blue prediction line moves closer to the data, and the residual errors shrink!
