TextAndMathEquations

Most machine learning models update their parameters by gradient descent: you take the derivative of your loss function with respect to weights, and step downhill. Gradient boosting does the same thing, but in function space: instead of nudging numerical weights, each step adds an entire decision tree f_t(x) to the ensemble.
Most machine learning models update their parameters by gradient descent: we take the derivative of the loss function with respect to the weights and step downhill. Gradient boosting does the same thing, but in function space. Instead of nudging numerical weights, each step adds an entire decision tree f_t(x) to the ensemble.
Evaluating a complex loss function inside every prospective tree split is computationally brutal. XGBoost overcomes this by replacing the loss with its second-order Taylor expansion around the current prediction:
Evaluating a complex loss function for every candidate split would be computationally brutal, so XGBoost replaces the loss with its second-order Taylor expansion around the current prediction:
Simple to the point of being misleading, in fact — and it is worth being blunt about this, because every number in the rest of this article is computed under squared error. A quadratic's second-order Taylor expansion is not an approximation of it, it is it, and h_i = 1 for every point means the Hessian cancels out of everything below. Nothing you are about to see would change if XGBoost had stopped at first order.
In fact, it's so simple that it can be misleading, and it's worth being upfront about this, because every number in the rest of this article is computed under squared error. The second-order Taylor expansion of a quadratic isn't an approximation of it; it is the quadratic. And since h_i = 1 for every point, the Hessian cancels out of everything below. Nothing you're about to see would change if XGBoost had stopped at first order.
Where the second derivative earns its keep is anywhere the loss is not a parabola. Under logistic loss, with p_i the model's current predicted probability, g_i = p_i - y_i and h_i = p_i(1 - p_i) — so the Hessian becomes a per-point measure of how unsure the model still is:
The second derivative earns its keep wherever the loss isn't a parabola. Under logistic loss, where p_i is the model's current predicted probability, g_i = p_i - y_i and h_i = p_i(1 - p_i). This means the Hessian becomes a per-point measure of how unsure the model still is:
Since h_i sits in the denominator of both the leaf weight and the gain, a point the model already calls at 95% confidence carries about a fifth of the weight of one sitting at 50% when the next split is chosen — and at 99% confidence, about a twenty-fifth. Gradient boosting with only first-order information has no way to express that; it sees the gradient and nothing about the curvature underneath it. Regression with squared error is the one case where you cannot tell the difference.
Since h_i sits in the denominator of both the leaf weight and the gain, a point that the model already calls with 95% confidence carries about a fifth of the weight of one sitting at 50% when the next split is chosen. At 99% confidence, it carries about a twenty-fifth. Gradient boosting with only first-order information has no way to express this, because it sees the gradient but nothing about the curvature underneath it. Regression with squared error is the one case where you can't tell the difference.
Let I_j = \i \mid q(x_i) = j\ be the set of data points assigned to leaf j. Dropping constant terms from prior rounds and grouping the objective by leaf gives:
Let I_j = \i \mid q(x_i) = j\ be the set of data points assigned to leaf j. If we drop the constant terms from prior rounds and group the objective by leaf, we get:
This is just a quadratic equation in w_j. Taking its derivative and setting it to zero gives the exact optimal leaf weight:
This is just a quadratic in w_j, so taking its derivative and setting it to zero gives us the exact optimal leaf weight:
The left-hand form is general. The right-hand one is what it collapses to under squared error, where h_i = 1 makes the denominator a plain count of the points in the leaf.
The left-hand form is the general one, and the right-hand form is what it collapses to under squared error, where h_i = 1 turns the denominator into a plain count of the points in the leaf.
Substituting w_j^* back into our objective tells us the quality of any leaf structure. The paper calls this the structure score (you will also see it taught as the "similarity score"):
Substituting w_j^* back into our objective tells us the quality of any leaf structure. The paper calls this the structure score (you'll also see it taught as the "similarity score"):
The \gamma at the end is what turns this into a pruning rule: a split has to buy at least \gamma worth of improvement before it is allowed to exist.
The \gamma at the end is what turns this into a pruning rule, since a split has to buy at least \gamma worth of improvement before it's allowed to exist.
It is tempting to read that as "stop as soon as the gain goes negative", and the demonstrations in this article do exactly that for simplicity. The real implementation is more patient: it grows the tree out to \textttmax\_depth first and only then prunes bottom-up, removing nodes whose gain came out negative. The difference matters, because a split that looks worthless can sit above one that is extremely valuable — stop early and you never find out.
It's tempting to read that as "stop as soon as the gain goes negative", and for simplicity, the demonstrations in this article do exactly that. The real implementation is more patient, though. It grows the tree out to \textttmax\_depth first, and only then prunes from the bottom up, removing nodes whose gain came out negative. The difference matters, because a split that looks worthless can sit above one that's extremely valuable, and if you stop early, you never find out.

Title

Why predict the answer when you can predict your own mistakes? How trees, gradients, and shrinkage turn a sequence of simple rules into the champion of tabular data.
Why predict the answer when you can predict your own mistakes? Let's see how trees, gradients, and shrinkage turn a sequence of simple rules into the champion of tabular data.

lightgbm

BinSweep

The obvious way to go faster is to use fewer bins, and the folklore says you can drop to 63 or 31 for nearly nothing. On this problem that is not what happens. Against the exact model, max_bin = 255 gives up BIN_SWEEP[BIN_SWEEP.leng… of held-out error; max_bin = 32 gives up sweep32.excess, 0.
The obvious way to go faster is to use fewer bins, and folklore says you can drop to 63 or 31 at almost no cost. On this problem, that isn't what happens. Compared with the exact model, max_bin = 255 gives up BIN_SWEEP[BIN_SWEEP.leng… of held-out error, while max_bin = 32 gives up sweep32.excess, 0.
And look at what the coarser setting bought. Gain evaluations fall from compact to compact(sweep32.gainEvals) — a real reduction. Rows touched fall from compact to compact, which is to say they do not fall at all, because building a histogram is a pass over the rows no matter how many buckets you are dropping them into. Turning max_bin down trades away accuracy to save the operation that was already cheap.
Now look at what the coarser setting bought us. Gain evaluations fall from compact to compact(sweep32.gainEvals), which is a real reduction. Rows touched go from compact to compact. In other words, they don't fall at all, because building a histogram takes a pass over the rows no matter how many buckets they're dropped into. So turning max_bin down trades away accuracy to save on the operation that was already cheap.
The four curves are the interesting part. As min_data_in_leaf rises the whole curve slides left: at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[0].minDataInL… even 255 bins is LEAF_FLOOR[0].bins[LEAF_… short of exact, while at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[LEAF_FLOOR.le… you are within TOL, 0 by firstWithin bins.
The four curves are the interesting part, and you can switch between them with the buttons in the card above. As min_data_in_leaf rises, the whole curve slides to the left. At a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[0].minDataInL…, even 255 bins falls LEAF_FLOOR[0].bins[LEAF_… short of exact, while at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[LEAF_FLOOR.le…, you're within TOL, 0 by firstWithin bins.
Which makes sense once you say what a bin is for. It is a grid for placing a boundary, and the boundaries being placed are the edges of leaves. There is no value in resolving a cut point to a hundredth of the data when every leaf is required to hold a fiftieth of it — the leaf's own prediction is already an average over far more rows than the bin edge is uncertain by. So max_bin and min_data_in_leaf are one knob wearing two labels, and tuning either without the other is tuning half a parameter.
This makes sense once we spell out what a bin is for. It's a grid for placing a boundary, and the boundaries being placed are the edges of leaves. There's no value in resolving a cut point to a hundredth of the data when every leaf must hold a fiftieth of it, since the leaf's own prediction is already an average over far more rows than the bin edge is uncertain by. So max_bin and min_data_in_leaf are really one knob with two labels, and tuning either one without the other means tuning only half a parameter.
That is one problem, one target and one noise level, so read the direction and not the digits. But the direction is mechanical rather than empirical, and it is a better rule of thumb than a number copied off a forum.
Keep in mind that this is one problem, with one target and one noise level, so pay attention to the direction rather than the exact digits. That said, the direction comes from the mechanics rather than from this particular dataset, which makes it a better rule of thumb than a number copied off a forum.

Conclusion

Replacing a column with a histogram of it makes split finding times(GAIN_EVAL_RATIO) cheaper in arithmetic, times(ROW_TOUCH_RATIO) cheaper in trips to memory, and about eight times smaller on the way in. It costs ACCURACY_COST, 1 of held-out error. Nothing in this article is an argument against doing it.
Replacing a column with its histogram makes split finding times(GAIN_EVAL_RATIO) cheaper in arithmetic and times(ROW_TOUCH_RATIO) cheaper in trips to memory, and it makes the data about eight times smaller on the way in. All of this costs ACCURACY_COST, 1 of held-out error, so nothing in this article is an argument against doing it.
But the three savings do not come from the same place, and the usual one-sentence version — "it buckets the data so there is less to scan" — gets the largest one wrong. The arithmetic saving is the bucketing. The memory saving is the bucketing. The saving in passes over your data is entirely the subtraction identity: with it switched off, the histogram touches compact rows against the pre-sorted scan's compact, which is times(NOSUB_VS_EXACT) — no saving at all. Turn it on and SUBTRACT_SAVING, 0 of that disappears. The histogram's real contribution is that it is a summary you can subtract, and a sorted index list is not.
However, the three savings don't come from the same place, and the usual one-sentence explanation ("it buckets the data so there's less to scan") gets the largest one wrong. The arithmetic saving and the memory saving both come from the bucketing, but the saving in passes over your data comes entirely from the subtraction identity. With the subtraction switched off, the histogram touches compact rows compared with the pre-sorted scan's compact, a ratio of times(NOSUB_VS_EXACT), which is no saving at all. Once we turn it on, SUBTRACT_SAVING, 0 of that work disappears. So the histogram's real contribution is that it's a summary you can subtract, which a sorted index list isn't.
The limits worth knowing before you tune anything
Things to know before you tune anything
Turning max_bin down is the wrong trade. On this problem, going from 255 bins to 32 costs sweep32.excess, 0 of held-out error and saves 1 - sweep32.rowTouches /… of the row work, because building a histogram is a pass over the rows whatever its width. What you save is gain evaluations, and those were already the cheap half. If you need the model faster, the parameters that touch the expensive half are the ones that reduce how many rows a tree looks at — bagging_fraction, feature_fraction, GOSS — not max_bin.
Turning max_bin down is the wrong trade. On this problem, going from 255 bins to 32 costs sweep32.excess, 0 of held-out error and saves 1 - sweep32.rowTouches /… of the row work, because building a histogram takes a pass over the rows whatever its width. What you save is gain evaluations, and those were already the cheap half. If you need a faster model, the parameters that affect the expensive half are the ones that reduce how many rows a tree looks at, such as bagging_fraction, feature_fraction and GOSS, not max_bin.
max_bin and min_data_in_leaf are one parameter. The bins are a grid for placing leaf boundaries; how fine the grid needs to be depends on how small the leaves are allowed to be. Measured here: within 3% of the exact model needs firstWithin bins at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[1].minDataInL… and only firstWithin at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[3].minDataInL…. Sweeping one with the other pinned will find you a local optimum and tell you nothing.
max_bin and min_data_in_leaf are really one parameter. The bins are a grid for placing leaf boundaries, and how fine that grid needs to be depends on how small the leaves are allowed to be. In our measurements, getting within 3% of the exact model takes firstWithin bins at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[1].minDataInL…, but only firstWithin at a floor of LEAF_FLOOR[3].minDataInL…. If you sweep one while keeping the other fixed, you'll find a local optimum that tells you nothing.
The bin edges are estimated from a sample. bin_construct_sample_cnt defaults to 200,000 rows, so on anything larger the edges are a random variable. Two fits of the same pipeline on the same data can bin differently and therefore split differently. If you are diffing two models and the splits have moved, check this before concluding anything about the data.
The bin edges are estimated from a sample. The bin_construct_sample_cnt parameter defaults to 200,000 rows, so on any larger dataset, the edges are a random variable. This means two fits of the same pipeline on the same data can bin differently, and therefore split differently. If you're comparing two models and the splits have moved, check this before concluding anything about the data.
A cut that is not a bin edge does not exist. The gain is exact and the leaf values are exact sums; the approximation is entirely in the candidate set. That is usually a rounding error and occasionally it is the whole signal, as the narrow-window example above shows. Suspect it when a feature's effect lives in a small range that the bulk of the distribution does not visit — a threshold effect, a regulatory cutoff, a sensor limit.
A cut that isn't a bin edge doesn't exist. The gain is exact and the leaf values are exact sums, so the approximation lies entirely in the candidate set. Usually that amounts to a rounding error, but occasionally it's the whole signal, as the narrow-window example above shows. Be suspicious when a feature's effect lives in a small range that most of the distribution never visits, such as a threshold effect, a regulatory cutoff or a sensor limit.
Low-cardinality columns are untouched. A feature with fewer distinct values than max_bin gets one bin per value. Whatever binning costs you, it is not being paid by your integer, ordinal or one-hot columns — it is concentrated in the continuous ones.
Low-cardinality columns are untouched. A feature with fewer distinct values than max_bin gets one bin per value. So whatever binning costs you, it isn't paid by your integer, ordinal or one-hot columns. The cost is concentrated in the continuous ones.
Histograms are not the paper's contribution. Histogram-based split finding was in the literature and in implementations before LightGBM; the 2017 paper is about two other things, GOSS and EFB, both of which are built on the histogram. And XGBoost's own documentation describes its tree_method="hist" as "an approximation tree method used in LightGBM with slight differences in implementation". If you are comparing the two libraries and one is on hist, binning is not the difference between them.
Histograms aren't the paper's contribution. Histogram-based split finding appeared in the literature and in implementations before LightGBM. The 2017 paper is about two other ideas, GOSS and EFB, both of which are built on top of the histogram. In fact, XGBoost's own documentation describes its tree_method="hist" as "an approximation tree method used in LightGBM with slight differences in implementation". So if you're comparing the two libraries and one of them uses hist, binning isn't what separates them.
Leaf-wise growth is a separate idea and gets blamed for this one. LightGBM grows the leaf with the largest gain rather than a level at a time, and its defaults — num_leaves = 31 with max_depth = -1 — will happily build a deep, narrow, overfitted tree on a small dataset. That is a growth policy, not a consequence of binning, and the two get argued about as if they were the same choice.
Leaf-wise growth is a separate idea, and it often gets confused with this one. LightGBM grows the leaf with the largest gain rather than growing one level at a time, and its defaults (num_leaves = 31 with max_depth = -1) will happily build a deep, narrow, overfitted tree on a small dataset. That's a growth policy, not a consequence of binning, yet the two often get argued about as if they were the same choice.
A note on the numbers here
A note on the numbers
The model is CONFIG.numTrees trees of CONFIG.numLeaves leaves on N_TRAIN synthetic rows with FEATURES.length features, scored against N_TEST held-out rows, with an irreducible noise floor of NOISE_SD minutes; the exact-split version reaches rmse(PRE.exact.test) and the 255-bin version rmse(PRE.hist.test). Every cost figure is an exact operation count taken from counters inside the split finders, not a timing — the constant-factor wins that binning also brings, sequential access and a one-byte payload, are real and do not show up in a count, which means the ratios here are if anything conservative.
The model here is CONFIG.numTrees trees with CONFIG.numLeaves leaves each, trained on N_TRAIN synthetic rows with FEATURES.length features and scored against N_TEST held-out rows, with an irreducible noise floor of NOISE_SD minutes. The exact-split version reaches rmse(PRE.exact.test), and the 255-bin version reaches rmse(PRE.hist.test). Every cost figure is an exact operation count taken from counters inside the split finders, not a timing. Binning also brings constant-factor wins, such as sequential access and a one-byte payload, which are real but don't show up in a count, so if anything, the ratios here are conservative.
The first split in the chart at the top is computed in your browser as you drag the slider. The sweeps are not: twelve boosted forests is several seconds of work, so they are computed by a script in the repository, from the same modules this page imports, and committed as data. The checking script re-runs every one of those fits and fails if a number has moved, which makes a stale figure a broken build rather than a quiet lie.

Twelve boosted forests take several seconds to fit, so they're computed by a script in the repository, using the same modules this page imports, and committed as data. The checking script re-runs every one of those fits and fails if any number has moved, which turns a stale figure into a broken build rather than a quiet error.
It is worth noticing what the trick actually was. Nobody found a better way to search for a split. They noticed that the thing being searched over depends on the data only through a handful of sums, that sums can be computed in advance, and that sums subtract. Everything else — the byte-wide storage, the cheap siblings, the distributed training where you send histograms instead of rows — falls out of that one observation about the shape of the formula.
Finally, it's worth noticing what the trick actually was. Nobody found a better way to search for a split. Instead, they noticed that the thing being searched over depends on the data only through a handful of sums, that sums can be computed in advance, and that sums can be subtracted. Everything else, from the byte-wide storage and the cheap siblings to distributed training that sends histograms instead of rows, follows from that one observation about the shape of the formula.

Intro

A gradient boosting library spends nearly all of its time answering one question over and over: given these rows, and this column, where should the split go? The honest way to answer it is to try everywhere. Sort the rows by the column, walk along, and at every point where the value changes, score the split that would put everything to the left in one child and everything to the right in the other. Take the best. It is not clever, and there is nothing better — that scan finds the optimal split, by construction.
A gradient boosting library spends nearly all of its time answering the same question over and over: given these rows and this column, where should the split go? The most thorough way to answer it is to try everywhere. We sort the rows by the column and walk along them, and at every point where the value changes, we score the split that would put everything on the left in one child and everything on the right in the other. Then we take the best one. It isn't clever, but nothing does better, because this scan finds the optimal split by construction.
The cost is the problem. The root node below holds N_TRAIN rows, and its FEATURES[0].name column has EXACT_CURVE.length places you could cut it. That is one node, one column. Do it for every column at every node of every tree and the small model in this article — CONFIG.numTrees trees, CONFIG.numLeaves leaves each, FEATURES.length columns, N_TRAIN rows — scores big(PRE.exact.gainEvals) candidate splits before it is done.
The problem is the cost. The root node below holds N_TRAIN rows, and its FEATURES[0].name column has EXACT_CURVE.length places where we could cut it, and that's just one node and one column. If we do this for every column at every node of every tree, the small model in this article (CONFIG.numTrees trees with CONFIG.numLeaves leaves each, FEATURES.length columns and N_TRAIN rows) scores big(PRE.exact.gainEvals) candidate splits before it's done.
Sorting once instead of at every node helps, and that is what "pre-sorted" means; it removes the sort, not the walk. The walk is still every row, at every node, for every feature, and it needs the sort order kept alongside the data for as long as the tree is growing.
Sorting once instead of at every node helps, and that's what "pre-sorted" means. However, it only removes the sort, not the walk. The walk still visits every row, at every node, for every feature, and it needs the sort order kept alongside the data for as long as the tree is growing.
So look at what the score actually depends on. Scoring a split needs the sum of the gradients on each side and the sum of the hessians on each side: four numbers. It does not need the rows. It cannot tell whether a sum arrived from ten thousand rows one at a time or from a bucket that had already added them up.
So let's look at what the score actually depends on. Scoring a split needs the sum of the gradients on each side and the sum of the hessians on each side, which is just four numbers. It doesn't need the rows themselves, and it can't tell whether a sum arrived from ten thousand rows one at a time or from a bucket that had already added them up.
That is the whole trick. Bucket each column once, into a fixed number of bins. Add each row's gradient into its bin. Now the search for a split is a walk along the bins — and there are 255 of those however many rows you have.
That's the whole trick. We bucket each column once, into a fixed number of bins, and add each row's gradient into its bin. Now the search for a split is a walk along the bins, and there are 255 of those no matter how many rows we have.

At 255 bins there are NEEDLE_ROW edges inside it and the error falls to rmse(NEEDLE_ROW(255).test). The approximation is not in the gain or the leaf values, which stay exact sums - it is in which cuts you are allowed to consider at all.

Bins hold roughly equal numbers of rows, not equal spans of the axis. On this column - most deliveries short, a few long - 32 bins put more than half their edges below DISTANCE_EDGES[32][Math.… km and spread the rest across the long tail.

Which is the right thing and worth noticing, because it means a skewed feature does not waste its resolution on empty range, and it means the edges are a property of the data rather than of the axis. Rescale the column - take a logarithm, standardise it - and the bins, and therefore the model, are unchanged.
This is the right behaviour, and it's worth noticing for two reasons. It means a skewed feature doesn't waste its resolution on empty range, and it means the edges are a property of the data rather than of the axis. If you rescale the column (say, by taking a logarithm or standardising it), the bins, and therefore the model, stay exactly the same.
If a feature has fewer distinct values than max_bin, every value gets its own bin and no approximation happens at all. The stops column here has STOPS.distinct distinct values, so it has STOPS.distinct bins at max_bin = 255, at 128, and at 32.
If a feature has fewer distinct values than max_bin, every value gets its own bin, and no approximation happens at all. The stops column here has STOPS.distinct distinct values, so it has STOPS.distinct bins at max_bin = 255, at 128 and at 32.
Two things follow. Tuning max_bin does nothing whatsoever to your integer and low-cardinality columns. And the accuracy cost of binning is not spread evenly across a dataset - it is concentrated entirely in the continuous columns, which is where to look when a coarser setting suddenly hurts.
Two things follow from this. First, tuning max_bin does nothing at all to your integer and low-cardinality columns. Second, the accuracy cost of binning isn't spread evenly across a dataset. It's concentrated entirely in the continuous columns, so that's where to look when a coarser setting suddenly hurts.
The count can also come out below both numbers, and the table shows it: hour gets HOUR.edges[255] bins rather than 255. Its most common value is shared by HOUR.maxTie rows, more than the Math.round a bin is entitled to at that setting, and a value cannot be split across two bins - so it swallows a boundary. Every heaped or clipped column does this.

Everything above says the approximation is cheap. It is worth being precise about what is being approximated, because it is narrower than "the model is approximate" and it fails in a specific way. The gain is exact. The leaf values are exact sums over the rows that landed there. The only thing binning changes is the set of cuts you are allowed to consider — and a cut that is not in that set is not available at any depth, in any tree, ever.

What it actually buys
What binning actually buys you
Two costs matter and they are not the same cost. One is how many candidate splits get scored — arithmetic on four numbers, twice a division. The other is how many times a row's gradient has to be read out of memory. Binning does very different things to the two, and the second one is the reason LightGBM is fast.
There are two costs that matter here, and they're not the same. The first is how many candidate splits get scored, where each one is some arithmetic on four numbers, including two divisions. The second is how many times a row's gradient has to be read out of memory. Binning does very different things to these two costs, and it's the second one that makes LightGBM fast.

Here is the part that turns a nice idea into a fast one, and it is a single line of arithmetic. A node's rows are exactly its two children's rows — every row goes left or right, none is lost and none is duplicated. So bin by bin:
Here's the part that turns a nice idea into a fast one, and it takes a single line of arithmetic. A node's rows are exactly its two children's rows, since every row goes either left or right, and none is lost or duplicated. So, bin by bin:
Which means you never build both children. Build whichever holds fewer rows, and get its sibling by subtracting, at a cost of B subtractions no matter how many rows that sibling holds. Splits are usually lopsided, so the cheap half is usually much the cheaper half.
This means we never need to build both children. We build whichever child holds fewer rows and get its sibling by subtraction, at a cost of B subtractions no matter how many rows that sibling holds. Since splits are usually lopsided, the cheaper half is usually much cheaper.
The pre-sorted algorithm cannot do this
The pre-sorted algorithm can't do this
Not because nobody thought of it. A pre-sorted node is a list of row indices in sorted order, and there is no operation on two such lists that yields the third without touching the rows — a list is not a sum, and you cannot subtract it. The histogram is a summary that happens to be additive, and additivity is the whole reason it is worth having. Keep hold of that: the next section measures how much of LightGBM's speed is the histogram itself and how much is this one identity, and the answer is not the one most explanations imply.
This isn't because nobody thought of it. A pre-sorted node is a list of row indices in sorted order, and there's no operation on two such lists that yields the third without touching the rows, because a list isn't a sum and can't be subtracted. The histogram is a summary that happens to be additive, and additivity is the whole reason it's worth having. Keep that in mind, because the next section measures how much of LightGBM's speed comes from the histogram itself and how much comes from this one identity, and the answer isn't the one most explanations imply.
A second consequence, less discussed and not smaller. Once a column is a bin index rather than a number, it needs \lceil \log_2 B \rceil bits. LightGBM's documentation is explicit: at max_bin = 255 it stores a feature value in a uint8_t — one byte, against the eight of a double — and there is no permutation index to keep beside it, because there is nothing to sort. That is the reason the default is 255 rather than 256: the byte has to hold a code for a missing value too.
There's a second consequence that gets discussed less but matters just as much. Once a column is a bin index rather than a number, it only needs \lceil \log_2 B \rceil bits. LightGBM's documentation is explicit about this: at max_bin = 255, it stores a feature value in a uint8_t, which is one byte compared with the eight bytes of a double. There's also no permutation index to keep beside it, because there's nothing to sort. This is why the default is 255 rather than 256, since the byte also has to hold a code for a missing value.
A tenfold reduction in the memory the training data occupies is not a footnote. It decides whether the data fits in cache, whether it fits in memory, and — in the distributed setting — it means the thing you send across the network is a few hundred numbers per feature rather than the feature.
A tenfold reduction in the memory taken up by the training data isn't a footnote. It decides whether the data fits in cache and whether it fits in memory at all, and in the distributed setting, it means that what you send across the network is a few hundred numbers per feature rather than the whole feature.

Title

Bucket a column into 255 bins and the search for its best split stops being a scan over rows. It costs less than half a percent of the accuracy — and the speed does not come from where almost everyone says it does.
If we bucket a column into 255 bins, the search for its best split stops being a scan over rows. It costs less than half a percent of the accuracy, and as we'll see, the speed doesn't come from where most explanations say it does.

autoencoders

Conclusion

A subspace. Trained on squared error with no activation functions, a CONFIG.k-unit bottleneck finds the CONFIG.k-dimensional subspace that loses the least, exactly and reliably, and it comes with a theorem saying gradient descent will get there. That is a real result and it is worth knowing that the network is not doing anything mysterious: it is solving a problem from 1936 by a different route.
In short, it gives you a subspace. When it's trained on squared error with no activation functions, a CONFIG.k-unit bottleneck finds the CONFIG.k-dimensional subspace that loses the least, exactly and reliably, and a theorem guarantees that gradient descent will get there. That's a real result, and it's worth knowing that the network isn't doing anything mysterious. It's solving a problem from 1936 by a different route.
It does not give you a basis of that subspace, and it cannot, because the loss is exactly constant across every basis. What you get instead is whichever basis the random initialisation happened to start near — deg(FROZEN_ANGLE, 1) away from the principal directions in the run above, and frozen there from step SETTLED_STEP to step CONFIG.STEPS without moving.
What it doesn't give you is a basis for that subspace, and it can't, because the loss is exactly the same for every basis. Instead, you get whichever basis the random initialisation happened to start near. In the run above, that was deg(FROZEN_ANGLE, 1) away from the principal directions, and it stayed frozen there from step SETTLED_STEP to step CONFIG.STEPS.
The limits worth knowing before you read anything off a latent space
Things to know before you read anything off a latent space
Latent units are not features. Unit 1 is not "the most important direction", because nothing sorted them; it is not independent of unit 2, because nothing decorrelated them — in one of the three runs above they correlate at num; and its scale is arbitrary, because halving the encoder and doubling the decoder changes every code and nothing observable. Any sentence of the form "the third latent dimension captures…" needs to say what fixed the third latent dimension.
Latent units aren't features. Unit 1 isn't "the most important direction", because nothing sorted the units. It isn't independent of unit 2 either, because nothing decorrelated them (in one of the three runs above, they correlate at num). And its scale is arbitrary, because halving the encoder and doubling the decoder changes every code but nothing observable. So any sentence of the form "the third latent dimension captures…" needs to say what fixed the third latent dimension.
Reconstruction error cannot detect the problem. This is the part that bites. The metric you are watching is invariant to precisely the thing that is arbitrary: two models whose codes look nothing alike agree on their outputs to UNREG[0].reconGap.toExpo…. Early stopping, model selection, hyperparameter search — none of them can see the basis, so none of them will warn you.

Nothing converges to it either. It is tempting to assume a longer run would settle the basis down. It will not: the loss is flat along that direction, so there is no gradient, and training for another twenty thousand steps moves the angle by nothing at all. If two runs of your pipeline disagree about a latent space, more epochs is not the fix.

It won't, because the loss is flat in that direction, so there's no gradient to follow, and training for another twenty thousand steps doesn't move the angle at all. If you want the components, take an SVD of the decoder. The decoder's left singular vectors are an orthonormal basis of the right subspace, and once the scaling is pinned down they are the principal directions themselves. A modest L2 penalty pins it: it cut the symmetry from every invertible matrix to just the rotations in the run above, and the angle went to 0°. Two caveats. It costs a little accuracy, because the weights get shrunk — the reconstruction gap went from UNREG[0].reconGap.toExpo… to REG[0].reconGap.toExpone…. And it fixes the decoder, not the codes: the latent coordinates stay a rotation of PCA's, correlated at num.
If you want the components, take an SVD of the decoder. The decoder's left singular vectors form an orthonormal basis of the right subspace, and once the scaling is pinned down, they're the principal directions themselves. A modest L2 penalty does the pinning. In the run above, it cut the symmetry from every invertible matrix down to just the rotations, and the angle went to 0°. There are two caveats, though. First, it costs a little accuracy, because the weights get shrunk (the reconstruction gap went from UNREG[0].reconGap.toExpo… to REG[0].reconGap.toExpone…). Second, it fixes the decoder, not the codes, so the latent coordinates remain a rotation of PCA's, correlated at num.
If PCA is what you want, run PCA. On data this size the eigendecomposition is instant, deterministic, has no learning rate, and hands you an ordered orthonormal basis with the variances attached. The autoencoder formulation earns its place when you are going to add something to it — a nonlinearity, a denoising objective, a sparsity penalty, a convolutional encoder — not as a way of computing something a direct method computes better.
If PCA is what you want, run PCA. On data this size, the eigendecomposition is instant and deterministic, has no learning rate, and hands you an ordered orthonormal basis with the variances attached. The autoencoder formulation earns its place when you're going to add something to it, such as a nonlinearity, a denoising objective, a sparsity penalty or a convolutional encoder, not as a way of computing something that a direct method computes better.
A nonlinearity removes the guarantee along with the limit. On the arc it cut the error times(ARC_GAIN), and it did so by giving up the object the whole discussion was about. There is no subspace to have a basis of, the parameterisation of the learned curve is arbitrary in a much larger way, and Baldi and Hornik's theorem does not survive the tanh — so you also lose the assurance that the fit you have is the best one available. All of that may be a good trade. It is a trade.

All of that may well be a good trade, but it's still a trade.
A note on the numbers here
A note on the numbers
The first chart runs in your browser: PLANE.length points, one latent unit, and gradient descent stepping live while you watch. Everything after it is CONFIG.STEPS gradient steps twice over, which is about fifty seconds, so it is computed by a script in the repository from the same modules this page imports and committed as data. The checking script re-runs every one of those fits and fails if a number has moved, and separately verifies the analytic gradients against numerical ones, the eigendecomposition against its own definition, and the autoencoder's optimum against the sum of the discarded eigenvalues.
The first chart runs in your browser, with PLANE.length points, one latent unit, and gradient descent stepping live while you watch. Everything after it takes CONFIG.STEPS gradient steps twice over, which is about fifty seconds of work, so it's computed by a script in the repository, using the same modules this page imports, and committed as data. The checking script re-runs every one of those fits and fails if any number has moved. It also separately verifies the analytic gradients against numerical ones, the eigendecomposition against its own definition, and the autoencoder's optimum against the sum of the discarded eigenvalues.
What is nice about the linear case is that it is one of the few places in this subject where "what did the network learn" has an exact answer, written down before anybody was training networks. And the answer contains a warning that generalises well past the linear case: a loss function is a statement about what you want, and everything it is indifferent to will be decided by something else — an initialisation, an optimiser, a random seed — quietly, and without ever appearing in the number you are watching.
What's nice about the linear case is that it's one of the few places in this field where "what did the network learn?" has an exact answer, one that was written down before anyone was training networks. And that answer carries a warning that applies well beyond the linear case. A loss function is a statement about what you want, and everything it's indifferent to will be decided by something else, such as an initialisation, an optimiser or a random seed. That decision happens quietly, and it never shows up in the number you're watching.

Intro

An autoencoder is a network asked to copy its input to its output, through a layer too small to carry it. Nothing about that sounds useful — you already have the input — and the usefulness is entirely in the narrow layer. Whatever survives the squeeze is what the network judged worth keeping.
An autoencoder is a network that's asked to copy its input to its output, through a layer that's too small to carry it. At first, that doesn't sound useful, since we already have the input. The usefulness lies entirely in the narrow layer: whatever survives the squeeze is what the network judged worth keeping.
Take the simplest possible one. Two numbers in, one number in the middle, two numbers out, and no activation functions anywhere: just a matrix down and a matrix back up. Train it to minimise the squared distance between what went in and what came out.
Let's start with the simplest possible one. It takes two numbers in, squeezes them into one number in the middle, and puts two numbers back out, with no activation functions anywhere. It's just one matrix going down and another coming back up. We train it to minimise the squared distance between what went in and what came out.
There is a shape to that problem before any training happens. The code is a single number, so every reconstruction is that number times a fixed vector — which means every reconstruction the network can possibly produce lies on one line through the origin. Training cannot change that. All it can do is choose the line, and then choose how to land each point on it.
This problem has a shape even before any training happens. The code is a single number, so every reconstruction is that number times a fixed vector. This means every reconstruction the network can possibly produce lies on one line through the origin. Training can't change that; all it can do is choose the line, and then choose how to land each point on it.
So the whole question is which line. Below are PLANE.length points and a decoder you can point wherever you like. The hollow circles are what comes back out; the short segments are what was lost. The number underneath is the only thing the network is trying to make small.
So the whole question is which line to choose. Below are PLANE.length points and a decoder that you can point wherever you like. The hollow circles show what comes back out, and the short segments show what was lost. The number underneath the chart is the only thing the network is trying to make small.

#if solveEncoder With the encoder solved, every point drops onto the line at a right angle and the error depends on the line's direction alone — press double the decoder and watch every digit stay where it is. :else The encoder is now a free parameter and no longer matched to the decoder, so the drops are slanted and the error is above the curve. A line cannot follow a curve
A line can't follow a curve
These ARC_DATA.length points sit on an arc. There is one degree of freedom in them — position along the curve — so a one-unit bottleneck ought to be enough, and it is not.
These ARC_DATA.length points sit on an arc. They have only one degree of freedom (their position along the curve), so a one-unit bottleneck ought to be enough. But it isn't.
The linear autoencoder settles at num, which is the second eigenvalue to three decimals, which is to say it found exactly the line PCA would have drawn and could do nothing else. The reconstructions all lie on it, and the two ends of the arc get folded on top of the middle.
The linear autoencoder settles at num, which matches the second eigenvalue to three decimals. In other words, it found exactly the line PCA would have drawn, and it couldn't do anything else. The reconstructions all lie on that line, and the two ends of the arc get folded on top of the middle.

Add a nonlinearity, and it follows the curve

The code has become position along that curve. This is what people mean when they say an autoencoder generalises PCA: not a bigger subspace, but no longer a subspace.
The code has now become the position along that curve. This is what people mean when they say an autoencoder generalises PCA: the result isn't a bigger subspace, it's no longer a subspace at all.
And that is precisely what you gave up
And that's exactly what you gave up
Everything the first half of this article complained about was a complaint about the basis of a subspace. There is no subspace here. There is a curve, whose parameterisation is arbitrary in a far larger way than a matrix A was — any smooth invertible relabelling of the code leaves the reconstruction untouched.
Every problem we raised in the first half of this article was about the basis of a subspace. Here, there's no subspace at all. Instead, there's a curve whose parameterisation is arbitrary in a far bigger way than the matrix A was, since any smooth, invertible relabelling of the code leaves the reconstruction untouched.
The other loss is quieter. Baldi and Hornik's theorem — that every critical point is either the global optimum or a saddle — is a theorem about the linear case. Put the tanh layers in and it stops applying: this fit is one of many, it depends on the seed, and there is no longer any guarantee that the thing you trained is the best thing available.
The other loss is quieter. Baldi and Hornik's theorem, which says that every critical point is either the global optimum or a saddle point, only applies to the linear case. Once we add the tanh layers, it no longer holds. This fit is one of many, it depends on the seed, and there's no longer any guarantee that the model you trained is the best one available.
All of the above is about the linear case, and the obvious objection is that nobody uses linear autoencoders — the point of the architecture is the activation functions. That is fair, and it is worth being exact about what changes and what is traded for it.
Everything so far has been about the linear case, and the obvious objection is that nobody uses linear autoencoders, since the whole point of the architecture is the activation functions. That's a fair point, so let's be precise about what changes and what we trade away for it.

Kunin and colleagues proved why in 2019: the penalty cuts the symmetry group down from every invertible matrix to just the rotations, and that is exactly enough structure for a singular value decomposition of the decoder to hand back the principal directions. It costs a little accuracy — the weights are shrunk, so the reconstruction is off by about REG[0].reconGap.toExpone… where the unregularised one was off by UNREG[0].reconGap.toExpo….

TextAndMathEquations

Write the encoder as a matrix W_1 of shape k \times d and the decoder as W_2 of shape d \times k, with k < d. There is no activation function anywhere, so the whole network is x \mapsto W_2 W_1 x and what is being minimised is:
Let's write the encoder as a matrix W_1 of shape k \times d and the decoder as W_2 of shape d \times k, with k < d. Since there's no activation function anywhere, the whole network is x \mapsto W_2 W_1 x, and what we're minimising is:
The product W = W_2 W_1 is a d \times d matrix that has been squeezed through k columns, so its rank is at most k whatever the two factors are. Which means the network is not really searching over pairs of matrices at all. It is searching over rank-k linear maps, for the one that changes the data least.
The product W = W_2 W_1 is a d \times d matrix that has been squeezed through k columns, so its rank is at most k, whatever the two factors are. This means the network isn't really searching over pairs of matrices at all. Instead, it's searching over rank-k linear maps for the one that changes the data the least.
That problem has been solved since 1936. The Eckart–Young theorem says the best rank-k approximation is the one that keeps the k directions of greatest variance and discards the rest, and that the error it leaves behind is exactly the sum of the variances discarded. For the @html katexify-dimensional data later in this article, the six eigenvalues below the top two sum to num(WIDE_PCA.bestError, 4) — and num(WIDE_PCA.bestError, 4) is the number the autoencoder converges to, not approximately but to every digit its arithmetic has.
It turns out that this problem has been solved since 1936. Not something like PCA, and not PCA as a special case of something more general. A linear autoencoder trained on squared error is a reparameterisation of the same optimisation problem, and its optimum is the same subspace. Everything the first chart does — the perpendicular drops, the line that loses least — is what principal component analysis does, arrived at by gradient descent instead of by an eigendecomposition.
We don't mean something like PCA, or PCA as a special case of something more general. A linear autoencoder trained on squared error is a reparameterisation of the same optimisation problem, and its optimum is the same subspace. And what the loss cannot see
And what the loss can't see
Now take any invertible k \times k matrix A at all, and replace the two factors:
Now let's take any invertible k \times k matrix A at all, and replace the two factors like this:
The product is unchanged, because (W_2 A^-1)(A W_1) = W_2 W_1. Every reconstruction is identical. The loss is identical, to the last bit. And the two matrices are now completely different — rotated, stretched, sheared, in any combination you like.
The product doesn't change, because (W_2 A^-1)(A W_1) = W_2 W_1, so every reconstruction is identical and the loss is identical, down to the last bit. Yet the two matrices are now completely different: they can be rotated, stretched and sheared in any combination you like.
So the minimum is not a point. It is a whole k^2-dimensional family of points, all with exactly the same value, and gradient descent has no reason to prefer any member of it over any other. In the two-dimensional chart above, A is a single nonzero number and the family is "scale the decoder by anything, scale the encoder by its reciprocal" — which is precisely why the length of that arrow did not matter.
This means the minimum isn't a single point. It's a whole k^2-dimensional family of points, all with exactly the same value, and gradient descent has no reason to prefer any one of them over the others. In the two-dimensional chart above, A is a single nonzero number, and the family is "scale the decoder by anything and scale the encoder by its reciprocal", which is precisely why the length of that arrow didn't matter.
What that costs is every property of PCA that comes from how it is constructed rather than from what it optimises:

Principal components come out sorted by variance. Latent units do not come out sorted by anything, and the second one is as likely to be the bigger.
Principal components come out sorted by variance, but latent units aren't sorted by anything, so the second one is just as likely to be the bigger one.
The rows of W_1 span the right subspace and are under no obligation to be perpendicular to each other. Usually they are not.
The rows of W_1 span the right subspace, but nothing forces them to be perpendicular to each other, and usually they aren't.
PCA's coordinates are uncorrelated by construction. An autoencoder's are correlated by however much A happened to shear them.
PCA's coordinates are uncorrelated by construction, while an autoencoder's are correlated by however much A happened to shear them.
A latent value of 3 means nothing on its own. Halve the encoder and double the decoder and every code halves, with no change to anything observable.
A latent value of 3 means nothing on its own. If you halve the encoder and double the decoder, every code halves, with no change to anything observable.
There is one piece of good news, and it is a real theorem rather than a reassurance. Baldi and Hornik showed in 1989 that this loss has no local minima that are not global: every critical point that is not the global optimum is a saddle. So gradient descent does reliably find the right subspace. It simply has, and can have, no opinion about which basis of it to hand you.
There's one piece of good news, and it's a real theorem rather than just a reassurance. Baldi and Hornik showed in 1989 that this loss has no local minima that aren't global: every critical point other than the global optimum is a saddle point. So gradient descent does reliably find the right subspace. It simply has no opinion, and can't have one, about which basis of that subspace to hand you.

Title

Squeeze data through a narrow layer and ask for it back, and a linear autoencoder rediscovers principal component analysis — in a basis nobody asked for, which it will never fix, because nothing in the loss can tell the difference.
If we squeeze data through a narrow layer and ask for it back, a linear autoencoder rediscovers principal component analysis. But it does so in an arbitrary basis, and it will never fix that, because nothing in the loss can tell the difference.

shapley-values

Conclusion

What you actually have
What a Shapley value gives you
A Shapley value is the unique answer to a precisely stated question: given a payoff for every subset of players, how do you divide the total so that the division is efficient, blind to names, gives nothing to those who add nothing, and is linear in the game. Applied to a model, the players are features, the payoff is the model's average output when only those features are known, and the answer is a number per feature that sums exactly to the distance between this prediction and the average one.
A Shapley value is the unique answer to a precisely stated question: given a payoff for every subset of players, how should we divide the total so that the division is efficient, blind to names, gives nothing to those who add nothing, and is linear in the game? When we apply it to a model, the players are features, the payoff is the model's average output when only those features are known, and the answer is one number per feature, with the numbers summing exactly to the distance between this prediction and the average one.
That is a real guarantee, and it is unusual. Most attribution methods can be argued with on their axioms; this one can only be argued with on whether the axioms were the right ones to want. But the guarantee is narrower than the way these numbers usually get talked about, and the gap is where the trouble lives.
That's a real guarantee, and an unusual one. Most attribution methods can be challenged on whether they satisfy their axioms, but this one can only be challenged on whether those were the right axioms to want. However, the guarantee is narrower than the way these numbers are usually talked about, and that gap is where the trouble lies.
The limits worth knowing before you sign anything
Things to know before you sign off on anything
It explains the model, not the world. A SHAP value attributes f's output. If the model leans on a postcode that happens to correlate with income, the explanation faithfully reports the postcode — and says nothing whatsoever about what would happen to anyone who moved. There is no counterfactual claim in here anywhere. Reading one in is the most common and most expensive misuse of the method.
It explains the model, not the world. A SHAP value attributes f's output. If the model leans on a postcode that happens to correlate with income, the explanation faithfully reports the postcode, but it says nothing at all about what would happen to anyone who moved. There's no counterfactual claim in here anywhere, and reading one into it is the most common and most expensive misuse of the method.
Every value function is a compromise, and you have to pick one. The interventional version used throughout this article replaces the features outside a coalition with values drawn from other listings, which means it asks the model about combinations that do not exist — a 32 m² flat with the distance profile of a suburban house. The model has never seen such a thing and its answer there is extrapolation you are now averaging into an explanation. The conditional alternative stays on the data manifold, but then a feature the model provably ignores can still be paid, because it correlates with one the model uses. There is no third option that avoids both. Choose, and say which you chose.
Every value function is a compromise, and you have to pick one. The interventional version used throughout this article replaces the features outside a coalition with values drawn from other listings, which means it asks the model about combinations that don't exist, such as a 32 m² flat with the distance profile of a suburban house. The model has never seen anything like that, so its answer there is an extrapolation that you're now averaging into an explanation. The conditional alternative stays on the data manifold, but then a feature that the model provably ignores can still get credit, because it correlates with one the model does use. No third option avoids both problems, so choose one, and say which one you chose.
TreeSHAP's default is not the one most people think. Its path-dependent mode estimates a conditional expectation from the training counts stored in the tree; passing a background dataset gives you the interventional value function instead. These are different numbers, and with correlated features they are not close. If a validation report quotes SHAP values without naming the mode and the background, it has not specified the quantity it is reporting.
TreeSHAP's default isn't what most people think it is. Its path-dependent mode estimates a conditional expectation from the training counts stored in the tree, while passing a background dataset gives you the interventional value function instead. These produce different numbers, and with correlated features, they aren't close. If a validation report quotes SHAP values without naming the mode and the background, it hasn't specified the quantity it's reporting.
Global importance is a summary, not a test. Ranking features by mean |\phi_i| describes how much this model moved on this sample. It is not a significance test and it has no null hypothesis: a pure-noise feature will score above zero, and a feature that matters enormously in a region the sample barely covers will score low. It is a description of behaviour, not evidence of relevance.
Global importance is a summary, not a test. Ranking features by mean |\phi_i| describes how much this model moved on this sample. It isn't a significance test, and it has no null hypothesis, so a pure-noise feature will score above zero, and a feature that matters enormously in a region the sample barely covers will score low. It describes the model's behaviour; it isn't evidence of relevance.
Additivity buys uniqueness and charges expressiveness. The output is one number per feature, which is a linear surrogate for a model that is not linear. "These two only matter together" cannot be said in that language; the credit gets split and the joint effect disappears into two halves. Shapley interaction values recover it, at another factor of n in cost.
Additivity buys uniqueness at the cost of expressiveness. The output is one number per feature, which is a linear surrogate for a model that isn't linear. You can't say "these two only matter together" in that language, because the credit gets split and the joint effect disappears into two halves. Shapley interaction values can recover it, at the cost of another factor of n.
Fair is not the same as stable. A sampling estimator returns a different answer each run, and the spread is a function of your budget, not of the model. Quote the estimator, the number of permutations, and ideally the standard error — an attribution reported to four significant figures from 100 samples is claiming a precision it does not have.
Fair isn't the same as stable. A sampling estimator returns a different answer on each run, and the spread depends on your budget, not on the model. So report the estimator, the number of permutations and, ideally, the standard error. An attribution reported to four significant figures from 100 samples is claiming a precision it doesn't have.

It is worth ending where the maths did. Shapley was not thinking about models. He was answering a question about people in rooms, and the answer turned out to depend on nothing about people — only on having a set of participants and a number for every subset of them. Sixty-four years later Lundberg and Lee noticed that a prediction has exactly that shape. The reason SHAP has the properties it has is not that anyone designed them in. They were already there, in a 1953 paper about dividing a payout.
Let's end where the maths began. Shapley wasn't thinking about models. He was answering a question about people in rooms, and the answer turned out not to depend on anything specific to people, only on having a set of participants and a number for every subset of them. Sixty-four years later, Lundberg and Lee noticed that a prediction has exactly that shape. So SHAP doesn't have its properties because anyone designed them in. They were already there, in a 1953 paper about dividing a payout.

Intro

Ada builds things. Bo knows everyone worth knowing. Cleo is the specialist you call when a job has one genuinely hard part in it. Working together for a year, the three of them bill 120.
Meet Ada, Bo and Cleo. Ada builds things, Bo knows everyone worth knowing, and Cleo is the specialist you call when a job has one especially hard part in it. Working together for a year, the three of them bill 120.
Now split it. You know more than just the total, because you also know what every smaller version of this team could have managed. Ada on her own would have found enough work to bill 15. Bo, who can win contracts but has to subcontract the delivery, 30. Cleo, who is superb and unknown, nothing at all — she cannot win a job by herself. Ada and Bo together, 75. Ada and Cleo, 45. Bo and Cleo, 60.
How should they split it? We know more than just the total, because we also know what every smaller version of this team could have managed. On her own, Ada would have found enough work to bill 15. Bo, who can win contracts but has to subcontract the delivery, would have billed 30. Cleo, who is superb but unknown, would have billed nothing at all, since she can't win a job by herself. Together, Ada and Bo would have billed 75, Ada and Cleo 45, and Bo and Cleo 60.
Two answers suggest themselves immediately, and both fall apart on contact.
Two answers come to mind right away, but both fall apart as soon as we try them.
Paying everyone what they could have billed alone hands out 45 and leaves 75 on the table — the entire reason they teamed up is the part nobody gets paid for. Paying everyone what the team would lose without them is worse: it promises 180 against a fee of 120. Both Ada and Bo can truthfully say "drop me and you lose 60" and "drop me and you lose 75", and both are right, and there is no contradiction, because you cannot drop them both.
If we pay everyone what they could have billed alone, we hand out 45 and leave 75 on the table, so the whole reason they teamed up is the part nobody gets paid for. Paying everyone what the team would lose without them is even worse, since it promises 180 against a fee of 120. Ada can truthfully say "drop me and you lose 60", and Bo can say "drop me and you lose 75". Both are right, and there's no contradiction, because you can't drop them both.
That is the actual difficulty. Contributions overlap. Cleo is worth nothing on her own and 45 to whichever pair she joins, so any honest answer has to depend on who she is standing next to — and there is no single fact of the matter about who she is standing next to.
This is the real difficulty: contributions overlap. Cleo is worth nothing on her own and 45 to whichever pair she joins, so any fair answer has to depend on who she's standing next to, and there's no single fact about who she's standing next to.
Lloyd Shapley's move, in 1953, was to stop looking for one. If the credit depends on the order people showed up in, then consider every order they could have shown up in, give each person what they added on arrival, and average.
In 1953, Lloyd Shapley's solution was to stop looking for one. If the credit depends on the order in which people showed up, then we should consider every order they could have shown up in, give each person what they added when they arrived, and take the average. Try it yourself below.

Three players make six orderings, so the table earlier could just show all of them. Ten players make bigNumber. Twenty make bigNumber, which is not a number of anything you are going to enumerate.

Collapsing to coalitions helps enormously — katexify("2^n") instead of katexify("n!") — and for twenty features that is the difference between bigNumber subsets and bigNumber orderings. It is still exponential. A model with forty features has more coalitions than a modern CPU has clock cycles in a month, and forty features is a small model.

The definition is an average, and averages can be estimated. Draw orderings at random, take the marginal contribution in each, and stop when the answer is precise enough. This is the idea behind the sampling estimators, KernelSHAP among them.
The definition is an average, and averages can be estimated. We can draw orderings at random, take the marginal contribution in each one, and stop when the answer is precise enough. This is the idea behind the sampling estimators, including KernelSHAP.
Here it is on an eight-player game where the exact answer is known. Sixteen sampled orderings put the worst-estimated player within CONVERGENCE[2].pct.toFixed% of its true value; CONVERGENCE[7].m gets that to CONVERGENCE[7].pct.toFixed%. The error tracks {katexify("1/\\sqrtm")} — the grey line — which is the usual bad news about Monte Carlo: to halve your error, quadruple your budget.

The consolation is that this is an estimate, so it comes with a standard error. An attribution you can put a confidence interval on is worth more than one you cannot.
The consolation is that this is an estimate, so it comes with a standard error, and an attribution you can put a confidence interval on is worth more than one you can't.
Sampling treats the model as a black box. If you know it is a tree ensemble, you can do far better — and this is where the fourth axiom finally earns its place.
Sampling treats the model as a black box. But if we know it's a tree ensemble, we can do far better, and this is where the fourth axiom finally earns its place.
Additivity says the Shapley values of a sum of games are the sum of their Shapley values. An ensemble's prediction is a sum over trees, so you can attribute each tree separately and add. And a single tree is a much easier object: it only depends on the features that appear on its paths, and the paths can be walked once, keeping track of every subset at the same time. That is TreeSHAP, and it turns an exponential problem into a polynomial one — katexify("O(TLD^2)") for katexify("T") trees of katexify("L") leaves and depth katexify("D"), exactly, with no sampling error at all.
Additivity says that the Shapley values of a sum of games are the sum of their Shapley values. An ensemble's prediction is a sum over trees, so we can attribute each tree separately and add up the results. A single tree is also a much easier object to work with, since it only depends on the features that appear on its paths, and those paths can be walked once while keeping track of every subset at the same time. That's TreeSHAP, and it turns an exponential problem into a polynomial one: katexify("O(TLD^2)") for katexify("T") trees with katexify("L") leaves and depth katexify("D"), computed exactly, with no sampling error at all.
One thing no formula will decide for you: what absent means. Every number in this article is measured against the average prediction over a background sample, and changing that sample changes every attribution in the explanation.
There's one thing no formula will decide for you, and that's what absent means. Every number in this article is measured against the average prediction over a background sample, and changing that sample changes every attribution in the explanation.
Explaining a rejected loan against all applicants, against approved applicants, or against that one customer's own history gives three different and individually correct sets of numbers, answering three different questions. The reference population is part of the explanation, and an explanation that does not state it has not really said anything.
For example, explaining a rejected loan against all applicants, against approved applicants, or against that customer's own history gives three different sets of numbers. Each set is correct, but each answers a different question. The reference population is part of the explanation, and an explanation that doesn't state it hasn't really said anything.
Everything so far has been an existence argument. There is a unique fair attribution and here is what it looks like. Whether you can actually compute it is a separate question, and for a while the honest answer was no.

Works on anything you can call. Costs a chosen number of model evaluations, returns an estimate with a standard error, and slows down linearly in the precision you want squared.

Suppose a model @html "f" has made one prediction about one case, and let's call the features the players. For any subset of them, we can ask: what would the model predict on average if it knew only these features, with everything else looking like a listing drawn at random from the market? That gives us a payoff for every coalition, and the Shapley values of that game are what are now widely known as SHAP values.

In practice nobody enumerates orderings. Many orderings put the same people ahead of i and differ only in the shuffling around it, and those all produce the same marginal contribution. Counting the duplicates — |S|! ways to arrange the people before, and (n - |S| - 1)! ways to arrange the people after — collapses the sum from n! terms to 2^n-1:
In practice, nobody enumerates orderings. Many orderings put the same people ahead of i and differ only in how everyone is shuffled around it, and those orderings all produce the same marginal contribution. If we count the duplicates (|S|! ways to arrange the people before, and (n - |S| - 1)! ways to arrange the people after), the sum collapses from n! terms to 2^n-1:
That fraction is not a mysterious weight. It is the fraction of orderings in which exactly the people in S got there first. With three players it says a marginal contribution to the empty set counts for \tfrac13, to a single colleague \tfrac16, and to the pair \tfrac13 — first and last positions are each one of three, while the middle splits two ways.
That fraction isn't a mysterious weight. It's simply the fraction of orderings in which exactly the people in S got there first. With three players, it says that a marginal contribution to the empty set counts for \tfrac13, a contribution to a single colleague counts for \tfrac16, and a contribution to the pair counts for \tfrac13. That's because arriving first and arriving last each happen in a third of the orderings, while the middle third is split between the two colleagues who could have arrived first.
Why this one and not some other average
Why this average and not another?
Averaging over orderings is a reasonable-sounding idea, but so were the two answers it replaced. The reason to prefer it is a theorem, and the theorem is about four properties.
Averaging over orderings sounds reasonable, but so did the two answers it replaced. The reason to prefer it is a theorem, and that theorem is about four properties.
The payouts add up to what there is. Nothing is invented and nothing is left unclaimed — the failure mode of both first attempts.
The payouts add up to exactly what there is to share. Nothing is invented and nothing is left unclaimed, which is where both of our first attempts failed.
If swapping two players never changes what a group is worth, they are paid the same. Names carry no information; only the value function does.
If swapping two players never changes what a group is worth, they get paid the same. Names carry no information; only the value function does.
Someone who adds nothing to any group gets nothing. Note how strong the condition is: every group, not just the full one.
Someone who adds nothing to any group gets nothing. Notice how strong this condition is: it applies to every group, not just the full one.
Run two projects and settle up once, or settle each separately: same answer. The least intuitive of the four, and the one that will do the most work later.
Whether you run two projects and settle up once, or settle each one separately, you get the same answer. It's the least intuitive of the four, but it's the one that will do the most work later.
Shapley's result is that exactly one way of dividing the payoff satisfies all four at once, and it is the average over orderings. Not one of a family, not the best of several candidates — the only one. Any other rule you can invent breaks at least one of the four, and each of the four is hard to argue with on its own.
Shapley's result is that exactly one way of dividing the payoff satisfies all four properties at once, and it's the average over orderings. It isn't one of a family, or the best of several candidates; it's the only one. Any other rule you can invent breaks at least one of the four, and each of them is hard to argue with on its own.
Two of them you can watch happen. In the game above, switch to A free rider: Cleo now adds nothing to anybody, her column fills with zeros, and her average is zero — that is the null player axiom, arrived at rather than imposed. Switch to Two of a kind and Ada and Bo, who are interchangeable by construction, come out on the same number. Efficiency is on screen the whole time: the three averages always sum to the value of the grand coalition, whatever you drag.
You can watch two of them happen. In the game above, switch to A free rider. Cleo now adds nothing to anybody, so her column fills with zeros and her average is zero. That's the null player axiom, arrived at rather than imposed. Now switch to Two of a kind, and Ada and Bo, who are interchangeable by construction, come out with the same number. Efficiency is on screen the whole time, since the three averages always sum to the value of the grand coalition, whatever you drag.
The last axiom is the quiet one. Additivity says the value is linear in the game, which means if you can decompose a complicated game into simple ones, you can attribute each piece separately and add up. Hold on to that. It is the reason any of this is computable for a model with a thousand trees in it.
The last axiom is the quiet one. Additivity says the value is linear in the game, which means that if you can break a complicated game into simple ones, you can attribute each piece separately and add up the results. Keep that in mind, because it's the reason any of this is computable for a model with a thousand trees in it.

Title

Three people, one fee, and no obvious way to split it. The answer game theory settled in 1953 turns out to be the answer to a question nobody was asking then: what did each feature contribute to this prediction?
Three people share one fee, and there's no obvious way to split it. It turns out that the answer game theory settled on in 1953 also answers a question nobody was asking back then: what did each feature contribute to this prediction?
