smote

Conclusion

What to take from it
What to take away
SMOTE is not a bad idea. It is a specific idea — that a weighted average of two minority rows is a minority row — and it is worth knowing when you are betting on that and when you are not. The bet is safe when every kind of thing in your rare class shows up more than k times, and unsafe as soon as one of them does not.
SMOTE isn't a bad idea. It's a specific idea, namely that a weighted average of two minority rows is itself a minority row, and it's worth knowing when you're betting on that and when you aren't. The bet is safe when every kind of thing in your rare class shows up more than k times, and it becomes unsafe as soon as one of them doesn't.
Which is unfortunate, because the whole reason you reached for SMOTE is that the rare class is rare. The rarer it is, the more of its variety arrives in ones and twos, and the further five nearest neighbours have to reach to find each other. The method is under the most strain exactly where it is used.
Unfortunately, the whole reason you reached for SMOTE is that the rare class is rare. The rarer it is, the more of its variety arrives in ones and twos, and the further the five nearest neighbours have to reach to find each other. In other words, the method is under the most strain exactly where it gets used.
Resample inside the fold, never before the split. This is the one that turns up in real work. Oversample and then cross-validate, and the synthetic rows in your training folds are interpolations of rows sitting in your validation fold — a near-duplicate of the answer, in the training set. Every score comes back beautiful and none of them mean anything. Split first, then resample the training part only.
Resample inside the fold, never before the split. This is the one that keeps turning up in real work. If you oversample and then cross-validate, the synthetic rows in your training folds are interpolations of rows sitting in your validation fold, which puts a near-duplicate of the answer in the training set. Every score comes back looking beautiful, and none of them mean anything. So split first, and then resample only the training part.
There is no λ that makes sense for a categorical feature. Interpolating is_new_device two thirds of the way gives 0.67, which no transaction has ever been. The original paper's SMOTE-NC handles this — majority vote among the neighbours for nominal columns, and a distance that penalises disagreement — but plain SMOTE on one-hot encoded data is manufacturing rows the source system could not emit, and tree-based models will happily split on the difference.
No value of λ makes sense for a categorical feature. Interpolating is_new_device two thirds of the way gives 0.67, which no transaction has ever been. The original paper's SMOTE-NC handles this by taking a majority vote among the neighbours for nominal columns and using a distance that penalises disagreement. But plain SMOTE on one-hot encoded data manufactures rows that the source system could never emit, and tree-based models will happily split on the difference.
It inherits every property of the distance you gave it. SMOTE is a k-nearest-neighbours method wearing a hat, so the scaler is part of the algorithm: run it on raw euros and hours and you get a different set of synthetic rows than on standardised ones. In high dimensions it gets worse — Blagus and Lusa found that as the feature count grows the neighbours stop being meaningfully near, and for most classifiers SMOTE stops doing anything at all.
It inherits every property of the distance you give it. SMOTE is essentially a k-nearest-neighbours method in disguise, so the scaler is part of the algorithm. If you run it on raw euros and hours, you'll get a different set of synthetic rows than you would on standardised ones. In high dimensions, it gets worse. Blagus and Lusa found that as the number of features grows, the neighbours stop being meaningfully near, and for most classifiers, SMOTE stops doing anything at all.
Afterwards, the probabilities are wrong. A model trained on a balanced set is estimating probabilities under a prior you invented. If anything downstream consumes the number rather than the ranking — an expected-loss threshold, a queue sized by risk, a report of "how likely is this" — it needs correcting back, or it is quietly miscounting.
Afterwards, the probabilities are wrong. A model trained on a balanced set estimates probabilities under a prior you invented. If anything downstream uses the number itself rather than the ranking (for example, an expected-loss threshold, a queue sized by risk, or a report of "how likely is this?"), the probabilities need correcting back, or that process will quietly miscount.
Before generating anything, move the threshold. It costs one line, it leaves the data alone, the probabilities stay honest, and on all three label sets here it landed exactly where SMOTE did. If it is not enough — and for weak learners it often is not — then resample, in the fold, with k no larger than the smallest genuine subgroup you believe is in there, and prefer a variant that generates from the border rather than from everything.
Before generating anything, try moving the threshold. It takes one line of code, leaves the data alone and keeps the probabilities honest, and on all three label sets here, it landed exactly where SMOTE did. If that isn't enough (and for weak learners, it often isn't), then resample inside the fold, with k no larger than the smallest real subgroup you believe is in there, and prefer a variant that generates from the border rather than from everything.
And check where the synthetic rows landed. The rate this article draws needs a generative model, which real data does not come with — but a version that does not needs three lines. For each synthetic row, take the distance to its third nearest real minority row and to its third nearest majority row, and scale each by how many rows of that class there are. Whichever side wins is the denser class there, at equal priors. On the label set that broke here, that check agrees with the density one about AGREE of the time and reports a rate of CHEAP against the true TRUE_RATE.
Finally, check where the synthetic rows landed. The rate this article plots needs a generative model, which real data doesn't come with, but a version that doesn't need one takes just three lines. For each synthetic row, take the distance to its third nearest real minority row and to its third nearest majority row, and scale each distance by how many rows of that class there are. Whichever side wins is the denser class at that point, assuming equal priors. On the label set that broke here, this check agrees with the density-based one about AGREE of the time, and it reports a rate of CHEAP compared with the true TRUE_RATE.
Do not skip the scaling, incidentally. Ask the unweighted question — is the nearest real row fraud or legitimate — and the answer is dominated by the forty-to-one imbalance rather than by anything about the synthetic row, which is how the same data reports NAIVE instead. None of this tells you SMOTE was wrong. It tells you where it made a claim, which is the part nobody is currently looking at.

All prose, data, code and figures here are original. The fraud data is synthetic and isn't a model of any real payment system, and every number can be reproduced from scripts/precompute.mjs and is asserted in verify/. The comparison in the last section uses one classifier on one two-dimensional problem, which is enough to show a mechanism but not enough to settle the question.

Intro

A year of card transactions, HOOK.majority.length + H… of them, and HOOK.minority.length are fraud. That is the situation, and the awkward thing about it is that the data rewards a model for ignoring the question entirely: predict legitimate every time and you are right 1 - HOOK.minority.length… of the time. A nearest-neighbours classifier trained on exactly this data, at the usual threshold, does something very close to that — it catches 1 - hard.none.atHalf.miss of the fraud.
Suppose we have a month of card transactions, HOOK.majority.length + H… of them, and HOOK.minority.length are fraud. The awkward thing about this situation is that the data rewards a model for ignoring the question entirely. If we predict legitimate every time, we're right 1 - HOOK.minority.length… of the time. A nearest-neighbours classifier trained on exactly this data, at the usual threshold, does something very close to that: it catches 1 - hard.none.atHalf.miss of the fraud.
You cannot go and collect more fraud. So the standard move is to manufacture it. SMOTE — Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique — takes a fraud row, finds its k nearest fraud neighbours, picks one of them, and writes down a new fraud row somewhere on the straight line between the two. Repeat until the classes are the same size.
We can't just go out and collect more fraud, so the standard move is to manufacture it. SMOTE (Synthetic Minority Over-sampling Technique) takes a fraud row, finds its k nearest fraud neighbours, picks one of them, and writes down a new fraud row somewhere on the straight line between the two. It repeats this until the classes are the same size.
That is the whole method. It is twenty-odd lines of code, it has been the default answer to class imbalance for twenty years, and it is one import away in every toolbox anyone reaches for.
That's the whole method. It takes twenty-odd lines of code, it has been the default answer to class imbalance for twenty years, and it's one import away in just about every toolbox.
It also contains an assumption, stated so quietly that it is easy to miss: that the space between two fraud rows is itself fraud. Not that it might be. That it is — enough to label a point there and hand it to a classifier as evidence.
However, it also contains an assumption that's stated so quietly it's easy to miss: that the space between two fraud rows is itself fraud. The method doesn't just assume that it might be; it assumes that it is, strongly enough to label a point there and hand it to a classifier as evidence.
Below is the data. Amount runs left to right on a log scale, time of day runs bottom to top, and both are standardised before anything measures a distance, because "how far is €40 from 3am" has no answer until somebody picks one. Red points are the labelled fraud. The shaded regions are where fraud genuinely is the more common of the two classes — a luxury of synthetic data, and the thing we get to check the method against.

Two readings of the same picture. The first is that the default is not a safe default — it is a bet that every kind of fraud you have labelled shows up at least K_DEFAULT times, and on the left-hand panel that bet wins at every k while on the right it has already lost at k = K_DEFAULT. The second is that nothing in the method can tell you which panel you are on. The rate on the y axis is computable here only because the data is invented.

Whether interpolation is safe is not a property of SMOTE. It is a property of the shape your minority class happens to have, and of how that shape compares to k — which you almost certainly left at K_DEFAULT. Three label sets, same algorithm, same default.

Written out, the step you just watched is a convex combination — a weighted average of two points, with the weights adding to one:
If we write it out, the step you just watched is a convex combination, which is a weighted average of two points with weights that add up to one:
@html inlineLambda is drawn uniformly, so the new row is equally likely to land anywhere on the segment. Two things follow immediately, and neither of them depends on the data, the value of k, or how many rows you generate.
Since @html inlineLambda is drawn uniformly, the new row is equally likely to land anywhere on the segment. Two things follow immediately, and neither of them depends on the data, the value of k, or how many rows we generate.
1. SMOTE cannot leave the hull
1. SMOTE can't leave the hull
A weighted average of two points from a set is inside that set's convex hull, and so is a weighted average of averages. Whatever SMOTE produces, the outline of the minority class does not move:
A weighted average of two points from a set lies inside that set's convex hull, and so does a weighted average of such averages. So whatever SMOTE produces, the outline of the minority class doesn't move:
The sweep behind the next two sections generates HULL_TRIALS synthetic rows — every scenario, every k from 1 to HOOK.minority.length - 1. The number of them that landed outside the hull of the rows they were built from is HULL_ESCAPES, and it is not {HULL_ESCAPES === 0 ? The sweep behind the next two sections generates HULL_TRIALS synthetic rows, covering every scenario and every k from 1 to HOOK.minority.length - 1. The number of them that landed outside the hull of the rows they were built from is HULL_ESCAPES, and it's not {HULL_ESCAPES === 0 ? "zero because the sample was lucky" : "small by luck"}. It's zero because it can't be anything else.
So the thing oversampling is usually sold as doing — giving the classifier a richer, more varied picture of the rare class — is exactly the thing it cannot do. It has no mechanism for a fraud larger than the largest one you logged, or earlier than the earliest. It fills in; it never reaches out.
This means that what oversampling is usually sold as doing, giving the classifier a richer and more varied picture of the rare class, is exactly what it can't do. It has no way to produce a fraud larger than the largest one you logged, or earlier than the earliest one. It fills in, but it never reaches out.
2. And at large k it pulls inward
2. At large k, it pulls inward
There is a second, quieter effect. If the neighbour is drawn uniformly from every other point — SMOTE at k = n-1 — the covariance of the synthetic cloud is not the covariance of the real one but a fixed fraction of it, where @html inlineS is the sample covariance of the @html inlineN real points:
There's also a second, quieter effect. If the neighbour is drawn uniformly from every other point (SMOTE at k = n-1), the covariance of the synthetic cloud isn't the covariance of the real one, but a fixed fraction of it, where @html inlineS is the sample covariance of the @html inlineN real points:
For the HOOK.minority.length fraud rows here that is num(VAR_IDENTITY, 3) — a third of the variance gone, not as an artefact of any particular sample but as a property of averaging pairs. The measured ratio, computed exactly from the same points, is num(atFull, 3).
For the HOOK.minority.length fraud rows here, that's num(VAR_IDENTITY, 3). In other words, a third of the variance is gone, not because of any particular sample, but as a property of averaging pairs. The measured ratio, computed exactly from the same points, is num(atFull, 3).
It is worth being precise about when that matters, because it is usually quoted as a general warning against SMOTE and it is not one. At the default k = K_DEFAULT the same calculation on the same points gives num(atDefault, 3): the synthetic cloud is very slightly wider than the real one, not narrower. The contraction is a large-k phenomenon and at the setting almost everybody uses it is simply not happening.

Title

The whole algorithm is one line: put a new minority point somewhere on the segment between two old ones. That line is a claim about everything in between — and it is the only thing the method knows.
The whole algorithm fits on one line: put a new minority point somewhere on the segment between two existing ones. That line makes a claim about everything in between, and as we'll see, it's the only thing the method knows.

Verdict

So what did it buy?
So what did it buy us?
Putting bad rows in a training set is only a problem if it changes what the model does, and the honest way to find out is to look at the boundary. Below, on the hardest of the three label sets: what a nearest-neighbours classifier learns from the SMOTE-balanced data, against what the same classifier learns from the original data with nothing done to it except moving the threshold at which it says "fraud".
Putting bad rows in a training set is only a problem if it changes what the model does, and the most reliable way to find out is to look at the decision boundary. The chart below uses the hardest of the three label sets. It compares what a nearest-neighbours classifier learns from the SMOTE-balanced data with what the same classifier learns from the original data, where the only change is moving the threshold at which it says "fraud".
There is a third boundary that cannot be drawn: the original data at the usual threshold of 0.5 has none, anywhere on the plane. It never says fraud.
There's a third boundary that can't be drawn: the original data at the usual threshold of 0.5 has no boundary anywhere on the plane, because it never says fraud.
They track each other. Both find all three places the fraud actually is and draw much the same line around each of them; what is left is ragged detail along the edges. Across the whole plane the two classifiers disagree about hard.disagreement.cells, 1 of it — hard.disagreement.weight… once you weight by where the transactions actually are — and on the two easier label sets that falls to {VERDICT.slice(0, 2).map((v) => pct(v.disagreement.weighted, 1)).join(" and ")}. Two quite different procedures, one of which wrote down hard.smote.synthetic rows that nobody observed, arriving at nearly the same classifier.
The two boundaries track each other closely. Both find all three places where the fraud actually is and draw much the same line around each of them, leaving only ragged detail along the edges. Integrated over the plane against the true class densities, so these are not estimates. The "threshold moved" rows use the best threshold there is for this metric — an oracle, and therefore the most generous possible reading of that option.
These are integrated over the plane against the true class densities, so they aren't estimates. The "threshold moved" rows use the best possible threshold for this metric, which makes them an oracle, and therefore the most generous possible reading of that option.
SMOTE does an enormous amount of good here. On the hardest label set it takes a classifier that catches none of the fraud and gets it to 1 - hard.smote.atHalf.miss — and on the other two it is a similar story. Anyone who has watched a model refuse to predict the rare class knows exactly why the method is popular.
SMOTE does an enormous amount of good here. On the hardest label set, it takes a classifier that catches none of the fraud and gets it to 1 - hard.smote.atHalf.miss, and it's a similar story on the other two. Anyone who has watched a model refuse to predict the rare class knows exactly why the method is so popular.
And in all three, so does moving the threshold — at no cost, without touching the data, without inventing a row. On the hardest label set that is hard.none.tuned.balanced… against SMOTE's hard.smote.atHalf.balanc…; on the other two, {VERDICT.map((v) => pct(v.none.tuned.balanced, 1) + " against " + pct(v.smote.atHalf.balanced, 1)).slice(0, 2).join(" and ")}. Every one of those gaps is a tenth of a point, which is a dead heat — except that it is a dead heat in the same direction three times, and one of the two contestants required generating hard.smote.synthetic rows that were not in the data.
But in all three cases, simply moving the threshold does just as much good, at no cost, without touching the data, and without inventing a single row. On the hardest label set, it achieves hard.none.tuned.balanced… compared with SMOTE's hard.smote.atHalf.balanc…, and on the other two, {VERDICT.map((v) => pct(v.none.tuned.balanced, 1) + " against " + pct(v.smote.atHalf.balanced, 1)).slice(0, 2).join(" and ")}. Each of those gaps is a tenth of a point, which is a dead heat. However, it's a dead heat in the same direction three times, and one of the two contestants had to generate hard.smote.synthetic rows that weren't in the data.
The two remedies built to patch exactly the failure this article is about — generating only from the border, and cleaning up afterwards — do not change that. On the hardest set they come in at hard.borderline.atHalf.b… and hard.enn.atHalf.balanced…, a few tenths either side of plain SMOTE and still not clear of the threshold. They are fixing the symptom this article measures, and the thing they are competing against never had it.
The two remedies designed to patch exactly the failure this article is about, generating only from the border and cleaning up afterwards, don't change that. On the hardest set, they come in at hard.borderline.atHalf.b… and hard.enn.atHalf.balanced…, a few tenths either side of plain SMOTE, and still not clearly ahead of the threshold. They're fixing the symptom this article measures, but the approach they're competing against never had that symptom in the first place.
This is not a novel finding and it is worth saying so. Elor and Averbuch-Elor compared balancing across weak learners and strong modern classifiers and found it helped the weak ones and not the strong; van den Goorbergh and colleagues found that imbalance corrections left discrimination unchanged while wrecking calibration, and that adjusting the threshold got the same classifications without the damage. What the picture above adds is where the damage comes from: not from resampling as an abstraction, but from a specific assertion about specific points, which you can see being made.
This isn't a new finding, and it's worth saying so. Elor and Averbuch-Elor compared balancing across weak learners and strong modern classifiers, and found that it helped the weak ones but not the strong ones. Van den Goorbergh and colleagues found that imbalance corrections left discrimination unchanged while wrecking calibration, and that adjusting the threshold gave the same classifications without the damage. What the picture above adds is where the damage comes from. It doesn't come from resampling as an abstract idea, but from a specific claim about specific points, which you can watch being made.

f1-score

Conclusion

What to Take From It
What to Take Away
The F_1 score is not a flawed mathematical formula. It does exactly what it was defined to do: calculate the harmonic mean of precision and recall.
The F_1 score isn't a flawed mathematical formula. It does exactly what it was defined to do, which is to calculate the harmonic mean of precision and recall.
The mistake is treating it as a neutral summary metric suitable for operational decision-making.
The mistake is treating it as a neutral summary metric that's suitable for operational decision-making.
When you tune a decision threshold to maximize F_1, you are not taking a middle path between precision and recall. You are asserting a precise, unchosen cost ratio:
When you tune a decision threshold to maximize F_1, you aren't taking a middle path between precision and recall. Instead, you're asserting a precise cost ratio that nobody chose, and that has three consequences:
The rule is about your model, not your problem. In expectation, the optimal rule is always t* = F1max / 2. If your model gets better, the threshold moves up and you penalise false alarms more; if your model degrades, it moves down. The reality of pump breakdowns did not change.
The rule is about your model, not your problem. In expectation, the optimal rule is always t* = F1max / 2. If your model gets better, the threshold moves up and you penalise false alarms more, and if your model degrades, it moves down, even though the reality of pump breakdowns hasn't changed.
Finite validation sets inject massive noise. Finding the argmax on an empirical sample of 6,000 units yielded thresholds spanning from 0.124 to 0.386, swinging your asserted tradeoff from 1.6× to 7.0× purely by sample luck.
Finite validation sets add a lot of noise. Finding the argmax on an empirical sample of 6,000 units gave thresholds ranging from 0.124 to 0.386, which swings your asserted trade-off from 1.6× to 7.0× purely through sample luck.
Prevalence drift quietly shifts your operational goals. When failure frequency rises from 1.95% to 12.3%, F_1^\max surges from 0.399 to 0.590 and the implied cost ratio drops from 4.01× to 2.39× on a frozen model.
Prevalence drift quietly shifts your operational goals. When the failure rate rises from 1.95% to 12.3%, F_1^\max surges from 0.399 to 0.590, and the implied cost ratio drops from 4.01× to 2.39×, all on a frozen model.
When F1 is appropriate (and when it is not)
When F1 is appropriate (and when it isn't)
When it works: In academic benchmarks, competitive leaderboards, and general information retrieval. If you are comparing two ranking models on a standardized dataset where downstream deployment costs are genuinely unknown or unquantifiable, F_1 serves as a reasonable single-number proxy to prevent either extreme (predicting all positive or all negative).
When it works: F1 is useful in academic benchmarks, competitive leaderboards and general information retrieval. If you're comparing two ranking models on a standardized dataset where the downstream deployment costs are unknown or can't be quantified, F_1 is a reasonable single-number proxy that guards against either extreme (predicting all positive or all negative).
When it fails: In production systems that trigger real actions — preventive maintenance, fraud blocking, medical screening, loan underwriting, or spam filtering. In every one of these domains, false positives and false negatives carry asymmetric, real-world costs.
When it fails: F1 falls short in production systems that trigger real actions, such as preventive maintenance, fraud blocking, medical screening, loan underwriting or spam filtering. In every one of these domains, false positives and false negatives carry asymmetric, real-world costs.
Before sliding a threshold until F_1 peaks, have the conversation everyone avoids: ask what a false alarm costs, ask what a missed event costs, and calibrate your probabilities. Once you have honest probabilities and explicit costs, decision theory does the rest — with no magic metrics required.
So before sliding a threshold until F_1 peaks, have the conversation everyone avoids: ask what a false alarm costs, ask what a missed event costs, and calibrate your probabilities. Once you have honest probabilities and explicit costs, decision theory does the rest, with no magic metrics required.

CostFigure

If maximising F_1 produces an arbitrary, noise-sensitive threshold that drifts with the base rate, what should an engineer do instead?

Setting \beta = 2 weights recall twice as heavily as precision; \beta = 0.5 weights precision twice as heavily. Unlike F_1, which pretends to be neutral while enforcing an arbitrary ratio, F_\beta forces the designer to make their preference explicit.

Here's the catch: F1 isn't a property of the model alone. It depends heavily on the base rate (or prevalence) of the positive class.

Intro

You have trained a machine learning model to monitor industrial coolant pumps across a refinery fleet. For each pump, the model outputs a score: the probability that the pump will suffer a catastrophic mechanical failure within the next 48 hours.
Imagine you've trained a machine learning model to monitor industrial coolant pumps across a refinery fleet. For each pump, the model outputs a score: the probability that the pump will suffer a catastrophic mechanical failure within the next 48 hours.
A score alone does not run a plant. To take action, someone has to choose a decision threshold: every pump scored above that threshold gets an emergency maintenance dispatch; every pump below it is left running.
A score alone doesn't run a plant, though. To take action, someone has to choose a decision threshold. Every pump that scores above the threshold gets an emergency maintenance dispatch, and every pump below it is left running.
The operations team can name two very real costs. An inspection is quick and relatively inexpensive, but sending a technician to a healthy pump is a wasted false alarm. An unplanned breakdown, on the other hand, halts a production line for days.
The operations team can name two very real costs. An inspection is quick and relatively cheap, but sending a technician to a healthy pump is a wasted false alarm. An unplanned breakdown, on the other hand, can halt a production line for days.
How do practitioners choose that threshold? In machine learning textbooks, Kaggle competitions, and production teams worldwide, almost everyone follows the same received recipe: evaluate the model on a validation set and pick the threshold that maximises the F1 score.
So how do practitioners choose that threshold? In machine learning textbooks, Kaggle competitions and production teams around the world, almost everyone follows the same recipe: evaluate the model on a validation set and pick the threshold that maximises the F1 score.

SpreadFigure

A common reaction to these identities is reassurance: "Sure, but I have a large validation set. Six thousand pumps is plenty of data to find the right threshold."
A common reaction to these results is to reassure yourself: "Sure, but I have a large validation set. Six thousand pumps is plenty of data to find the right threshold."
It is not. While 6,000 total pumps sounds large, a ~3% failure rate means your validation set contains only ~182 actual failures. On an empirical sample, the precision-recall curve is a jagged staircase. Small variations in which pump happens to fail first create wild local fluctuations in the argmax of F_1.

TheReveal

The best this validation set has to offer is an F_1 of 0.512, at a threshold of 0.158. Run the same model over the whole fleet rather than these six thousand rows and the true maximum is 0.5105, at a threshold of 0.2552.
The best this validation set has to offer is an F_1 of 0.512, at a threshold of 0.158. But if we run the same model over the whole fleet rather than just these six thousand rows, the true maximum is 0.5105, at a threshold of 0.2552.
Now notice that your two numbers are not related that way at all. Half of 0.512 is 0.256, and you landed on 0.158 — a long way off, and not because you hunted badly. There was nothing better to find: 0.158 really is where F_1 peaks on the rows you were given. Both of those facts matter, and the second one turns out to matter more. Take the theorem first.
Now notice that your two numbers aren't related that way at all. Half of 0.512 is 0.256, but you landed on 0.158, which is a long way off. That isn't because you hunted badly. There was nothing better to find, since 0.158 really is where F_1 peaks on the rows you were given. Both of these facts matter, and the second one turns out to matter more. But let's start with the theorem.
This is not a coincidence of pump mechanics or random seeds. It is a theorem. In 2014, Zachary Lipton, Charles Elkan, and Balakrishnan Narayanaswamy proved that for any calibrated probability classifier, the optimal threshold that maximises expected F1 has an exact closed form: act whenever the score exceeds @html inlineF1Max / 2.
The half isn't a coincidence of pump mechanics or random seeds; it's a theorem. In 2014, Zachary Lipton, Charles Elkan and Balakrishnan Narayanaswamy proved that for any calibrated probabilistic classifier, the threshold that maximises expected F1 has an exact closed form: act whenever the score exceeds @html inlineF1Max / 2.

Title

Maximising F1 on a validation set is the standard way to pick a classification threshold. The rule has a closed form, it is not about your problem, and it asserts a cost ratio nobody chose.
Maximising F1 on a validation set is the standard way to pick a classification threshold. As we'll see, the rule has a closed form, it isn't about your problem, and it quietly asserts a cost ratio that nobody chose.

Of course not. That number was never an engineering estimate; it came entirely from F_1^\max / 2. In other words, the threshold came from the model’s own achievable accuracy, not from the reality of the plant.

This leads to an unnerving inversion. Suppose your team spends three months engineering better vibration features, improving the model so that its achievable F_1^\max climbs from 0.51 to 0.70.
What does the F1-maximising recipe do? Because t^* = F_1^\max / 2, the threshold jumps from 0.255 to 0.350. The implied cost ratio plunges from 2.92× down to 1.86×. By improving the model, you have quietly decided that missing an engine blowout is suddenly less serious than it was last month.
What does the F1-maximising recipe do then? Because t^* = F_1^\max / 2, the threshold jumps from 0.255 to 0.350, and the implied cost ratio plunges from 2.92× to 1.86×. By improving the model, you've quietly decided that missing a catastrophic pump failure is suddenly less serious than it was last month.
Conversely, if a camera fails and the model degrades to F_1^\max = 0.30, the optimal threshold drops to 0.150, asserting an implied ratio of 5.67×. The physical consequences of a pump failure have not changed by a single cent. Yet the metric drastically alters the operational policy based solely on how well the algorithm is doing.
Conversely, if a sensor fails and the model degrades to F_1^\max = 0.30, the optimal threshold drops to 0.150, which asserts an implied ratio of 5.67×. The physical consequences of a pump failure haven't changed by a single cent, yet the metric drastically changes the operational policy based solely on how well the algorithm is doing.

dbscan-hdbscan

Borders

DBSCAN is not deterministic
DBSCAN isn't deterministic
Back to the border pings from the walkthrough: not crowded enough to be core, close enough to a core ping to be swept in. The original definition says a border ping belongs to a cluster it is density-reachable from — and if it is reachable from two, it does not say which. Every implementation resolves that the same way, by giving it to whichever cluster gets there first, which is to say by the order of the rows.
Let's return to the border pings from the walkthrough, which aren't crowded enough to be core but are close enough to a core ping to be swept in. The original definition says a border ping belongs to a cluster it's density-reachable from, but if it's reachable from two clusters, it doesn't say which one. Every implementation resolves that the same way, by giving the ping to whichever cluster gets there first, which means the answer depends on the order of the rows. Try shuffling them below.
Sweeping eps and repeating this pre.order.trials times at each value, the worst it gets on this day is pre.order.partitions different answers from pre.order.trials orderings at eps = num(pre.order.eps, 2) m, with pre.order.flips pings moving between clusters. There are pre.order.border border pings at that setting, and they are exactly the ones that can move.
If we sweep eps and repeat this pre.order.trials times at each value, the worst case on this day is pre.order.partitions different answers from pre.order.trials orderings at eps = num(pre.order.eps, 2) m, with pre.order.flips pings moving between clusters. There are pre.order.border border pings at that setting, and they're exactly the ones that can move.
Small, and it is not nothing: df.sample(frac=1) before you cluster and your cluster sizes change. It is also why HDBSCAN is built on DBSCAN*, in which a non-core ping is simply noise. A hierarchy needs the clusters at each level to nest inside the clusters at the level above, and a ping that belongs to whichever neighbour asked first does not nest. So hdbscan with cluster_selection_epsilon set is not the same algorithm as DBSCAN(eps=…), and the difference is precisely these pings.
That's a small effect, but it isn't nothing: if you run df.sample(frac=1) before clustering, your cluster sizes change. It's also why HDBSCAN is built on DBSCAN*, in which a non-core ping is simply noise. A hierarchy needs the clusters at each level to nest inside the clusters at the level above, and a ping that belongs to whichever neighbour asked first doesn't nest. So hdbscan with cluster_selection_epsilon set isn't the same algorithm as DBSCAN(eps=…), and the difference is precisely these pings.

Conclusion

What to take from it
What to take away
DBSCAN's answer has one number in it. When your clusters really do share a density, that is the right shape of answer, and DBSCAN is an excellent thing to reach for: it is fast, it does not care what shape anything is, and it will tell you when a row belongs nowhere. When they do not share a density, no value of that number is right — not a hard-to-find one, not a compromise, none — because the branches of the tree it is slicing do not line up. HDBSCAN's contribution is not a cleverer way of choosing the number. It is an answer that is allowed to have several.
DBSCAN's answer has one number in it. When your clusters really do share a density, that's the right shape of answer, and DBSCAN is an excellent tool to reach for. It's fast, it doesn't care what shape anything is, and it tells you when a row belongs nowhere. But when the clusters don't share a density, no value of that number is right (not a hard-to-find one, and not a compromise either), because the branches of the tree it's slicing don't line up. HDBSCAN's contribution isn't a cleverer way of choosing that number. It's an answer that's allowed to contain several.
The practical version, and the one thing worth taking away if nothing else is: build the tree before you tune anything. You do not need labels to look at it. Condense the hierarchy at some sensible min_cluster_size, look at the branches you believe in, and check whether any horizontal line crosses all of them. If one does, DBSCAN will work and the tree has just told you which eps to use — for free, without a grid search, and without you having chosen an answer because you liked how it looked. If none does, you have learned the most useful thing about your data that any of this was going to tell you.
If you take away just one practical lesson, make it this: build the tree before you tune anything. You don't need labels to look at it. Condense the hierarchy at a sensible min_cluster_size, look at the branches you believe in, and check whether any horizontal line crosses all of them. If one does, DBSCAN will work, and the tree has just told you which eps to use, for free, without a grid search, and without you choosing an answer because you liked how it looked. If none does, you've learned the most useful thing about your data that any of this could have told you.
The usual warning about dimension is the wrong warning. Three well-separated Gaussian blobs, with every extra axis pure noise: the best achievable agreement stays at 1.000 all the way to eighty dimensions. Nothing fails. What collapses is the window.
The usual warning about dimension is the wrong warning. Take three well-separated Gaussian blobs and make every extra axis pure noise. The best achievable agreement stays at 1.000 all the way up to eighty dimensions, so nothing fails. What collapses is the window.
The last column is distance concentration happening in front of you: at two dimensions a point's nearest neighbour is num(100 * lo.ratio, 1)% as far away as the nearest point from another cluster, and at eighty it is num(100 * hi.ratio, 0)%. Everything is becoming equidistant, and the range of thresholds that separates the clusters narrows from num(lo.width, 1) to num(hi.width, 1), a factor of num. So the algorithm does not stop working in high dimensions. It stops being tunable, which is worse, because it fails silently and looks like a parameter problem.
The last column shows distance concentration happening in front of you. At two dimensions, a point's nearest neighbour is num(100 * lo.ratio, 1)% as far away as the nearest point from another cluster, and at eighty dimensions, it's num(100 * hi.ratio, 0)%. Everything is becoming equidistant, and the range of thresholds that separates the clusters narrows from num(lo.width, 1) to num(hi.width, 1), a factor of num. So the algorithm doesn't stop working in high dimensions. Instead, it stops being tunable, which is worse, because it fails silently and looks like a parameter problem.
The scaler is part of the algorithm. Both of these are distance methods wearing a hat, so whatever you did to the columns before they arrived is part of the model. This article had the luxury of metres on both axes; almost nothing else does, and if eps is in units of a StandardScaler fitted on the training set, then refitting the scaler changes the clustering.
The scaler is part of the algorithm. Both of these are distance methods at heart, so whatever you did to the columns before they arrived is part of the model. This article had the luxury of metres on both axes, but almost nothing else does, and if eps is in the units of a StandardScaler fitted on the training set, then refitting the scaler changes the clustering.
Nothing here is a probability, and stability is not a p-value. There is no null model anywhere in this. "We found seven clusters" is not evidence that seven things exist, and a large stability is not evidence that a branch is real — it is a statement about how much density sat above a level, on this sample, with no reference distribution to compare it against. HDBSCAN's per-point membership strengths are a monotone function of a distance and are not posteriors, whatever they look like on a colour scale. If you need to defend the number of clusters, you need a resampling argument on top: cluster many bootstrap draws and count how often a branch survives.
Nothing here is a probability, and stability isn't a p-value. There's no null model anywhere in this. "We found seven clusters" isn't evidence that seven things exist, and a large stability isn't evidence that a branch is real. It's a statement about how much density sat above a level in this sample, with no reference distribution to compare it against. HDBSCAN's per-point membership strengths are a monotone function of a distance, not posterior probabilities, whatever they look like on a colour scale. If you need to defend the number of clusters, you need a resampling argument on top, such as clustering many bootstrap draws and counting how often each branch survives.
Neither of them predicts. Both label the rows you gave them and nothing else. approximate_predict does something sensible — it works out where a new point would have entered the existing tree — but it does not re-cluster, so a genuinely new cluster arriving is invisible until you refit. Anything running on a schedule needs a monitor for that, and the monitor is not "did the model error"; it is something like the share of new rows landing in noise, tracked over time.
Neither of them predicts. Both label the rows you gave them and nothing else. The approximate_predict function does something sensible, working out where a new point would have entered the existing tree, but it doesn't re-cluster, so a brand-new cluster stays invisible until you refit. Anything running on a schedule needs a monitor for that, and the monitor shouldn't be "did the model throw an error?". It should be something like the share of new rows landing in noise, tracked over time.
Two knobs, not one. min_samples sets the core distance and therefore how aggressively thin structure is suppressed; min_cluster_size sets the smallest thing you are willing to call a cluster. They default to being equal in the usual implementation and they are answering different questions. Turn the first up if filaments are bridging things; turn the second up if you are getting clusters you would not act on.
There are two knobs, not one. min_samples sets the core distance, and therefore how aggressively thin structure is suppressed, while min_cluster_size sets the smallest thing you're willing to call a cluster. They're equal by default in the usual implementation, but they answer different questions. Turn the first one up if filaments are bridging things, and turn the second one up if you're getting clusters you wouldn't act on.
And the cost is not the thing that gets criticised. Both are near-linear in low dimensions with a spatial index and quadratic without one; the implementations here are the naive quadratic ones, on a few hundred points, deliberately, so that everything on the page is re-derivable from a page of code. The complexity argument about DBSCAN has been had properly elsewhere, and the conclusion was that the index was on trial rather than the algorithm.
Cost isn't the real problem. Both methods are near-linear in low dimensions with a spatial index, and quadratic without one. The implementations here are deliberately the naive quadratic ones, running on a few hundred points, so that everything on the page can be re-derived from a page of code. The debate about DBSCAN's complexity has been settled properly elsewhere, and the conclusion was that the index, not the algorithm, was what was really on trial.

Condensed

The tree has every answer in it and DBSCAN takes one horizontal slice. The obvious alternative is to go branch by branch and ask a local question: is this branch worth keeping, on its own terms? HDBSCAN is that question, made precise in two steps.
The tree contains every answer, and DBSCAN takes one horizontal slice of it. The obvious alternative is to go branch by branch and ask a local question: is this branch worth keeping, on its own terms? HDBSCAN makes that question precise in two steps.
One: throw away the branches that are not places
Step one: throw away the branches that aren't places
Most of the tree is noise in the literal sense — two pings that happened to be near each other, forming a branch of size two that exists over a few centimetres of eps before something absorbs it. So pick a min_cluster_size and walk down from the root. At each split, if both sides are at least that big, it was a real split and both sides become clusters. If only one side is, the small side did not split off — it fell off, and its pings leave the cluster at that level while the cluster carries on. If neither is, the cluster has come apart, and everything still in it leaves.
Most of the tree is noise in the literal sense. For example, two pings that happened to be near each other form a branch of size two that exists over a few centimetres of eps before something absorbs it. So we pick a min_cluster_size and walk down from the root. At each split, if both sides are at least that big, it was a real split, and both sides become clusters. If only one side is, the small side didn't split off; it fell off, and its pings leave the cluster at that level while the cluster carries on. If neither side is big enough, the cluster has come apart, and everything still in it leaves.
That is called condensing, and it turns a tree with hundreds of internal nodes into one with a handful. Crucially the parameter is a count of pings, not a distance. "I do not care about places I visited for under ten minutes" is an opinion you can have. "I do not care about places smaller than 8.4 metres" is not really an opinion about anything.
This process is called condensing, and it turns a tree with hundreds of internal nodes into one with just a handful. Crucially, the parameter is a count of pings, not a distance. "I don't care about places I visited for under ten minutes" is an opinion you can actually have, whereas "I don't care about places smaller than 8.4 metres" isn't really an opinion about anything.
Two: keep the branches that persist
Step two: keep the branches that persist

A ping that joined the cluster at its birth and stayed until the cluster died contributes the full height of the branch; one that dropped out early contributes a sliver. Add them up and you have the branch's stability — literally the mass of density sitting above the level where the cluster came into existence. Then choose greedily, from the bottom up:
A ping that joined the cluster at its birth and stayed until the cluster died contributes the full height of the branch, while one that dropped out early contributes only a sliver. Adding these up gives the branch's stability, which is literally the mass of density sitting above the level where the cluster came into existence. Then we choose greedily, from the bottom up:
Keep a branch if it is more stable than everything below it put together; otherwise pass its children up unchanged. The root is not eligible, because "it is all one cluster" is an answer HDBSCAN declines to give.
In other words, we keep a branch if it's more stable than everything below it put together, and otherwise we pass its children up unchanged. The root isn't eligible, because "it's all one cluster" is an answer HDBSCAN declines to give. Try changing min_cluster_size below to see how the selection responds.

There is the whole difference, and it is visible rather than argued. That's the whole difference, and you can see it rather than take it on argument. The coloured bars are the cuts, and they aren't level. #if spread > 0.5 On this day, they're spread over num(spread, 1) metres of eps, which is exactly the range that a single value couldn't cover. :else On this day, they happen to be nearly level, which is what an easy dataset looks like; the horizontal line would have worked here too. /if Nothing about the tree has changed, since it's the same object DBSCAN was slicing. What has changed is that the answer is now allowed to contain more than one number.
One wrinkle worth knowing, because it is a real property of the criterion and it is rarely mentioned. \lambda is one over a distance, so stability is measured in pings per metre. Scale a whole dataset by s and every stability scales by exactly 1/s — which is fine, because everything scaled and the selection is unchanged.
There's one wrinkle worth knowing about, because it's a real property of the criterion and it's rarely mentioned. Since \lambda is one over a distance, stability is measured in pings per metre. If you scale a whole dataset by s, every stability scales by exactly 1/s, which is fine, because everything scaled together and the selection doesn't change.
What is less fine is what happens when only part of the data scales. Take two identical clusters, side by side, and shrink one of them: at half the size it is num(half.ratio, 2)× as stable as its twin, and at a quarter, num(quarter.ratio, 2)× — the scale factor, to two decimals, from a pair of clusters that are the same shape and the same size in pings. So the criterion carries a systematic preference for the denser candidate, inside the algorithm whose entire selling point is coping with clusters of different density.
What's less fine is what happens when only part of the data scales. Take two identical clusters side by side and shrink one of them. At half the size, it's num(half.ratio, 2)× as stable as its twin, and at a quarter of the size, it's num(quarter.ratio, 2)× as stable. That's exactly the scale factor, to two decimals, for a pair of clusters with the same shape and the same number of pings. So the criterion carries a systematic preference for the denser candidate, inside an algorithm whose whole selling point is coping with clusters of different densities.
It does not break anything here — the loose one still gets selected, because it is competing against its own children rather than against the compact one. But it is the thing to look at first when HDBSCAN returns a big coarse cluster where you expected two, and it is why cluster_selection_method="leaf" exists: it takes the deepest branches instead of the most stable ones, and gives up the excess-of- mass argument in exchange for not having this thumb on the scale.

But it's the first thing to check when HDBSCAN returns one big, coarse cluster where you expected two, and it's why cluster_selection_method="leaf" exists. That option takes the deepest branches instead of the most stable ones, giving up the excess-of-mass argument in exchange for removing this thumb on the scale.

Elbow

But you just did not pick eps properly
But maybe you just didn't pick eps properly
This is the right objection and it deserves a real answer, because there is a standard recipe and almost every tutorial repeats it. Take every ping's distance to its kth nearest neighbour, sort those distances, plot them, and read eps off the elbow — the place where the curve stops crawling and turns upwards.
This is a fair objection, and it deserves a real answer, because there's a standard recipe that almost every tutorial repeats. You take every ping's distance to its kth nearest neighbour, sort those distances, plot them, and read eps off the elbow, which is the place where the curve stops crawling along and turns upwards.
It is worth going back to what the recipe was for. Ester and colleagues proposed it in the original 1996 paper as a sorted 4-dist graph, and the thing they asked you to find on it was the first valley, which separates the noise from everything else — you pick a point by eye, and the pings to one side of it are noise. That is a way of locating the noise floor. The modern retelling automates the same picture and treats it as a way of locating the cluster scale. Those are the same number only when your clusters all have roughly the same density, which is the assumption the previous section was about.
It's worth going back to what the recipe was originally for. Ester and colleagues proposed it in the original 1996 paper as a sorted 4-dist graph, and what they asked you to find on it was the first valley, which separates the noise from everything else. You pick a point by eye, and the pings on one side of it are noise. In other words, it's a way of locating the noise floor. The modern retelling automates the same picture but treats it as a way of locating the cluster scale. Those two are the same number only when all your clusters have roughly the same density, which is exactly the assumption the previous section was about.
Try it on each day. On the platforms the elbow lands inside the working range for easy.hit of the easy.n values of minPts in the grid behind this figure. On a whole day — where a window exists, and is num metres wide at minPts = 8 — it lands inside for day.hit of day.n. Overall, hits of withWindow.length. On the café day it cannot land anywhere, because there is nowhere to land, and the best it manages is Math.max of HARD.nStops stops.
Try it on each day. On the platforms, the elbow lands inside the working range for easy.hit of the easy.n values of minPts in the grid behind this figure. On the whole day, where a window exists and is num metres wide at minPts = 8, it lands inside for day.hit of day.n. Overall, that's hits of withWindow.length. On the café day, it can't land anywhere, because there's nowhere to land, and the best it manages is Math.max of HARD.nStops stops.
The recipe works on the day you did not need it for and misses on the day you did. Which is not bad luck. Look at where on the curve the elbow keeps landing: across E.n combinations of dataset and minPts, it sits between the Math.round(100 * E.min)th and the Math.round(100 * E.max)th percentile of the sorted distances, median the Math.round(100 * E.median)th, with a standard deviation of num(100 * E.sd, 1) percentage points. The elbow is a high percentile wearing a geometric hat. Any sorted distance curve has roughly that shape, so the recipe is returning a property of the shape of sorted distances rather than a property of your clusters.
So the recipe works on the day you didn't need it and misses on the day you did, and that isn't bad luck. Look at where on the curve the elbow keeps landing. Across E.n combinations of dataset and minPts, it sits between the Math.round(100 * E.min)th and Math.round(100 * E.max)th percentiles of the sorted distances, with a median at the Math.round(100 * E.median)th and a standard deviation of num(100 * E.sd, 1) percentage points. In other words, the elbow is really just a high percentile in disguise. Any sorted distance curve has roughly that shape, so the recipe returns a property of sorted distances in general rather than a property of your clusters.
And it is fragile in a specific, cheap way: the chord it measures against is anchored at the far end of the curve, which is one order statistic. Tick the box. One additional ping, logged once, in a corner where nothing happened, moves the recommendation from num(E.stray.before, 2) m to num(E.stray.after, 2) m — a 75% change to the central parameter of the model, from a single row you would never look at.
It's also fragile in a specific and easily triggered way. The chord it measures against is anchored at the far end of the curve, which is just one order statistic. Try ticking the box. A single extra ping, logged once in a corner where nothing happened, moves the recommendation from num(E.stray.before, 2) m to num(E.stray.after, 2) m. That's a 75% change to the model's central parameter, caused by a single row you'd never look at.
None of which makes the heuristic useless. Finding the noise floor is a real question and the sorted curve is a reasonable way to look at it. It is just not, and was never advertised as, a way of finding a window that is not there.
None of this makes the heuristic useless. Finding the noise floor is a real question, and the sorted curve is a reasonable way to look at it. It just isn't a way of finding a window that doesn't exist, and it was never advertised as one.

Intro

A phone logged where it was, all day, HOOK.n times. Some of those pings are places the person stopped — a platform, a café, twenty minutes on a bench — and the rest were logged while they walked from one to the next. The job is to get the places back.
Suppose a phone logged its location HOOK.n times over the course of a day. Some of those pings come from places where the person stopped (a platform, a café, twenty minutes on a bench), and the rest were logged while they walked from one place to the next. Our job is to recover the places.
k-means will not do it, and it is worth being exact about why, because there are two reasons and only one of them is the famous one. The first is that you would have to say how many places there were, which is the question. The second is that a place is not a blob. Two railway platforms are two long thin strips num(GAP, 0) metres apart, and the cheapest way to cut points on two strips into two groups is to cut across them rather than between them. k-means with k = 2 splits the first platform into km.split[0] and km.split[1] and scores an adjusted Rand index of num(km.ari, 3) against the truth — fractionally worse than dealing the labels out at random.
k-means won't do the job, and it's worth being precise about why, because there are two reasons and only one of them is the famous one. The first is that you'd have to say how many places there were, which is exactly the question we're trying to answer. The second is that a place isn't a blob. Two railway platforms are two long, thin strips num(GAP, 0) metres apart, and the cheapest way to split points on two strips into two groups is to cut across them rather than between them. k-means with k = 2 splits the first platform into km.split[0] and km.split[1] and scores an adjusted Rand index of num(km.ari, 3) against the truth, which is fractionally worse than dealing out the labels at random.
DBSCAN asks for something else entirely. Not how many clusters, but how close counts as close — eps, a radius — and how many neighbours make a crowd — minPts. A ping with at least minPts pings within eps of it is a core ping; core pings within eps of each other belong to the same cluster; whatever is left over is noise. No k anywhere, clusters of any shape at all, and the walking comes back labelled as walking instead of being forced into a group.
DBSCAN asks for something else entirely. Instead of how many clusters there are, it asks how close counts as close (eps, a radius) and how many neighbours make a crowd (minPts). A ping with at least minPts pings within eps of it is a core ping, core pings within eps of each other belong to the same cluster, and whatever is left over is noise. There's no k anywhere, clusters can have any shape at all, and the walking comes back labelled as walking instead of being forced into a group.
Both axes below are metres. That is not a detail. eps is a distance, and in almost every write-up of DBSCAN it is a distance in whatever space a StandardScaler produced, which nobody has an intuition about. Here it is metres on the ground, so a value for it is a claim you can argue with: is twelve metres the same place, or two places?
Both axes below are in metres, and that's not a minor detail. eps is a distance, and in almost every write-up of DBSCAN, it's a distance in whatever space a StandardScaler produced, which nobody has any intuition about. Here, it's metres on the ground, so any value you pick is a claim you can argue with: are two pings twelve metres apart still in the same place, or in two different places?
One definition, used for the rest of the article. A stop counts as found when some cluster holds at least 80% of its pings and less than 20% of any other stop's. It is a blunt test, and it has the merit of saying out loud what it is treating as success.
We'll use one definition for the rest of the article. A stop counts as found when some cluster holds at least 80% of its pings and less than 20% of any other stop's pings. It's a blunt test, but it has the merit of stating clearly what it treats as success.
So: move eps. The strip below the map is the whole sweep — every distinct clustering the knob can produce, one clustering per step — and the band is where the answer recovers every stop. Get a feel for it on the first day. Then switch to the second one.

MechScroll

Radius eps, which is EPS metres here. Count what is inside it, the ping itself included: (EX_NBRS.length + 1). minPts is MINPTS, so this ping is core — it has enough company to count as being somewhere. This is the ping with the least company that still qualifies; most of them are not close.

That is the entire density test. Not a density estimate, not a kernel: a count inside a ball, compared against a threshold. Everything DBSCAN knows about how crowded a place is, it knows from this one comparison.
That's the entire density test. It isn't a density estimate or a kernel, just a count inside a ball, compared against a threshold. Everything DBSCAN knows about how crowded a place is comes from this one comparison.
Core — nCore of them here — has minPts inside its circle. Border — nBorder — does not, but sits inside somebody else's. Noise — nNoise — is neither, and DBSCAN will hand it back to you unlabelled rather than pretend it belongs somewhere.
A core ping (there are nCore of them here) has minPts inside its circle. A border ping (there are nBorder) doesn't, but it sits inside some core ping's circle. A noise ping (there are nNoise) is neither, and DBSCAN hands it back to you unlabelled rather than pretending it belongs somewhere.
Being willing to say this ping is not anywhere is the thing that makes this worth doing on location data at all. k-means has no way to say it: every ping logged mid-stride gets assigned to a place, and drags that place's centre towards the road.
Being willing to say this ping isn't anywhere is what makes this approach worth using on location data at all. k-means has no way to say that. Every ping logged mid-stride gets assigned to a place, and it drags that place's centre towards the road.
Start from any core ping, take everything inside its circle, and repeat from each of those that is also core. One round in, the cluster is the shaded region: every point within EPS metres of a core point it has reached.
Next, we start from any core ping, take everything inside its circle, and repeat from each of those pings that is also core. After one round, the cluster is the shaded region: every point within EPS metres of a core point it has reached.
Nothing here is optimising anything. There is no objective function, no assignment step, nothing to converge to. It is a flood fill.
Notice that nothing here is optimising anything. There's no objective function, no assignment step and nothing to converge to. It's simply a flood fill.
(ROUNDS.length - 1) rounds and the frontier runs out — every core ping it can reach has been expanded, and what is left is more than EPS metres from all of them. That is one cluster, finished. Start again somewhere unvisited for the next one.
After (ROUNDS.length - 1) rounds, the frontier runs out. Every core ping it can reach has been expanded, and everything left is more than EPS metres from all of them. That's one finished cluster, and to find the next one, we start again somewhere unvisited.
The cluster's shape is whatever the data's shape was. Nobody chose an ellipse or a centre; the region is the union of a few dozen circles and it can be as bent, as long or as knobbly as the pings are.
The cluster's shape is simply whatever shape the data had. Nobody chose an ellipse or a centre. The region is the union of a few dozen circles, and it can be as bent, as long or as knobbly as the pings are.
The ringed pings are border: near enough to a core ping to be swept into its cluster, not crowded enough to spread it any further. The fill stops at them.
The ringed pings are border pings. They're near enough to a core ping to be swept into its cluster, but not crowded enough to spread it any further, so the fill stops at them.
They look like a detail and they are not. A border ping can be inside the circle of two core pings from two different clusters, and the definition does not say which one it belongs to — so it goes to whichever cluster is grown first, which is to say, to whatever order your rows happen to be in. We come back to this.
They might look like a detail, but they aren't. A border ping can sit inside the circles of two core pings from two different clusters, and the definition doesn't say which cluster it belongs to. So it goes to whichever cluster is grown first, which means it depends on whatever order your rows happen to be in. We'll come back to this later.
Look at what eps just did. It set the density threshold: how many pings have to be inside the circle. And it set the linking rule: how far the flood fill may step at a time. Same number, both jobs.
Look at what eps just did. First, it helped set the density threshold, since a core ping needs minPts pings inside a circle of that size. Second, it set the linking rule, which decides how far the flood fill may step at a time. One number is doing both jobs.
Those two jobs want different values, and worse, they want different values in different places on the map. A busy café needs a small circle to stay distinct from the shop next door; an hour on a lawn needs a big one to hold together at all. There is one eps, and it is the same everywhere.

But there's only one eps, and it's the same everywhere.

Modes

Neither of them can split one hill
Neither of them can split a single hill
DBSCAN and HDBSCAN are both level-set methods: a cluster is a connected piece of the region where the density is above some threshold. That is the source of the good property everybody quotes — clusters can be any shape — and of a limit almost nobody does. If two groups are not separated by a valley, there is nothing to find, at any parameter, ever.
DBSCAN and HDBSCAN are both level-set methods, which means a cluster is a connected piece of the region where the density is above some threshold. That's the source of the good property everyone mentions (clusters can have any shape), and also of a limit that almost nobody mentions. If two groups aren't separated by a valley, there's nothing to find, at any parameter setting, ever.
How much of a valley? For the simplest case there is an exact answer. Take an equal mixture of two isotropic Gaussians and look along the axis joining their centres:
So how much of a valley does it take? For the simplest case, there's an exact answer. Take an equal mixture of two isotropic Gaussians and look along the axis joining their centres:
The midpoint is always a stationary point. Differentiate twice and it is a maximum — one hill, not two — for anything closer than two standard deviations:
The midpoint is always a stationary point. If we differentiate twice, we find that it's a maximum (one hill, not two) whenever the centres are closer than two standard deviations apart:
Left: PRE.config.MODE_N points and the mixture density along the axis between the centres, with the midpoint marked. Right: the dashed red line is 2σ, where the second mode appears. Grey is what a perfect split at the midpoint would score; blue is the best any DBSCAN* over every eps and five values of minPts manages.
On the left are PRE.config.MODE_N points and the mixture density along the axis between the centres, with the midpoint marked. On the right, the dashed red line is at 2σ, where the second mode appears. The grey line shows what a perfect split at the midpoint would score, and the blue line shows the best that any DBSCAN* manages, over every eps and five values of minPts.
Below 2σ the blue line is flat on the floor, and it should be — there is one hill and finding two clusters in it would be a bug. What is worth staring at is the stretch just above 2σ. At 3σ the density is genuinely bimodal, the valley is real, and a perfect split scores num(three.bayes, 3) while the best density clustering in existence scores num(three.best, 3). The two do not come within a tenth of each other until about num(caught.sep, 1)σ.
Below 2σ, the blue line is flat on the floor, and it should be, because there's only one hill, and finding two clusters in it would be a bug. The part worth staring at is the stretch just above 2σ. At 3σ, the density really is bimodal and the valley is real, yet a perfect split scores num(three.bayes, 3), while the best density clustering there is scores num(three.best, 3). The two don't come within a tenth of each other until about num(caught.sep, 1)σ.
So the mode appears at 2σ and becomes findable somewhere past 5σ, and the num(caught.sep - 2, 1)σ in between is a region where the structure is really there, a density method really cannot get it, and nothing about your parameters is at fault. Every textbook picture of density-based clustering is drawn well to the right of that gap.
So the second mode appears at 2σ, but it only becomes findable somewhere past 5σ. In the num(caught.sep - 2, 1)σ in between, the structure is really there, a density method really can't get it, and nothing about your parameters is at fault. Every textbook picture of density-based clustering is drawn well to the right of that gap.
k-means, meanwhile, will cheerfully cut one hill down the middle, and for plenty of purposes that is what you wanted. Neither answer is wrong. They are answers to different questions: where are the separated concentrations, and if I have to have k groups, which k. Choosing between the methods is choosing which question you are asking, and it is a decision to make before you look at any output, because both will give you something that looks like an answer.
k-means, meanwhile, will cheerfully cut a single hill down the middle, and for plenty of purposes, that's what you want. Neither answer is wrong. They're answers to different questions: where are the separated concentrations? and if I have to have k groups, which k should they be? Choosing between the methods means choosing which question you're asking, and you should make that decision before looking at any output, because both will give you something that looks like an answer.
One practical note while we are here. When HDBSCAN decides your data is one hill, what it returns is everything labelled noise — the root is not an eligible cluster, so if nothing below it wins, nothing wins. An empty result from hdbscan usually means "I see one thing", not "I see nothing", and the two are easy to confuse when what you are looking at is an array of minus ones.
Here's one practical note while we're at it. When HDBSCAN decides your data is a single hill, it returns everything labelled as noise. The root isn't an eligible cluster, so if nothing below it wins, nothing wins at all. An empty result from hdbscan usually means "I see one thing", not "I see nothing", and the two are easy to confuse when you're looking at an array of minus ones.

NoWindow

It is not a narrow window. There is no window.
It isn't a narrow window. There's no window at all.

Raising minPts inflates every point's sense of how far away its neighbours are, and it inflates the lawn's faster than the gap between two shops. That's because a two-dimensional patch has to grow its radius to find more neighbours, while two clusters a fixed distance apart stay a fixed distance apart.
Nor do border points, which is worth checking rather than assuming: the version above is DBSCAN*, which throws non-core pings away, and the DBSCAN in your toolbox sweeps them into whichever cluster reaches them. Running that one instead, on a 10 cm grid of eps from 1 to 40 metres and every minPts from mLo to mHi, it {classicFails ? "never recovers all three stops either" : "sometimes recovers all three"}.

One thing should be said here rather than buried, because it is the obvious objection and it is correct. Run k-means on this day, with k = HARD.kmeans.k, and it finds all three stops — adjusted Rand num(HARD.kmeans.ari, 2), against the num that the best eps in existence manages. The café, the bakery and the lawn are three roughly round blobs, which is precisely the shape k-means assumes, and it does not care in the slightest that one of them is four times looser than another.
There's one thing we should say here rather than bury, because it's the obvious objection, and it's correct. If you run k-means on this day with k = HARD.kmeans.k, it finds all three stops, with an adjusted Rand index of num(HARD.kmeans.ari, 2), compared with num for the best eps there is. The café, the bakery and the lawn are three roughly round blobs, which is exactly the shape k-means assumes, and it doesn't care in the slightest that one of them is four times looser than another.
So this is not a story about one algorithm being better. It is a story about what each of them is committed to. k-means is committed to round clusters of comparable spread and to a number you supply; it was fine here and it scored num(HOOK.kmeans.ari, 3) on the platforms, which is worse than guessing. DBSCAN is committed to one density threshold for the whole map — and that commitment, unlike the shape assumption, cannot be relaxed by tuning, because it is not a parameter. It is the form of the answer.
