Reach

Everything from here on is HDBSCAN, and it starts by replacing the distance. The first piece is a number per ping rather than per pair — its core distance, which is exactly the quantity the last two sections kept measuring:
Everything from here on is about HDBSCAN, which starts by replacing the distance. The first piece is a number for each ping rather than for each pair. It's called the core distance, and it's exactly the quantity the last two sections kept measuring:
It is a density estimate that happens to be measured in metres. Small where the phone sat still, large where it was moving. On this day the median is medStop m inside the stops and num m along the routes walked between them — inflation× further, which is the whole of the mechanism in one number. The circles below are each ping's own core distance — a sample of them, because five hundred overlapping circles is a fog rather than a figure.
You can think of it as a density estimate that happens to be measured in metres. It's small where the phone sat still and large where it was moving. On this day, the median is medStop m inside the stops and num m along the routes walked between them, which is inflation× further. That one number captures the whole mechanism. The circles below show each ping's own core distance for a sample of the pings, because five hundred overlapping circles would be a fog rather than a figure.
Circles of radius coremSamples. The ones strung out along the walking routes are the point: a ping logged mid-stride is a long way from its mSamplesth neighbour, and turning m up makes it further, faster than it does for a ping inside a stop.
Circles of radius coremSamples. The ones strung out along the walking routes are the important ones: a ping logged mid-stride is a long way from its mSamplesth neighbour, and turning m up pushes that neighbour further away, faster than it does for a ping inside a stop.
Now the second piece. Take the distance between two pings and refuse to let it be smaller than either one's core distance:
Now for the second piece. We take the distance between two pings and don't let it be smaller than either ping's core distance:
Read it as: two pings are as far apart as the greater of how far apart they are and how lonely either of them is. Three things follow, and none of them require any calculation. It can only ever push points further apart, never closer, because it is a maximum with @html inlineD in it. Inside a dense stop it changes nothing, because there @html inlineCore is much smaller than the distances between neighbours and the maximum picks @html inlineD. And a ping out on its own is pushed away from everything at once, by its own core distance — so it joins whatever it eventually joins late, and it cannot act as a stepping stone before then.
You can read this as: two pings are as far apart as the greater of their actual distance and how lonely either of them is. Three things follow, and none of them require any calculation. First, it can only ever push points further apart, never closer, because it's a maximum with @html inlineD in it. Second, inside a dense stop, it changes nothing, because there @html inlineCore is much smaller than the distances between neighbours, so the maximum picks @html inlineD. And third, a ping out on its own is pushed away from everything at once, by its own core distance. This means it joins whatever it eventually joins late, and it can't act as a stepping stone before then.
That last one is what it is for. A route walked between two stops is a one-dimensional filament of pings at moderate spacing, and a filament is a bridge: at the eps a loose stop needs to hold together, the filament is dense enough to connect everything it touches. Mutual reachability inflates exactly those pings and leaves the stops alone.
That last property is the whole point. A route walked between two stops is a one-dimensional filament of pings at moderate spacing, and a filament acts as a bridge. At the eps a loose stop needs in order to hold together, the filament is dense enough to connect everything it touches. Mutual reachability inflates exactly those pings and leaves the stops alone.
What it actually buys
What it actually buys you
Here is the part usually left out. It does not buy a better answer. On this day, plain single linkage — which is what you get when m = 1 and every core distance is zero — recovers all five stops at its best threshold, and so does mutual reachability at every m. What changes is how much room you have to be wrong:
Here's the part that usually gets left out: it doesn't buy you a better answer. On this day, plain single linkage (which is what you get when m = 1 and every core distance is zero) recovers all five stops at its best threshold, and so does mutual reachability at every m. What changes is how much room you have to be wrong:
m = 1 is plain single linkage: no core distance, no mutual reachability. Bars are labelled with their height in metres.
m = 1 is plain single linkage, with no core distance and no mutual reachability. Each bar is labelled with its height in metres.
From num(plain.width, 1) metres of usable threshold with plain single linkage to num(widest.width, 1) metres at m = widest.m. Same answer inside the window, more than twice as much window. That is the honest description of what mutual reachability is: not accuracy, tolerance. It makes the choice of threshold matter less, which is a strange thing to want until you remember that the next step is going to stop choosing one.
The usable range of thresholds grows from num(plain.width, 1) metres with plain single linkage to num(widest.width, 1) metres at m = widest.m. The answer inside the window is the same, but the window is more than twice as wide. So the honest description of mutual reachability is that it buys tolerance, not accuracy. It makes the choice of threshold matter less, which seems like a strange thing to want, until you remember that the next step is going to stop choosing a threshold at all.

Title

A single eps is a horizontal line drawn across a tree. When the branches do not line up, no line works — and no amount of tuning is going to produce one.
A single eps is a horizontal line drawn across a tree. When the branches don't line up, no single line works, and as we'll see, no amount of tuning is going to produce one.

TreeCut

Go back to the slider for a moment and notice something about it. As eps grows, clusters only ever merge. A ping that has joined a cluster cannot leave it, and two clusters that have become one cannot come apart again — a larger radius can only add edges. So the whole sweep is a merge history, and a merge history is a tree.
Let's go back to the slider for a moment and notice something about it. As eps grows, clusters only ever merge. A ping that has joined a cluster can't leave it, and two clusters that have become one can't come apart again, because a larger radius can only add edges. So the whole sweep is a merge history, and a merge history is a tree.
That is not an analogy. It is an identity, and it is the reason this article is built the way it is. Two pings are in the same DBSCAN* cluster at eps exactly when there is a chain of pings between them on which every ping is core and every step is at most eps — and with the mutual reachability distance from the last section, both of those conditions collapse into one:
This isn't just an analogy. It's an identity, and it's the reason this article is built the way it is. Two pings are in the same DBSCAN* cluster at eps exactly when there's a chain of pings between them in which every ping is core and every step is at most eps. With the mutual reachability distance from the last section, both of those conditions collapse into one:
Which is the definition of being connected in the single-linkage hierarchy of dmreach, and a minimum spanning tree records that for every threshold at once. So one tree, computed once, contains every answer DBSCAN* can give on this data — not approximately, and not for a grid of values. Running brute-force DBSCAN* and reading the tree instead, at every eps the sweep can tell apart, across three days and three values of minPts: 3,581 thresholds, zero disagreements.
That's exactly the definition of being connected in the single-linkage hierarchy of dmreach, and a minimum spanning tree records this for every threshold at once. And a value of eps is a horizontal line drawn across that tree. Whatever branches the line crosses are the clusters. That is not a way of thinking about DBSCAN — it is the complete description of what DBSCAN is able to say.

That is what "no eps works" is, structurally. Not a sensitive parameter, not a hard search, not a compromise between two objectives. A horizontal line has one height; this data has branches at three. One caveat, because it is the version most people will actually run. Those are opinions a person can hold about their own data, and hold before seeing any output. On a whole day the best eps beats HDBSCAN by num, both of them finding all five stops; HDBSCAN sweeps more of the walking in with them, and pays for it. On the platforms they are within num of each other. It is only on the café day, where no eps exists at all, that the gap is real.
On the whole day, the best eps beats HDBSCAN by num, with both of them finding all five stops, because HDBSCAN sweeps more of the walking in with the stops and pays for it. Which is the correct summary of the whole thing. HDBSCAN is not a more accurate DBSCAN. On data where a single density threshold is the right shape of answer, it is a slightly worse one — it has more freedom than the problem needs and spends some of it. What it is, is a method whose answer does not stop existing when your clusters have different densities, bought with a parameter you can reason about instead of one you have to search for.
That's the right way to summarise the whole thing. HDBSCAN isn't a more accurate DBSCAN. On data where a single density threshold is the right shape of answer, it's slightly worse, because it has more freedom than the problem needs and spends some of it. What it offers instead is an answer that doesn't stop existing when your clusters have different densities, bought with a parameter you can reason about rather than one you have to search for.

population-stability-index

Anatomy

Three properties of that formula do real work later, and none of them is usually stated. Take one bin, and write r for the ratio of what arrived to what was expected. The bin contributes
Three properties of that formula do real work later on, and none of them is usually spelled out. Let's take one bin and write r for the ratio of what arrived to what was expected. The bin then contributes
First: that is never negative. When a bin fills up, both factors are positive; when it empties, both are negative. So no bin can cancel another, PSI is a sum of non-negative pieces, and it is zero only when every bin lands exactly on its expected share. Sampling noise cannot average out of it — it can only add. That single observation is the whole of the next two sections.
First, that contribution is never negative. When a bin fills up, both factors are positive, and when it empties, both are negative. So no bin can cancel out another: PSI is a sum of non-negative pieces, and it's zero only when every bin lands exactly on its expected share. This means sampling noise can't average out of it. Noise can only add to the total, and that single observation is what the next two sections are built on.
Second: the cost is asymmetric, by exactly the ratio. A bin that doubles costs twice what a bin that halves costs; one that goes ten times over costs ten times what one that drops to a tenth costs. It is an identity, not a tendency:
Second, the cost is asymmetric, and by exactly the ratio. A bin that doubles costs twice as much as a bin that halves, and a bin that grows tenfold costs ten times as much as one that drops to a tenth. This isn't just a tendency. It's an identity:
Third: the sum is not a credit-scoring invention. Multiply out the bracket and the two halves separate:
Third, the sum isn't a credit-scoring invention. If we multiply out the bracket, the two halves separate:
PSI is the Kullback–Leibler divergence J — the symmetric one, defined in the same 1951 paper that gave us the directed version everyone quotes. That matters twice over. It means PSI is a sample estimate of a real population quantity rather than an index, which is why the correction later in this article works at all. And it means PSI inherits that quantity's properties, including the one that makes the bin count a free parameter.
In other words, PSI is the Kullback–Leibler divergence J, the symmetric version defined in the same 1951 paper that gave us the directed one everyone quotes. This matters for two reasons. It means PSI is a sample estimate of a real population quantity, not just an index, which is why the correction later in this article works at all. It also means PSI inherits that quantity's properties, including the one that makes the bin count a free parameter.
It is tempting to think the split carries something — that a big KL(A‖E) means the population piled into somewhere new and a big KL(E‖A) means it vacated somewhere old. Measured across thirteen different shifts here, the forward half's share runs from num to num — it is a coin flip every time, and there is nothing in it.
It's tempting to think the split tells us something, for example that a big KL(A‖E) means the population piled into somewhere new, while a big KL(E‖A) means it moved out of somewhere old. However, when we measure it across thirteen different shifts, the forward half's share runs from num to num. It's a coin flip every time, so there's nothing to read into it.

Two bins carry of it, and they are the two at the bottom of the score range. That is the useful part of PSI and the part a monitoring pack should always print: not the total, but which bins it came from and which way they went.

BinCount

The two settings that aren't in the report

The corrected number, on the other hand, runs from psiFmt to psiFmt(last.adjMean) and tracks the dashed line, which is the quantity that subtraction estimates: the divergence between the two populations, with the development sample's own frozen error taken off along with the rest of the floor. The shaded right-hand end is where that stops being true, and it is the same failure as the top of the validity table in the last section wearing a different hat. More bins at a fixed N applications means fewer applications in each, and below about 60 per bin the chi-square formula understates the real floor — so the subtraction does not take off enough, and the corrected reading starts climbing again on residue. At 100 bins there are num applications in each and about a fifth of what is left is still floor. That is a reason to keep the bin count modest which has nothing to do with how big the raw number looks: past a point, more bins stop being resolution and start being noise that the correction cannot reach.

This gives us a reason to keep the bin count modest that has nothing to do with how big the raw number looks. Past a certain point, more bins stop adding resolution and start adding noise that the correction can't reach.
There is a reason refining can only push the raw number up. PSI is a divergence, and divergences obey a data-processing inequality: throwing information away — merging two adjacent bins — can never increase one. Across DPI.trials merges of random pairs, {DPI.violations === 0 ? "not one increased PSI" : DPI.violations + " increased PSI"}. Quantile bins at two different counts are not nested, though, so nothing forces the middle of that first chart to rise smoothly — and it does not quite: the true divergence at 8 bins is psiFmt, fractionally below the psiFmt at 6. The inequality is about refinement, not about the size of the number.
However, quantile bins at two different counts aren't nested, so nothing forces the middle of that first chart to rise smoothly, and in fact it doesn't quite. The true divergence at 8 bins is psiFmt, which is fractionally below the psiFmt at 6. The inequality is about refinement, not about the size of the number.
The practical consequence is duller and worse than "PSI depends on the bins". It is that two teams monitoring the same model with the same data will report different numbers against the same threshold, and nothing in the report says why. Fix the bin count, fix the edges, and write both on the page.

It's that two teams monitoring the same model with the same data will report different numbers against the same threshold, and nothing in the report will say why. So fix the bin count, fix the edges, and write both of them on the page.

Blind

Binning is the whole method, and it has a consequence that follows immediately: if the applications stay in the deciles they were already in, every proportion is unchanged and the reading does not move. Not barely — not at all, to the last bit of a double. Everything that happens inside a decile is invisible, and the deciles of this scorecard are num to num score points wide, with two more running off to either end.
Binning is the whole method, and it has an immediate consequence. If the applications stay in the deciles they were already in, every proportion is unchanged, and the reading doesn't move. We don't mean that it barely moves. It doesn't move at all, down to the last bit of a double. This means that everything that happens inside a decile is invisible, and the deciles of this scorecard are num to num score points wide, with two more running off to either end.
The strongest setting there moves the mean score num points, takes num points off the approval rate and raises the expected bad rate by num(W[3].dMeanPdPct, 0)%. The decile proportions are identical to the last bit at every setting, so the reading holds at psiFmt(FROZEN) throughout — and that psiFmt(FROZEN) is not a response to the rearrangement either. It is the development sample's own frozen error from two sections ago, and it would be there if nothing had happened at all.

Yet the decile proportions are identical to the last bit at every setting, so the reading holds at psiFmt(FROZEN) throughout. And that psiFmt(FROZEN) isn't a response to the rearrangement either. It's the development sample's own frozen error, which we met in the section on PSI's sampling distribution, and it would be there even if nothing had happened at all.
That rearrangement is contrived; this one is not. The cut-off sits at BG.cutoff, and decile BG.bin of the development sample runs from num(BG.lo, 0) to num(BG.hi, 0) — so the line the whole lending decision turns on is inside a bin, with BG.massBelow / of that bin below it.
That rearrangement was contrived, but this next one isn't. The cut-off sits at BG.cutoff, and decile BG.bin of the development sample runs from num(BG.lo, 0) to num(BG.hi, 0). This means the line that the whole lending decision turns on sits inside a bin, with BG.massBelow / of that bin below it.
Brokers learn where cut-offs are. When applications start gathering on the profitable side of one — a product switched, a co-applicant added, an application held back a fortnight — the mass moves a few points, stays inside decile BG.bin, and changes nothing PSI can see.
Brokers learn where cut-offs are. When applications start gathering on the profitable side of one (because a product was switched, a co-applicant was added, or an application was held back a fortnight), the mass moves a few points, stays inside decile BG.bin, and changes nothing that PSI can see.
The applications that cross the line did not become safer; their score did. Their real default rate is BN[3].pdOfMoved, 1 against PRE.population.acceptedB… for the book they are joining — nearly three times the risk of the average approval — and the scorecard now says they are ordinary. The reading does not move by so much as a last bit at any of those settings, and neither does anything else in the pack that looks only at the distribution of the score.
The applications that cross the line didn't become safer. Only their scores did. Their real default rate is BN[3].pdOfMoved, 1, against PRE.population.acceptedB… for the book they're joining, which is nearly three times the risk of the average approval. Yet the scorecard now says they're ordinary. The reading doesn't move by so much as a last bit at any of those settings, and neither does anything else in the pack that looks only at the distribution of the score.

Conclusion

Every PSI you will ever read is two numbers added together. One of them is how far the population moved. The other is (B − 1)(1/N + 1/M), and it is there whether anything moved or not, because every term in the sum is non-negative and noise has nowhere to cancel.
Every PSI you'll ever read is really two numbers added together. One of them is how far the population moved. The other is (B − 1)(1/N + 1/M), and it's there whether anything moved or not, because every term in the sum is non-negative, so noise has nowhere to cancel.
On broker.n applications with ten bins that second number is psiFmt(floorOf(broker.n)), which is most of a reading. On online.n it is psiFmt(floorOf(online.n)), which is none of one. A fixed threshold applied to their sum is therefore a rule about sample size that has been written down as a rule about model risk, and it will keep being read the wrong way round. The quiet channel in this article's pack outscored the one that lost Math.abs approvals in broker.pBeatsOnline, 0 of months; about one month in Math.round it crosses the amber line on a population that did not move at all; and online.name, the channel that lost those approvals, stayed green in every month of the simulated year.
On broker.n applications with ten bins, that second number is psiFmt(floorOf(broker.n)), which is most of a reading. On online.n, it's psiFmt(floorOf(online.n)), which is next to nothing. So a fixed threshold applied to their sum is really a rule about sample size that has been written down as a rule about model risk, and it will keep being read the wrong way round. The quiet channel in this article's pack outscored the one that lost Math.abs approvals in broker.pBeatsOnline, 0 of months. About one month in Math.round, it crosses the amber line on a population that didn't move at all. Meanwhile, online.name, the channel that lost those approvals, stayed green in every month of the simulated year.
The repair is one subtraction, and it is available in every monitoring system in the world today, because B, N and M are already on the page. What it buys is a number that means the same thing on a broker panel and on a national channel — which is the only thing anyone wanted from a stability index in the first place.
The repair is a single subtraction, and every monitoring system in the world can make it today, because B, N and M are already on the page. What it buys is a number that means the same thing on a broker panel as on a national channel, which is all anyone wanted from a stability index in the first place.
What it does not buy is a model that is still right. PSI never looks at an outcome, which is exactly why it arrives two years before anything else and exactly why it cannot tell you the one thing you most want to know. The calibration can slip forty points, the losses can double, and the histogram will not move at all. Keep PSI, fix its threshold, print its bins, and put something beside it that knows what the model was for.
What it doesn't buy is a model that's still right. PSI never looks at an outcome, which is exactly why it arrives two years before anything else, and also exactly why it can't tell you the one thing you most want to know. The calibration can slip forty points and the losses can double without the histogram moving at all. So keep PSI, fix its threshold, print its bins, and put something beside it that knows what the model was for.
And treat the 0.10 line for what it turned out to be. It is not a tolerance. It is num(amberPoints.points, 0) score points — about a third of a standard deviation of an entire acquisition channel, and num points of approval rate. By the time a population has moved that far, nobody needed a statistic to notice.
Finally, treat the 0.10 line as what it turned out to be. It isn't a tolerance. It's num(amberPoints.points, 0) score points, which is about a third of a standard deviation of an entire acquisition channel and num points of approval rate. By the time a population has moved that far, nobody needs a statistic to notice.

Damage

cap(int(S.length)) things that can happen to this population in a month: it drifts down, it drifts up, it spreads out, it tightens, the thin-file share moves, a new lead source appears at the bottom of the book, or the scorecard's calibration slips while the score distribution stays exactly where it was. Along one axis, the PSI each one produces. Along the other, the number of bad loans per thousand applications it leaves the lender holding.

The population can drift down or up, spread out or tighten, or see its thin-file share move. Along one axis, we plot the PSI that each month produces, and along the other, the number of bad loans per thousand applications that it leaves the lender holding.
There is no relationship. The four most expensive months in that chart are the four furthest to the left, all of them well inside the green band, and the most expensive of all — num(c40.dBadPer1000, 2) extra bad loans per thousand, which is c40.badPer1000 / base - … more than the book was built to carry — has a PSI of psiFmt(c40.psi). Meanwhile the readings above 0.10 are almost all population drifts down, which cost the lender volume rather than losses: fewer applications clear the cut-off, so the accepted book barely changes — every one of them leaves the lender with {num(Math.max(...S.filter((s) => s.psi > 0.1).map((s) => s.dBadPer1000)), 2)} or fewer extra bad loans per thousand, and most of them with fewer than it started with.

The red points are worth a paragraph of their own, because they are not a failure of PSI so much as a fact about what it is for. Nothing about the population changed in any of them. The same applicants arrive with the same scores in the same proportions, and what moved is the relationship between score and default — a recession, a change in what a bureau field means, a shift in the products people are applying for. The reading does not move at all — what those four points show is the development sample's own frozen error and nothing else — because PSI never looks at an outcome.

What does catch it: observed against expected, band by band. It needs outcomes, which is why it arrives two years late and why nobody wants it to be the only thing watching.

Direction

Two months, at the same channel. In one, the share of thin-file applicants rises from PRE.population.thinShare… to 0.52, 0; in the other it falls to 0.22, 0. Because the population is a mixture, those two moves take exactly the same amount of mass out of each decile and put it back in the other direction — the measured antisymmetry is MI.maxAntisymmetry.toExp…, which is the last bit of a double.
Let's compare two months at the same channel. In one, the share of thin-file applicants rises from PRE.population.thinShare… to 0.52, 0, and in the other, it falls to 0.22, 0. Because the population is a mixture, those two moves take exactly the same amount of mass out of each decile and put it back in the other direction. The measured antisymmetry is MI.maxAntisymmetry.toExp…, which is the last bit of a double.
They are the same size and opposite in every way that matters to the lender. The approval rate moves num points down in one and num(MI.approvalUp, 2) points up in the other. One is a channel getting worse; the other is a channel getting better.
So the two moves are the same size, and they're opposite in every way that matters to the lender. The approval rate moves num points down in one month and num(MI.approvalUp, 2) points up in the other. In other words, one is a channel getting worse, and the other is a channel getting better.
The top panel is a perfect mirror; the bottom one is not. PSI calls the improvement num times worse than the deterioration, and if the pack had a threshold anywhere between the two it would open an investigation into the good news.
The top panel is a perfect mirror, but the bottom one isn't. PSI calls the improvement num times worse than the deterioration, so if the pack had a threshold anywhere between the two readings, it would open an investigation into the good news.
It is not the famous r against 1/r asymmetry, which needs the ratios to be reciprocal, and these are not. Take the quadratic part of PSI on its own — the Pearson statistic, which is what the chi-square law is about — and it comes out identical for the two months to eight decimal places: num(MI.quadUp, 6) against num(MI.quadDown, 6). The entire difference lives in the cubic term and beyond.
Why does this happen?
What that term says is this. Move the same amount of mass out of a bin as into it, and the move out costs more. At a decile that loses or gains half its share, num(half.ratio, 2) times more; at one that loses or gains big.d / 0.1, 0 of it, num(big.ratio, 2) times. So a shift that empties the crowded end of the distribution scores higher than a shift that fills it, whatever it means for the book.
It isn't the famous r against 1/r asymmetry, because that needs the ratios to be reciprocal, and these aren't. If we take the quadratic part of PSI on its own (the Pearson statistic, which is what the chi-square law is about), it comes out identical for the two months to eight decimal places: num(MI.quadUp, 6) against num(MI.quadDown, 6). So the entire difference lives in the cubic term and beyond.
None of this is a defect in the formula. J is a divergence and divergences do not have signs; asking one which way the population went is asking the wrong question of the right object. The defect is in summing it and printing the total. Every ingredient of the direction is in the bottom panel above, computed on the way to the number and then discarded.
What that cubic term tells us is this: if we move the same amount of mass out of a bin as into it, the move out costs more. At a decile that loses or gains half its share, it costs num(half.ratio, 2) times more, and at one that loses or gains big.d / 0.1, 0 of its share, it costs num(big.ratio, 2) times more. As a result, a shift that empties the crowded end of the distribution scores higher than a shift that fills it, whatever that shift means for the book.

It's worth stressing that none of this is a defect in the formula. J is a divergence, and divergences don't have signs, so asking one which way the population went is asking the right object the wrong question. The defect is in summing the terms and printing only the total. Every ingredient of the direction is in the bottom panel above, computed on the way to the number and then thrown away.

Fix

None of this needs a new statistic, a new library or a model-risk paper. It needs four columns where there is currently one.
Fixing this doesn't need a new statistic, a new library or a model-risk paper. All it needs is four columns where there's currently one.
(B − 1)(1/N + 1/M) is three numbers the pack already knows. Subtracting it turns a reading that cannot be compared across channels into one that can. Here is the month this article opened on, with that one change.
The floor, (B − 1)(1/N + 1/M), is built from three numbers the pack already knows. Subtracting it turns a reading that can't be compared across channels into one that can. Here's the month this article opened on, with just that one change.
What the correction buys is comparability, and it buys it in expectation rather than in every month. Over a long run the corrected readings settle at psiFmt(SEGS[0].expAdj) for Online, psiFmt(SEGS[2].expAdj) for Motor dealer, and psiFmt(SEGS[1].expAdj) for both channels that did not move — the same number at SEGS[3].n applications as at SEGS[1].n, which is exactly what the raw column could not do. In any particular month the readings still wander, which is why the band beside them is not decoration.
What the correction buys us is comparability, and it buys it on average rather than in every single month. That's the same number at SEGS[3].n applications as at SEGS[1].n, which is exactly what the raw column couldn't manage. In any particular month, however, the readings still wander, which is why the band beside them isn't just decoration.
Press through a year and watch the verdict column rather than the numbers. Compared against its own no-drift band at the 5% level, Online is flagged in SEGS[0].pFlag, 0 of months and Motor dealer in SEGS[2].pFlag, 0; the two channels that did not move are flagged in SEGS[1].pFlag, 0 and SEGS[3].pFlag, 0 of them. Those last two numbers are not failures. They are what a five per cent test does, stated in advance, on a statistic whose distribution is known — which is the thing the fixed thresholds never offered in either direction.
Try pressing next month through a whole year, and watch the last column rather than the numbers. When each channel is compared against its own no-drift band at the 5% level, Online is flagged in SEGS[0].pFlag, 0 of months and Motor dealer in SEGS[2].pFlag, 0, while the two channels that didn't move are flagged in SEGS[1].pFlag, 0 and SEGS[3].pFlag, 0 of them. Those last two numbers aren't failures. 2. Print the band, not only the number
2. Print the band, not just the number
The corrected reading is an estimate and estimates have error bars. At SEGS[3].n applications its standard error is psiFmt, which is larger than most of the shifts anyone cares about — so the honest output for that channel is not a corrected number at all, it is this window cannot distinguish a num-point shift from nothing, and here is the band. A corrected value that comes out negative is not a bug either; it is what an unbiased estimate of a non-negative quantity does when the quantity is near zero, and it is information.
The corrected reading is an estimate, and estimates have error bars. At SEGS[3].n applications, its standard error is psiFmt, which is larger than most of the shifts anyone cares about. So the honest output for that channel isn't a corrected number at all. It's a sentence: this window can't distinguish a num-point shift from nothing, and here's the band. A corrected value that comes out negative isn't a bug either. It's what an unbiased estimate of a non-negative quantity does when the quantity is near zero, and it carries information.
If a single number is wanted, the sample-size-aware critical value is one line and is the test the thresholds were pretending to be:

The bin count, the edges and the zero substitute belong in the report next to the number, because all three change it and none of them is visible now. Freeze the edges at the development sample and never re-derive them, or the series is not a series. And if a characteristic gains a level that did not exist at development time, say so in words rather than letting a default argument decide how alarming it is.
The bin count, the edges and the zero substitute belong in the report next to the number, because all three change it, and right now none of them is visible. Freeze the edges at the development sample and never re-derive them, otherwise the series isn't really a series. And if a characteristic gains a level that didn't exist at development time, say so in words rather than letting a default argument decide how alarming it is.
PSI is in units nobody has an intuition for. Every reading can be translated into one people do: this month's Online shift is num points of mean score, num points of approval rate and Math.abs fewer approvals. At the other end of the scale, in a transaction-monitoring model scoring PRE.large.N transactions a month, a PSI of num(PRE.large.psi, 2) — deep inside the green — is {num((PRE.large.psi - PRE.large.floor) / (Math.sqrt(2 * (B - 1)) * (1 / PRE.large.N + 1 / M)), 0)} standard errors above its floor and, at a rule that alerts on the worst 0.01, 0 of transactions, num times the alert volume. num per cent more work for the investigations team, reported as no significant change.
PSI comes in units nobody has an intuition for, but every reading can be translated into units people do understand. For example, this month's Online shift amounts to num points of mean score, num points of approval rate and Math.abs fewer approvals. At the other end of the scale, consider a transaction-monitoring model scoring PRE.large.N transactions a month. There, a PSI of num(PRE.large.psi, 2), deep inside the green, is {num((PRE.large.psi - PRE.large.floor) / (Math.sqrt(2 * (B - 1)) * (1 / PRE.large.N + 1 / M)), 0)} standard errors above its floor. With a rule that alerts on the worst 0.01, 0 of transactions, it also means num times the alert volume. That's num per cent more work for the investigations team, reported as no significant change.
The traffic light is what turns a statistic with a known sampling distribution into a verdict with none. It also fails a palette check, which is a small thing next to the rest but worth knowing: the conventional amber sits at num(1.92, 2):1 against this page, below the 3:1 floor for a mark, and every amber dark enough to clear it collapses toward either the green or the red under the commonest form of colour blindness. There is no accessible green-amber-red. There was never a good reason for it to be three colours.
The traffic light is what turns a statistic with a known sampling distribution into a verdict with none. It also fails a palette check, which is a small thing next to the rest, but it's worth knowing. The conventional amber sits at num(1.92, 2):1 against this page, below the 3:1 floor for a mark, and every amber dark enough to clear that floor collapses toward either the green or the red under the most common form of colour blindness. In other words, there's no accessible green-amber-red, and there was never a good reason for the verdict to be three colours in the first place.
What this does not fix
What this doesn't fix
Three things, stated plainly. The chi-square law assumes the month is an independent sample, and a real month is not — applications arrive in correlated bursts, campaigns run for weeks, and a serially dependent population has a wider null band than the formula says, so the correction is the floor rather than the whole answer. Below about twenty applications per bin the formula understates the floor and the band has to be simulated. And none of it touches the two blindnesses: what happens inside a bin, and what happens to the relationship between the score and the outcome. Those are not calibration problems. They are what PSI is, and the answer to them is a different measurement, not a better threshold.
There are three things this doesn't fix, so let's state them plainly. First, the chi-square law assumes the month is an independent sample, and a real month isn't. Applications arrive in correlated bursts, campaigns run for weeks, and a serially dependent population has a wider null band than the formula says, so the correction gives us the floor rather than the whole answer. Second, below about twenty applications per bin, the formula understates the floor, so the band has to be simulated. And third, none of this touches PSI's two blind spots: what happens inside a bin, and what happens to the relationship between the score and the outcome. Those aren't calibration problems. They're part of what PSI is, and the answer to them is a different measurement, not a better threshold.

Intro

A lender builds an application scorecard on dev applications, puts it live, and then has to answer the same question every month for the rest of the model's life: is this still the population we built it on?
Suppose a lender builds an application scorecard on dev applications and puts it live. From then on, the lender has to answer the same question every month for the rest of the model's life: is this still the population we built it on?
The answer has to arrive long before the outcomes do. A consumer loan takes a year or two to say whether it was a good one, so for the first eighteen months of a scorecard's life there is nothing to check its predictions against. What there is, on the first of every month, is a fresh set of scores. So the industry watches the shape of those instead — and the Population Stability Index is how.
The answer has to arrive long before the outcomes do. It takes a year or two to find out whether a consumer loan was a good one, so for the first eighteen months of a scorecard's life, there's nothing to check its predictions against. What there is, on the first of every month, is a fresh set of scores. So the industry watches the shape of those scores instead, and the Population Stability Index (PSI) is how it does that.
Cut the development sample into ten equal slices at its own deciles, so each slice holds exactly 1 / B, 0 of it by construction. Count what fraction of this month's applications land in each slice. If nothing has moved, each fraction should come out near 1 / B, 0 as well. PSI adds up how far they are from it:
Here's how it works. We cut the development sample into ten equal slices at its own deciles, so each slice holds exactly 1 / B, 0 of it by construction. Then we count what fraction of this month's applications land in each slice. If nothing has moved, each fraction should also come out near 1 / B, 0. PSI adds up how far the fractions are from that:
with e the expected share of bin i and a the actual one. Then it compares the total against two numbers that have been in the credit literature since 1994: below 0.10, no significant change; 0.10 to 0.25, a moderate shift worth investigating; above 0.25, a significant shift — review the model.
where e is the expected share of bin i and a is the actual share. The total is then compared against two numbers that have been in the credit literature since 1994: below 0.10 means no significant change, 0.10 to 0.25 means a moderate shift worth investigating, and above 0.25 means a significant shift, so review the model.
That is the whole procedure, and it is everywhere. It runs on the score, on every input characteristic, on every segment, in every monitoring pack, in a one-line function in every credit analytics library, and the results go into a table with those two thresholds as a colour rule. It needs no outcomes, which is exactly why it won.
That's the whole procedure, and it's everywhere. It runs on the score, on every input characteristic and on every segment. It's in every monitoring pack and in a one-line function in every credit analytics library, and the results go into a table with those two thresholds as a colour rule. It needs no outcomes, which is exactly why it won.

MonitorLab

One says how far the population really moved. The other says how many applications happened to come through the door. The reading responds to both, and only one of them is about the model.
The lab below has two knobs. One sets how far the population really moved, and the other sets how many applications happened to come through the door. The reading responds to both, but only one of them has anything to do with the model.
Set the drift to zero and pull the second slider down. At smallN.N applications the top of the grey band is already at psiFmt(smallN.q95) — the amber line — on a population that is by construction identical to the one the model was built on. Keep going: at tinyN.N the median month reads psiFmt(tinyN.q50) and tinyN.pRed, 0 of them are red. Then push it the other way: past bigN.N the whole band has slid off to the left and sits around psiFmt(bigN.q50), and you can drag the top slider num(amberPoints.points, 0) points — num(amberPoints.sd, 2) standard deviations of the entire population — before the blue marker leaves the green.
Set the drift to zero and pull the second slider down. At smallN.N applications, the top of the grey band is already at psiFmt(smallN.q95) (the amber line), on a population that is identical by construction to the one the model was built on. If you keep going, at tinyN.N applications the median month reads psiFmt(tinyN.q50), and tinyN.pRed, 0 of months are red. Now push the slider the other way. Past bigN.N applications, the whole band has slid off to the left and sits around psiFmt(bigN.q50), and you can drag the top slider num(amberPoints.points, 0) points (num(amberPoints.sd, 2) standard deviations of the entire population) before the blue marker leaves the green.
Notice what the band does not do on the way: it slides without getting any narrower. That is the chi-square law made visible. The whole distribution of a no-drift reading is the same shape at every sample size, rescaled by @html "(1/N + 1/M)" — so on a logarithmic axis it keeps its width and simply moves. Ten times the applications does not make the reading ten times more certain about how much moved; it moves the entire question down a decade.
Also notice what the band doesn't do along the way: it slides without getting any narrower. That's the chi-square law made visible. The whole distribution of a no-drift reading has the same shape at every sample size, rescaled by @html "(1/N + 1/M)", so on a logarithmic axis, it keeps its width and simply moves. Ten times as many applications doesn't make the reading ten times more certain about how much moved. Instead, it moves the entire question down a decade.

A chi-square with B − 1 degrees of freedom has mean B − 1. Undo the scaling and the consequence is the one sentence this article exists for:
A chi-square with B − 1 degrees of freedom has a mean of B − 1. So when we undo the scaling, we arrive at the one equation this whole article is built around:
This is a bias, not a spread. Because every term is non-negative, a noisy month cannot read low to make up for one that read high — the noise all points the same way. At ten bins and a M-application development sample, the average reading on a channel that did not move at all is psiFmt(nullAt(180).mean) on 180 applications, psiFmt(nullAt(9300).mean) on 9300, and psiFmt on 200000 — simulated, not predicted. It's important to see that this is a bias, not a spread. Because every term is non-negative, a noisy month can't read low to make up for one that read high, so all of the noise points in the same direction. With ten bins and a M-application development sample, the average reading on a channel that didn't move at all is psiFmt(nullAt(180).mean) on 180 applications, psiFmt(nullAt(9300).mean) on 9300, and psiFmt on 200000. These figures are simulated, not predicted, and they all come from the same population, the same model and the same month.
The two terms are not the same kind of thing. The 1/N half is fresh noise: a different draw every month, averaging to nothing over a year of readings. The 1/M half is not noise at all after the first day — the bin edges were cut from one particular development sample, and whatever that sample got slightly wrong is frozen into every future reading in the same direction, for the life of the model.
The two terms in that formula aren't the same kind of thing. The 1/N half is fresh noise, a different draw every month, which averages out to nothing over a year of readings. The 1/M half, however, stops being noise after the first day. That's because the bin edges were cut from one particular development sample, and whatever that sample got slightly wrong is frozen into every future reading, in the same direction, for the life of the model.
Here it is worth psiFmt — a permanent floor under every month's reading for this scorecard, which is small because the development sample was large. Build on 2000 applications instead and the same term is psiFmt((B - 1) / 2000), and no amount of monitoring will ever see below it.
For this scorecard, that frozen term is worth psiFmt. It's a permanent floor under every month's reading, and it's small only because the development sample was large. It is the result the rest of the article leans on, so it is worth being explicit about where it stops holding. Below roughly twenty applications per bin the chi-square is no longer a good description, and it fails in the direction that makes the argument here stronger, not weaker: the real floor is higher than the formula says.
Since the rest of the article leans on this result, it's worth being explicit about where it stops holding. Below roughly twenty applications per bin, the chi-square is no longer a good description. TheCase

It would be easy to read the last few sections as an argument that the whole thing is worthless. It is not, and the reason is worth being precise about, because it is also the reason the fix at the end is a subtraction rather than a replacement.
It would be easy to read the last few sections as an argument that PSI is worthless. It isn't, and it's worth being precise about why, because the same reason explains why the fix at the end is a subtraction rather than a replacement.
It needs no outcomes. This is not a minor convenience, it is the whole game. A consumer loan written in January cannot be called good or bad until the following January at the earliest, and a twelve-month definition of default plus a performance window plus the time to assemble the data means the first honest backtest of a scorecard lands somewhere between eighteen and thirty months after it goes live. Every measure of whether a model is right — AUC, the Gini, calibration, the observed-against-expected table in the last section — is on that clock. PSI is available on the first working day of the month. There is no other statistic in the pack that can say anything at all that early, and the industry did not adopt it because it was confused.
First, it needs no outcomes. This isn't a minor convenience. It's the whole game. A consumer loan written in January can't be called good or bad until the following January at the earliest. Add a twelve-month definition of default, a performance window and the time it takes to assemble the data, and the first honest backtest of a scorecard lands somewhere between eighteen and thirty months after it goes live. Every measure of whether a model is right, including AUC, the Gini, calibration and the observed-against-expected table in the last section, runs on that clock. PSI, on the other hand, is available on the first working day of the month. No other statistic in the pack can say anything at all that early, and the industry didn't adopt PSI because it was confused.
It decomposes. The sum is over bins and every term is non-negative, so "PSI is psiFmt(online.truePsi)" is always refinable into "and of it is the bottom two deciles filling up". That is a real finding about a real channel, and it arrives free with the number.
Second, it decomposes. The sum runs over bins, and every term is non-negative, so "PSI is psiFmt(online.truePsi)" can always be refined into "and of it is the bottom two deciles filling up". That's a real finding about a real channel, and it comes free with the number.
It estimates something. Because PSI is the Kullback–Leibler divergence J rather than an index, it converges to a population quantity as the month gets longer, and that quantity does not depend on the sample size. Everything in this article that looks like a repair — the floor, the correction, the critical value — is available only because PSI is a real estimator of a real thing. An index would have had nothing to correct toward.
Third, it estimates something. Because PSI is the Kullback–Leibler divergence J rather than an index, it converges to a population quantity as the month gets longer, and that quantity doesn't depend on the sample size. Everything in this article that looks like a repair, whether it's the floor, the correction or the critical value, is only possible because PSI is a real estimator of a real thing. An index would have had nothing to correct toward.
And at a sensible sample size it works. On the Online channel, online.n applications, the reading was psiFmt(online.q05) to psiFmt(online.q95) across simulated months against a floor of psiFmt(online.floor) — an unmistakable signal, correctly detected, months before any outcome existed. The complaint in this article is not that PSI failed to see the shift. It is that the pack printed no significant change underneath it.
Finally, at a sensible sample size, it works. On the Online channel, with online.n applications, the reading ranged from psiFmt(online.q05) to psiFmt(online.q95) across simulated months, against a floor of psiFmt(online.floor). That's an unmistakable signal, correctly detected, months before any outcome existed. The complaint in this article isn't that PSI failed to see the shift. It's that the pack printed no significant change underneath it.
Every replacement proposed for PSI over the years has the same shape and most have the same problem. A Kolmogorov–Smirnov statistic has a null distribution that scales like 1/√N, so a fixed KS threshold is wrong in exactly the way a fixed PSI threshold is wrong. A chi-square test of homogeneity is PSI with the scaling left in — the same statistic to first order, honestly labelled, and at a few million transactions a month it rejects everything, which is why nobody uses it there. Earth-mover distance keeps the sign and the units, which is a real advantage, and needs a metric on the score, which a raw scorecard point does not obviously have.
Every replacement proposed for PSI over the years has the same shape, and most of them have the same problem. A Kolmogorov–Smirnov statistic has a null distribution that scales like 1/√N, so a fixed KS threshold is wrong in exactly the same way as a fixed PSI threshold. A chi-square test of homogeneity is PSI with the scaling left in. It's the same statistic to first order, just honestly labelled, and at a few million transactions a month it rejects everything, which is why nobody uses it at that scale. Earth-mover distance keeps the sign and the units, which is a real advantage, but it needs a metric on the score, and a raw scorecard point doesn't obviously have one.
None of them is a way out, because the thing everyone actually wants — a label-free statistic that says whether the model is still right — does not exist. A shift in the inputs is not the same event as a model going wrong, and no amount of looking at inputs will turn one into the other. PSI's honest job description is narrow and useful: it says whether the population the model is scoring is the population it was fitted on, with a known sampling distribution, before any outcome is available. Read that way it is a good statistic that has been asked the wrong question for thirty years.

Thresholds

A threshold applied to a statistic whose floor moves with N has to be wrong at every sample size but one. It is worth seeing both halves of that, because they fail in opposite ways and the second is the more expensive.
If we apply a fixed threshold to a statistic whose floor moves with N, the threshold has to be wrong at every sample size but one. It's worth looking at both halves of that problem, because they fail in opposite ways, and the second one is the more expensive.

Title

A reading of 0.05 can be a population that did not move at all, or one that has quietly cost you eight percent of your approvals. Which of those it is depends on a number that is not in the formula.
A PSI reading of 0.05 can mean a population that didn't move at all, or one that has quietly cost you eight percent of your approvals. As we'll see, which of those it is depends on a number that isn't in the formula.

Zeros

A bin with nothing in it makes the logarithm infinite, so every implementation substitutes a small number and moves on. The usual value is 0.0001. It is not a rounding convenience: the term diverges as the share goes to zero, so the substitute sets the size of the answer.

Where the zero is not bad luck
When the zero isn't bad luck
PSI is not only run on the score. The same formula goes over every input characteristic, one at a time, and there the bins are categories rather than quantiles — which changes the problem completely. A rare category can be empty by chance: a level holding 0.008, 1 of applications is missing entirely from a 180-application window r180.pRarest, 0 of the time. And a new category has an expected share of exactly zero, which no substitution on the actual side can repair.
PSI isn't only run on the score. The same formula is applied to every input characteristic, one at a time, and there the bins are categories rather than quantiles, which changes the problem completely. So for the same month, the same characteristic and the same single change, PSI reads num(eps2, 3), num(eps4, 3) or num(eps8, 3) depending on the substitute, and num(Z.merged, 3) if the new level is folded into "other" and the report says so. That's a hundredfold range, decided by a constant that isn't in the documentation, on a characteristic where the only thing that happened is that a code was added.
There is a right answer here and it is not a smaller epsilon. A level that did not exist at development time is not a distribution shift, it is a mapping change, and the honest treatment is to say so in words, map it to an existing level for the purpose of the comparison, and note the mapping in the pack. Every choice except that one is a number whose size was set by a default argument.
There is a right answer here, and it isn't a smaller epsilon. A level that didn't exist at development time isn't a distribution shift. It's a mapping change, and the honest way to treat it is to say so in words, map it to an existing level for the purpose of the comparison, and note the mapping in the pack. Every other choice gives us a number whose size was set by a default argument.

constrained-choice

App

The standard answer is that it depends, and it comes with a reason. An hour off is now more expensive — you are giving up twice as much to take it — so you should take fewer of them. But you are also better off, and people who are better off tend to buy more of the good things, free time included. Two effects, opposite directions, and the textbook closes the case by saying theory cannot tell you which is larger. That is an empirical question.
The standard answer is that it depends, and that answer comes with a reason. An hour off is now more expensive, because you give up twice as much pay to take it, so you should take fewer of them. But you're also better off, and people who are better off tend to buy more of the good things in life, including free time. So there are two effects pulling in opposite directions, and the textbook closes the case by saying that theory can't tell you which one is larger. That, it says, is an empirical question.
It is a good answer and it is taught everywhere. It is also, for the utility function that is drawn on the board while it is being said, exactly wrong: the two effects do not merely offset, they cancel to the last digit a computer can carry, at every wage, and the working day does not move by so much as a second. Getting to that takes three small pictures first.
It's a good answer, and it's taught everywhere. However, for the utility function that's drawn on the board while the answer is being given, it's exactly wrong. The two effects don't merely offset each other. They cancel to the last digit a computer can carry, at every wage, and the working day doesn't move by so much as a second. To see why, we'll need to build up three small pictures first.
Give the worker a day of sixteen waking hours and one decision: how many to keep. Whatever is not kept is worked, and the wage turns it into things to buy. Put free time on one axis and consumption on the other and every affordable combination sits under a single straight line.
Let's give our worker a day of sixteen waking hours and one decision to make: how many of those hours to keep as free time. Whatever isn't kept is worked, and the wage turns those working hours into things to buy. If we put free time on one axis and consumption on the other, every affordable combination sits under a single straight line.
The second ingredient is what the worker wants, and it is not a ranking of bundles so much as a set of exchange rates. Join up the bundles that are equally good and you get a curve; its slope at any point is how much consumption one more hour of free time is worth there.
The second ingredient is what the worker wants, which is less a ranking of bundles than a set of exchange rates. If we join up the bundles that the worker likes equally well, we get a curve called an indifference curve. Its slope at any point tells us how much consumption one more hour of free time is worth at that point.
The one substantive assumption is that the curves bow towards the origin — that the rate falls as free time gets plentiful. Somebody with two free hours will give up a lot for a third. Somebody with fourteen will not.
The one substantive assumption we make is that the curves bow towards the origin, which means the rate falls as free time becomes plentiful. Somebody with two free hours will give up a lot for a third, but somebody with fourteen won't.
Now the two pictures are the same picture. The worker picks the affordable bundle they like best, and wherever the rate they would trade at differs from the rate the wage trades at, there is a trade worth making. So the best bundle is where the two rates agree: @html mrsEq.
Now we can combine the two pictures into one. The worker picks the affordable bundle they like best, and wherever the rate they'd trade at differs from the rate the wage offers, there's a trade worth making. So the best bundle is where the two rates agree. Economists call the worker's rate the marginal rate of substitution, or MRS, so the condition for the best bundle is @html mrsEq.
That is the whole model. It is worth pausing on how little is in it — a line, a family of curves, and a tangency — because the same three objects reappear as the firm's isoprofit problem, the bargaining frontier, and the choice between spending now and spending later. Learn to read one and you have read all four.
And that's the whole model. It's worth pausing on how little is in it: a line, a family of curves and a tangency. The same three objects reappear in the firm's isoprofit problem, the bargaining frontier, and the choice between spending now and spending later, so once you've learned to read one, you've read all four.
So: what happens when the wage rises?

Below is the same diagram, but this time the wage is in your hands. Drag anywhere to pivot the frontier. The grey track shows every optimum the worker would choose across the whole range of the drag. In other words, it's the model's answer to the question at the top of this page, drawn all at once.
At σ = 1 the track is vertical. Not nearly vertical — vertical. The worker gets richer, consumption climbs, and the working day stays at eight hours whatever the wage does. Push the wage from 30 kr to 90 kr, or from 3 kr to 9,000 kr, and hours worked change by an amount that is not small: it is zero.
The lab starts at σ = 1, a setting we'll explain shortly, and there the track is vertical. We don't mean nearly vertical; it's exactly vertical. The worker gets richer and consumption climbs, but the working day stays at eight hours whatever the wage does. Whether you push the wage from 30 kr to 90 kr or from 3 kr to 9,000 kr, hours worked change by an amount that isn't just small. It's zero.
The reason is visible in the algebra the moment you write it down. With @html cdEq — Cobb–Douglas, the workhorse that gets drawn when nobody wants to commit to a functional form — the solution is @html fStar. The wage is not in it. It cancelled.
The reason becomes clear as soon as we write down the algebra. At σ = 1, the utility function is @html cdEq, which is known as Cobb–Douglas. It's the workhorse that gets drawn whenever nobody wants to commit to a functional form. With it, the solution is @html fStar, and the wage doesn't appear in that solution at all, because it cancelled out.
The effects are still there, and they are enormous
The effects are still there, and they're enormous
It would be comfortable if the cancellation happened because both effects were negligible. They are not. Hold the worker's satisfaction fixed and ask what the price change alone does, then hand back the money: each step moves a large fraction of the day, and they land on top of each other.
It would be reassuring if the cancellation happened because both effects were negligible, but they aren't. Let's hold the worker's satisfaction fixed and ask what the price change does on its own, and then hand back the money. Each of those two steps moves a large fraction of the day, and they land right on top of each other.
A sixteen-fold rise in the wage moves the substitution effect by six of eight free hours — three quarters of the worker's leisure — and the income effect hands back the same six. Two large numbers that a textbook would call "offsetting" are in fact equal to every digit. That is not a coincidence to be noted and moved past; it is a property of the function that was chosen, and choosing it settled the empirical question before any data arrived.
With a sixteen-fold rise in the wage, the substitution effect takes away six of the worker's eight free hours, which is three quarters of their leisure, and the income effect hands back the same six. So two large numbers that a textbook would call "offsetting" are in fact equal to every digit. This isn't a coincidence to note and move past. It's a property of the function that was chosen, and choosing that function settled the empirical question before any data arrived.
Cobb–Douglas is one member of a family. The parameter that distinguishes them is σ, the elasticity of substitution — how willing the worker is to swap consumption for free time when the rate changes.
Cobb–Douglas is just one member of a larger family of utility functions, known as CES, short for constant elasticity of substitution. The parameter that distinguishes the family's members is σ, the elasticity of substitution, which measures how willing the worker is to swap consumption for free time when the rate changes.
σ = 1 is Cobb–Douglas. Above it, consumption and free time are easy substitutes and the substitution effect wins; below it they are poor substitutes and the income effect wins. And the whole family collapses onto one straight line: take the log of the ratio of hours worked to free time, plot it against the log wage, and every member is exactly linear with slope σ − 1.
Setting σ = 1 gives us Cobb–Douglas. When σ is above 1, consumption and free time are easy substitutes, and the substitution effect wins. When it's below 1, they're poor substitutes, and the income effect wins. What's more, the whole family collapses onto one straight line. If we take the log of the ratio of hours worked to free time and plot it against the log wage, every member of the family is exactly linear, with a slope of σ − 1.
The dots are the model solved numerically at each wage; the line is the closed form. They agree to about a part in a trillion, which is the arithmetic and not the economics. Drag σ and the fan rotates about the point where the wage is 1 — and the answer to "which effect dominates" is just which side of horizontal the line has fallen on.
The dots are the model solved numerically at each wage, and the line is the closed form. They agree to about a part in a trillion, which reflects the arithmetic rather than the economics. As you drag σ, the fan rotates about the point where the wage is 1, and the answer to "which effect dominates?" is simply which side of horizontal the line has fallen on.
Written as an elasticity, the same statement is one line, and it says that the sign of the response is decided by σ and its size by how much of the day is already free:
If we write the same statement as an elasticity, it fits on one line. It says that σ decides the sign of the response, while how much of the day is already free decides its size:
So theory is not silent after all. It says: pick σ and you have picked the answer. The empirical question was never "which effect is bigger" — it was "what is σ", and that is a question about a number in a functional form, not a standoff between two forces.
So theory isn't silent after all. What it says is that once you pick σ, you've picked the answer. The empirical question was never "which effect is bigger?" but "what is σ?", and that's a question about a number in a functional form, not a standoff between two forces.
Working hours in rich countries fell a long way over the last century and a half, and the usual gloss is that the income effect won. Take that seriously and it means σ < 1. But a constant σ below 1 does not describe the history: it drives hours down without limit, towards a day with no work in it at all. What actually happened is that hours fell steeply and then flattened.
Working hours in rich countries fell a long way over the last century and a half, and the usual explanation is that the income effect won. If we take that seriously, it means σ < 1. But a constant σ below 1 doesn't match the history, because it drives hours down without limit, towards a day with no work in it at all. What actually happened is that hours fell steeply and then flattened out.
One small change to Cobb–Douglas produces that shape, and it is not a change to σ at all. Suppose some consumption has to be covered before any of it is a choice — rent, food, heat. At a low wage that floor eats most of the day and the worker has no room to choose; as the wage rises the floor shrinks to nothing and the choice converges on the one Cobb–Douglas was making all along.
One small change to Cobb–Douglas produces that shape, and it isn't a change to σ at all. Suppose some consumption, such as rent, food and heating, has to be covered before any of it is a choice. At a low wage, covering that floor eats up most of the day, and the worker has no room to choose. As the wage rises, the time it takes to cover the floor shrinks to nothing, and the choice converges on the one Cobb–Douglas was making all along.
Hours fall monotonically, steeply at first, and approach eight from above without ever going below. The decline is not a victory of one effect over another. It is the floor getting out of the way — and the destination is precisely the answer the effect-cancelling model gave in the first place.
Hours only ever fall as the wage rises, steeply at first, and they approach eight from above without ever dropping below it. So the decline isn't a victory of one effect over the other. It's the floor getting out of the way, and the destination is precisely the answer the effect-cancelling model gave in the first place.
The knife-edge is a property of this family, not of choice in general. σ is the parameter that settles the sign within CES; a different family can put the answer somewhere else, and the honest version of the claim is that the received account smuggles in a functional form and then reports its consequence as an open question.
The knife-edge is a property of this family of functions, not of choice in general. σ is the parameter that settles the sign within CES, and a different family could put the answer somewhere else. So the honest version of our claim is that the usual account smuggles in a functional form and then reports its consequence as an open question.
Nor is one worker with sixteen fungible hours a labour market. Real hours come in lumps, not in a continuum — most people choose between a job and no job, not between 7.4 and 8.1 hours. The household, not the person, is usually what allocates time, which is where most of the interesting variation in the data lives. And there is no tax here, no non-labour income, no unemployment, and nothing produced at home, all of which move the frontier in ways the wage alone does not.
Nor is one worker with sixteen interchangeable hours a labour market. Real hours come in lumps rather than on a continuum, and most people choose between a job and no job, not between 7.4 and 8.1 hours. It's also usually the household, not the individual, that allocates time, and that's where most of the interesting variation in the data lives. Finally, this model has no tax, no non-labour income, no unemployment and nothing produced at home, and all of these move the frontier in ways the wage alone doesn't.
What survives all that is the method. Write the constraint, write the preference, find where the rates agree, and then — the step that is usually skipped — check whether the answer you got depends on the assumption you were not paying attention to. Here it depended entirely on it.
What survives all of that is the method. Write down the constraint, write down the preference and find where the rates agree. Then comes the step that usually gets skipped: check whether the answer you got depends on an assumption you weren't paying attention to. Here, it depended on one entirely.

The worker's problem in this form, and the framing of hours as a choice between consumption and free time, follow CORE Econ's The Economy 2.0: Microeconomics (2023), Unit 3 and its mathematical Extension. However, every figure, number, parameter and derivation on this page is my own, and nothing is reproduced from it.
The indeterminacy this article is arguing with is old, and it is worth reading in the original: Lionel Robbins, "On the Elasticity of Demand for Income in Terms of Effort", Economica 29 (1930), 123–129. Robbins is answering Pigou and Knight, who held that higher wages must reduce effort, and his conclusion is the one still taught — that we cannot say a priori, and must ascertain the elasticity for the workers concerned. He is right that the model does not settle it. What this page adds is that choosing the function settles it for you, quietly, before any data is consulted.

The CES form is Arrow, Chenery, Minhas and Solow, "Capital–Labor Substitution and Economic Efficiency", Review of Economics and Statistics 43 (1961), 225–250.
